.

Structure of the paper.

We briefly introduce CompCert and the challenges we are facing in §
2
. We then use the WHILE language to illustrate the basic denotational semantics on KATs and extend it to realistic languages in §
3
. We further take procedure calls into account and build the semantics of open modules and a novel semantic linking operator via the PCALL languages in §
4
. We introduce the denotation of unstructured branches using the GOTO language in §
4.4
and that of control flow graphs (CFG) with the CFG language in §
5
. We present the full semantics of Clight in §
6
.

The highlight of this article is developing novel refinement algebras to establish behavior refinement between denotations in §
7
, and applying our theoretical framework to verify the CompCert front-end, a classical CFG generation, and optimizations on toy languages in §
8
. We further compare our approach with typical related efforts in §
9
, followed by code evaluations in §
10
and further discussion in §
11
. Finally, we discuss related work in §
12
and conclude the article in §
13
.

2.
Background and Challenges of Compiler Verification

2.1.
CompCert Verification Framework

The small-step semantics of a language
\mathcal{L}
in CompCert is modeled as a tuple (
\mathcal{I}
,
\hookrightarrow
,
\mathcal{F}
) where
\mathcal{I}
denotes the set of initial states loaded by a function
Load
:
\mathcal{L}\rightarrow\text{\footnotesize state}
,
\mathcal{F}
denotes the set of final states of program execution, and
\hookrightarrow\subseteq\text{\footnotesize state}\times\text{\footnotesize trace}\times\text{\footnotesize state}
is a stepping relation. For each single step of execution
(\sigma,\tau,\sigma^{\prime})\in\hookrightarrow
(written as
\sigma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\hookrightarrow}}\sigma^{\prime}
), state
\sigma
transitions to state
\sigma^{\prime}
with a finite sequence of triggered observable events (typically, I/O and other system calls). An observable behavior is defined as a (finite or infinite) trace of observable events occurring in a sequence of execution steps.
The CompCert compilation correctness theorem ensures that the target program preserves the observable behavior of the source program and does not introduce any new behavior that wasn’t present in the source program. Such compilation correctness is justified by a backward simulation.
We say a program
P_{s}
is backward simulated by a program
P_{t}
if given a state matching relation
R
, their initial states
\text{\footnotesize Load}(P_{s})
and
\text{\footnotesize Load}(P_{t})
is related by
R
, and (simplified for presentation)

\displaystyle\forall\sigma_{s}\ \sigma_{t}\ \sigma_{t}^{\prime}\ \tau,(\sigma_{s},\sigma_{t})\in R\Rightarrow\sigma_{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\hookrightarrow}}\sigma_{t}^{\prime}\Rightarrow\exists\sigma_{s}^{\prime},(\sigma_{s}^{\prime},\sigma_{t}^{\prime})\in R\wedge\sigma_{s}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\hookrightarrow}}{\!{}^{\!*}}\sigma_{s}^{\prime}

It reads that for every step taken by the compiled target program, there exists zero or more consistent steps in the source program, such that the two programs remain in a related state.
Despite the general success of operational semantics in CompCert, the simulation technique and small-step based proofs of CompCert face the following inconvenience compared to denotational semantics.

(a)

The backward simulation for
f
and
g

(b)

The backward simulation for whole programs

Figure 4
.
Backward simulation diagrams used in CompCert.
This figure describes two backward-simulation diagrams in CompCert. Subfigure (a) shows a previously established simulation between the internal functions \texttt{f} and \texttt{g}: execution steps of \texttt{g} are matched by corresponding execution steps of \texttt{f} through a sequence of related intermediate states, with no observable events involved. Subfigure (b) shows the larger source and target programs obtained after placing \texttt{f} and \texttt{g} into surrounding code with \texttt{print} statements. The observable outputs introduced by the reordered \texttt{print} operations change the simulation structure, so the intermediate correspondence used for \texttt{f} and \texttt{g} cannot be reused directly for the whole programs. The figure illustrates why backward simulation in CompCert is sensitive to surrounding context and observable events.

Intermediate correspondence.

Consider the following transformation, where
x
and
y
are temporary variables,

f
and
g
are pure internal functions, which return identical results for the same input and generate no observable events, and the built-in function
print
outputs the value of its argument.

\displaystyle\text{Source program }S:\ \ \text{\footnotesize print(x); y = f(x); print(y);}

\displaystyle\text{Transformed to }T:\ \ \text{\footnotesize y = g(x); print(x); print(y);}

Suppose that a (backward) simulation between
f
and
g
has been established in advance, where nontrivial intermediate correspondence may be constructed by a series of proof steps. In CompCert, however, these proof steps cannot be directly reused to establish simulation relations between the larger programs
S
and
T
, since the rearrangement of
print
statements introduces observable events that alter the simulation structure, as illustrated in Fig.
4
. Consequently, subroutine equivalence proofs must be revisited when establishing correspondence of larger programs.
In comparison, if using denotational semantics, the established semantic equivalence between
f
and
g
could potentially be reused in proving the equivalence of the programs
S
and
T
.

Nondeterminism correspondence

More importantly, it is hard to use CompCert’s (backward) simulation techniques to verify transformations
where (demonic) nondeterminism straddles event synchronization and thereby impacts later observable behavior. Consider the following example:

Source program:

choice(x = 0, x = 1); print(0); print(x);

Transformed to:

print(0); choice(x = 0, x = 1); print(x);

where the statement “
choice
” nondeterministically chooses to execute either “
x = 0
” or “
x = 1
”.
The transformation swaps the first two statements while preserving the set of possible observable behaviors (i.e., traces
00
and
01
). Intuitively, the denotational semantics of the programs before and after the transformation are equivalent. However, establishing this equivalence is difficult using CompCert’s current simulation theories.
Because CompCert simulation theories constrain a one-to-one match of observable events (i.e., strict event synchronization), the observable event produced by the first step of the target program must correspond to the observable event produced by the first two steps of the source program. At this point, the source program must first choose to execute either “
x = 0
” or “
x = 1
”, and whichever is chosen, the subsequent choice in the target program may differ, potentially leading to inconsistent results for “
print(x)
” in the end.

Program-state correspondence

CompCert models the semantics of high-level languages using mixed program states integrating continuations (to represent control flow and nested calls) together with
the definitions of statements and functions currently under execution.
As a result, establishing correspondences between program states necessarily involves
all components of these enriched states.
In comparison,
the standard denotational semantics of a statement
c
with procedure calls
(as presented, for example, in Winskel’s textbook
(
Winskel, 1993
)
)
interprets
c
as a function mapping the behaviors of callees to the overall behavior of
c
.
This naturally leads to a formulation of transformation correctness:
a transformation
\mathcal{T}
is correct if it preserves such semantic mappings.

\displaystyle\llbracket c\rrbracket:\text{mapping from ``behaviors of source callee procedures'' to ``behavior of }c\text{''}

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c)\rrbracket:\text{mapping from ``behaviors of compiled callee procedures'' to ``behavior of }\mathcal{T}(c)\text{''}

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}\text{ is correct iff for any callee behaviors }\chi_{s}\ \chi_{t},\text{ if }\chi_{t}\sqsubseteq\chi_{s}\text{, then }\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c)\rrbracket(\chi_{t})\sqsubseteq\llbracket c\rrbracket(\chi_{s})

This formalization benefits from two aspects: (i) we can simplify the definition of program states and their correspondence, and further decompose the refinement proof into smaller and simpler conclusions by splitting the original correspondence of CompCert into static environment correspondence and dynamic memory correspondence; (ii) we can define semantic linking operators by taking semantic fixed points, and we further find that the equivalence between semantic linking and syntactic linking is reduced to the concise Bekić’s theorem
(
Bekić, 1984
)
about fixed points.

To sum up,
the above statements demonstrate the potential benefits of denotational semantics for composing proofs, handling nondeterministic correspondence, and lightening program-state correspondence.
Moreover,
we also observe that recent research in simulation theories, such as FreeSim
(
Cho et al., 2023
)
, as well as more advanced approaches like prophecy variables
(
Abadi and Lamport, 1991
;
Lynch and Vaandrager, 1995
)
, proposes mechanisms intended to mitigate these challenges. Some of these approaches, such as FreeSim, have been formalized and partially integrated into existing proof frameworks, and some of them have not yet been formalized and used for realistic compiler verification like prophecy variables. Particularly, they face their own inherent difficulties in tackling the issues discussed above. We will examine these related works in greater detail in §
12
.

2.2.
Denotational Semantics: Achievements and Challenges

Denotational semantics enjoys good compositionality, meaning that the semantics of a syntactic construct can be systematically derived from the semantics of its substructures using semantic operators. A central concern in this approach is handling loops and recursion effectively. Existing research on deterministic programs demonstrates that the semantics of recursive constructs can be defined on the basis of domain theory
(
Scott, 1970
;
Scott and Strachey, 1971
)
or Kleene Algebras
(
Kozen, 1997
)
. In domain theory, the semantics of programs are modeled as elements of domains (typically CPOs), and the key tool in domain theory is the Kleene fixed-point theorem where recursive constructs are given meaning by finding the least fixed point of a continuous function. The concepts of CPOs, continuous functions and Kleene fixed points are outlined as follows.

Definition 2.1 (Complete Partial Order).

A
Directed Complete Partial Order (DCPO)
is a partially ordered set
P
such that every
directed
subset
D\subseteq P
has a supremum (i.e.
\sup D
) in
P
. A subset
D
of
P
is directed if it is nonempty and for any elements
x,y\in D
, there exists an element
z\in D
such that
x\leq z
and
y\leq z
. A DCPO with a least element
\bot
is usually called a
pointed
DCPO.

Definition 2.2 (Scott-Continuous Function).

A function
f
between two DCPOs is called
Scott-continuous
if it preserves the directed suprema, i.e., for any directed subset
D
of the domain,

f(\sup D)=\sup f(D)

Theorem 2.3 (Kleene fixed point).

Suppose
(A,\leq_{A})
is a directed-complete partial order (DCPO) with a least element
\bot
, and let
F:A\rightarrow A
be a Scott-continuous (and therefore monotone) function. Then
F
has a least fixed point
\mu F=\sup(\{F^{n}(\bot)\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\})
, where
\sup
means taking the supremum.

Kleene algebras are algebraic structures with operators
\circ
,
\cup
,
∗
,
\emptyset
,
\mathbb{1}
that satisfy certain axioms. Typically, the family of binary relations with operators
\cup
,
\circ
, the empty relation
\varnothing
, the identity relation
\mathbb{1}
and the reflexive transitive closure
∗
constitute a Kleene Algebra, where iteration is captured algebraically through the star operator.
The following example shows the definition of the textbook denotational semantics on a toy language, by domain theory or by Kleene Algebras.

Example 2.4.

Consider a simple deterministic and type-safe toy language whose syntactic constructs (extremely simplified for presentation purpose) and semantics can be defined as follows.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} c\triangleq\textbf{skip}\ |\ c_{1};c_{2}\ |\ \textbf{if}\ b\ \textbf{then}\ c_{1}\ \textbf{else}\ c_{2}\ |\ \textbf{while}\ b\ \textbf{do}\ c\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \llbracket\textbf{skip}\rrbracket\triangleq\mathbb{1}\quad\llbracket c_{1};c_{2}\rrbracket\triangleq\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket\circ\llbracket c_{2}\rrbracket\quad\textbf{test}(X)\triangleq\{(\sigma,\sigma)\mid\sigma\in X\}\text{, for }X\subseteq\text{\footnotesize state}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \llbracket\textbf{if}\ b\ \textbf{then}\ c_{1}\ \textbf{else}\ c_{2}\rrbracket\triangleq\mathbf{test}(\llbracket b\rrbracket.\text{\footnotesize(tts)})\circ\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket\cup\mathbf{test}(\llbracket b\rrbracket.\text{\footnotesize(ffs)})\circ\llbracket c_{2}\rrbracket\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \llbracket\textbf{while}\ b\ \textbf{do}\ c\rrbracket\triangleq\mu x.\textbf{test}(\llbracket b\rrbracket.\text{\footnotesize(ffs)})\cup\big(\textbf{test}(\llbracket b\rrbracket.\text{\footnotesize(tts)})\circ\llbracket c\rrbracket\circ x\big),\text{ by domain theory}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \llbracket\textbf{while}\ b\ \textbf{do}\ c\rrbracket&\triangleq\big(\textbf{test}(\llbracket b\rrbracket.\text{\footnotesize(tts)})\circ\llbracket c\rrbracket\big)^{*}\circ\textbf{test}(\llbracket b\rrbracket.\text{\footnotesize(ffs)}),\text{ by Kleene Algebras}\\
\text{where }\llbracket b\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize tts})}&\triangleq\{\sigma\mid\sigma(b)=\textbf{T}\},\ \llbracket b\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize ffs})}\triangleq\{\sigma\mid\sigma(b)=\textbf{F}\},\ \mathbb{1}=\{(\sigma,\sigma)\mid\sigma\in\text{\footnotesize state}\}\end{aligned}

Although domain theory and Kleene algebras have a long research history and a strong mathematical foundation, it remains challenging to precisely characterize the full semantics of practical programming languages, which may exhibit termination, divergence, or even abortion.
We may naively construct a domain by treating abortion as the least element in the partial order and regarding normal termination and divergence as incomparable (i.e., without any ordering between them). However, in a nondeterministic program, a state may nondeterministically transition to either a terminating or a diverging state. If these outcomes are incomparable, the semantics cannot represent their “join” (supremum) in a manner consistent with the domain’s ordering. This inconsistency prevents the definition of a continuous function required by the Kleene fixed-point theorem.
In order to handle nondeterministic and possible nonterminating behaviors
6
6

6

Unless otherwise specified, nondeterminism means demonic nondeterminism.
, computer scientists proposed three classical power domain constructions, known as Hoare powerdomain, Smyth powerdomain
(
Smyth, 1978
)
, and Plotkin powerdomain
(
Plotkin, 1976
)
, in the 1970s.
Power domains in our context are constructed from a flat domain
(D,\leqslant_{D})
, which may stand for possible results of executions. Suppose that for any
x
and
y
in
D
,

x\leqslant_{D}y\Leftrightarrow(x=\bot\vee x=y)

a power domain of
D
, written as
\mathcal{M}(D)
, involves the subsets of the ground domain
D
and a new partial order
\sqsubseteq
, such that (
\mathcal{M}(D)
,
\sqsubseteq
) forms a CPO and one can take a fixed point over it. For any
X
and
Y
in
\mathcal{M}(D)
, there are generally three natural ways to construct the new ordering:

\displaystyle X\sqsubseteq_{0}Y\Leftrightarrow\forall x\in X,\exists y\in Y,x\leqslant_{D}y

\displaystyle X\sqsubseteq_{1}Y\Leftrightarrow\forall y\in Y,\exists x\in X,x\leqslant_{D}y

\displaystyle X\sqsubseteq_{2}Y\Leftrightarrow X\sqsubseteq_{0}Y\wedge X\sqsubseteq_{1}Y

The Hoare powerdomain.

The Hoare powerdomain is established with the first ordering
\sqsubseteq_{0}
meaning that everything
X
can do,
Y
can do better. However, this ordering is just a preorder but not a partial order since it fails antisymmetry.
The issue is solved by defining an equivalence relation
\simeq_{0}
(
X\simeq_{0}Y
iff
X\sqsubseteq_{0}Y\text{ and }Y\sqsubseteq_{0}X
) and restricting that every set
X
in
\mathcal{M}(D)
is a
downset
. Specifically,

X\text{ is a \emph{downset} iff }X\simeq_{0}\ \downarrow X,\text{ where }\downarrow X\triangleq\{x_{0}\in D\mid\exists x\in X,x_{0}\leqslant_{D}x\}

Consequently, with the Hoare powerdomain, one gets an angelic semantics for nondeterminism since the downsets always include the least element
\bot
. That means, any program that could either terminate or diverge will have the same meaning with its terminating behavior. For instance, given the following four programs, program 1 and 3 will have the same semantics under this power domain (where“
x = ?
” in program 4 means “nondeterministically set
x
to any natural number”).

⬇

4.

$
\
textbf
{
while
}
$

(
y

$
==
$

2

$
||
$

x

>

0)

$
\
textbf
{
do
}
$

$
\
textbf
{
if
}
$

(
y

$
==
$

2)

$
\
textbf
{
then
}
$

y

=

1;

x

=

$
\
ttt
{?}
$

$
\
textbf
{
else
}
$

x

=

x

-

1

The Smyth powerdomain.

The Smyth powerdomain is obtained with the preorder
\sqsubseteq_{1}
, which says that everything
Y
can do is approximated by some behavior of
X
. Since this ordering is still a preorder but not a partial order, the Smyth powerdomain defines the preorder’s natural equivalence
\simeq_{1}
(
X\simeq_{1}Y
iff
X\sqsubseteq_{1}Y\text{ and }Y\sqsubseteq_{1}X
) and constrains every element
X
in
\mathcal{M}(D)
to be an
upset
, i.e.,

X\text{ is an \emph{upset} iff }X\simeq_{1}\ \uparrow X,\text{ where }\uparrow X\triangleq\{x_{0}\in D\mid\exists x\in X,x\leqslant_{D}x_{0}\}

As a result, one will achieve a demonic semantics in the sense that
any program that can diverge has the semantics
\uparrow\{\bot\}=D
. Thus, for instance, programs 2 and 3 will have the same semantics.

The Plotkin powerdomain.

The Plotkin powerdomain uses the well-known
Egli-Milner ordering

\sqsubseteq_{2}
, with which every set
X
in
\mathcal{M}(D)
is restricted to be equivalent with its
convex
closure:

conv(X)\triangleq\{x_{1}\in D\mid\exists x_{0},x_{2}\in X,x_{0}\leqslant_{D}x_{1}\leqslant_{D}x_{2}\}

By this powerdomain, all the first three programs will have different meanings as one would expect. However, some constructs like program 4 that can produce infinitely many different results and yet be certain to terminate, i.e., unbounded nondeterminism, are excluded (see
Back (1983)
for details).

To sum up, power domains often fail to capture the full range of possible program outcomes. Such inability is known as an intrinsic defect of domain theory.

Park’s approach

Park (1979)
proposed an alternative method to characterize the possible nonterminating behavior of nondeterministic programs, by introducing an additional set to capture nonterminating behaviors.
However, when observable events are considered, it is still challenging to explicitly distinguish reactive divergence and silent divergence for realistic compiler verification.

In a word, a notable gap exists between denotational semantics and realistic programming languages. Additionally, practical programs often include structured (e.g.,
break
,
continue
) and even unstructured branches (e.g.,
goto
), which may prematurely terminate the execution of subsequent statements and hence disrupt the elegant property shown in Formula (
1
). Precisely capturing the full meaning of realistic programs while still keeping good compositionality turns out to be a big challenge.
We show the solution of this problem in §
3
and then extend it to open modules in §
4
.

3.
Denotational Semantics of Statements (No Procedure Calls)

3.1.
The Formal Framework for Defining Denotations

Our solution is to separately define different program behaviors of interest using different sets, with each behavior set defined by corresponding semantic operators in the form of an extended Kleene Algebra with Tests (KAT).
This manipulation is somewhat similar to Park’s approach. In comparison, we design new mechanisms to handle divergence with event traces, capture the full meaning of realistic programs, and especially concisely formalize them in
Rocq
.

Consider a type-safe toy language WHILE whose set of statements
Com
, ranged over by
c
, is parameterized on the set of Boolean expressions
Exp
ranged over by
b
(no side effects):

c\triangleq\textbf{skip}\ |\ atom\ |\ \textbf{choice}(c_{1},c_{2})\ |\ c_{1};c_{2}\ |\ \textbf{if}\ b\ \textbf{then}\ c_{1}\ \textbf{else}\ c_{2}\ |\ \textbf{while}\ b\ \textbf{do}\ c

Nondeterminism can be introduced either by atomic statements (ranged over by
atom
) like nondeterministic assignments or by
choice
statements that unpredictably choose
c_{1}
or
c_{2}
to execute.
We formalize the denotation of statements and Boolean expressions with the following signature.

⬇

Record

CDenote
:

Type

:=

{

nrm
:

state

\rightarrow\;

state

\rightarrow\;

Prop
;

(*

nrm

\subseteq

state

\times

state

*)

dvg
:

state

\rightarrow\;

Prop

(*

dvg

\subseteq

state

*)

}.

Record

BDenote
:

Type

:=

{

tts
:

state

\rightarrow\;

Prop
;

(*

tts

\subseteq

state

*)

ffs
:

state

\rightarrow\;

Prop

(*

ffs

\subseteq

state

*)

}.

Specifically,
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\in\llbracket c\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm})
iff executing
c
from initial state
\sigma_{1}
could terminate on state
\sigma_{2}
, and
\sigma\in\llbracket c\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize dvg})
iff executing
c
from
\sigma
could diverge.
An element
\sigma\in\llbracket b\rrbracket
.(
tts
) iff state
\sigma
satisfies
b
, and
\sigma\in\llbracket b\rrbracket
.(
ffs
) iff state
\sigma
does not satisfy
b
.
We can use set operators (like union) and relation operators (like composition) to define program semantics as follows.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\llbracket\textbf{skip}\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm})\triangleq\mathbb{1}\quad\llbracket\textbf{skip}\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize dvg})\triangleq\mathbf{\varnothing}\;\;\;\!&\llbracket\textbf{choice}(c_{1},c_{2})\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm})\triangleq\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm})\cup\llbracket c_{2}\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm})\\
&\llbracket c_{1};c_{2}\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm})\triangleq\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm})\circ\llbracket c_{2}\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm})&\llbracket\textbf{choice}(c_{1},c_{2})\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize dvg})\triangleq\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize dvg})\cup\llbracket c_{2}\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize dvg})\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \llbracket\textbf{if}\ b\ \textbf{then}\ c_{1}\ \textbf{else}\ c_{2}\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm})\triangleq\mathbf{test}(\llbracket b\rrbracket.\text{\footnotesize(tts)})\circ\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm})\cup\mathbf{test}(\llbracket b\rrbracket.\text{\footnotesize(ffs)})\circ\llbracket c_{2}\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm})\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \llbracket\textbf{while}\ b\ \textbf{do}\ c\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm})\triangleq\big(\textbf{test}(\llbracket b\rrbracket.\text{\footnotesize(tts)})\circ\llbracket c\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm})\big)^{*}\circ\textbf{test}(\llbracket b\rrbracket.\text{\footnotesize(ffs)}),\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \ \ \text{where}\quad&\textbf{test}(X)\triangleq\{(\sigma,\sigma)\mid\sigma\in X\}\text{, for }X\subseteq\text{\footnotesize state}&\text{identity relation on }X\\
&\mathbb{1}\triangleq\{(\sigma,\sigma)\mid\sigma\in\text{\footnotesize state}\}&\text{identity relation on }\text{\footnotesize state}\\
&R^{*}\triangleq\mathbb{1}\cup R\cup(R\circ R)\cup\cdots\cup R^{n}\cup\cdots&\text{reflexive transitive closure}\\
&R^{+}\triangleq R\cup(R\circ R)\cup\cdots\cup R^{n}\cup\cdots&\text{transitive closure}\\
&R^{\infty}\triangleq R\circ R\circ R\circ\cdots&\text{infinitely iterated composition}\end{aligned}

As is well known, the family of binary relations with operators
\cup
,
\circ
, the empty relation
\varnothing
, the identity relation
\mathbb{1}
and the reflexive transitive closure
∗
constitute a Kleene Algebra.
Moreover, if we overload the “
\circ
’’ operator as follows,

\displaystyle R_{1}\circ R_{2}\triangleq\{\left(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{3}\right)\mid\exists\sigma_{2},(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\in R_{1}\wedge(\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3})\in R_{2}\}

original

\displaystyle R\circ X\triangleq\{\sigma_{1}\mid\exists\sigma_{2},(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\in R\wedge\sigma_{2}\in X\}

overloaded

these operators form a typed Kleene Algebra
(
Kozen, 1998
)
with two base types.
The diverging behavior of sequential,
if
, and
while
statements can then be formalized as follows.

\displaystyle\llbracket c_{1};c_{2}\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize dvg})\triangleq\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize dvg})\cup\big(\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm})\circ\llbracket c_{2}\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize dvg})\big)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \llbracket\textbf{if}\ b\ \textbf{then}\ c_{1}\ \textbf{else}\ c_{2}\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize dvg})\triangleq\big(\mathbf{test}(\llbracket b\rrbracket.\text{\footnotesize(tts)})\circ\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize dvg})\big)\cup\big(\mathbf{test}(\llbracket b\rrbracket.\text{\footnotesize(ffs)})\circ\llbracket c_{2}\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize dvg})\big)\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \llbracket\textbf{while}\ b\ \textbf{do}\ c\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize dvg})\triangleq\,&\big({\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}(}\mathbf{test}(\llbracket b\rrbracket.\text{\footnotesize(tts)})\circ\llbracket c\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm}){\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0})}^{*}\circ{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}(}\mathbf{test}(\llbracket b\rrbracket.\text{\footnotesize(tts)})\circ\llbracket c\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize dvg}){\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0})}\big)\ \cup\\
&\big(\mathbf{test}(\llbracket b\rrbracket.\text{\footnotesize(tts)})\circ\llbracket c\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm})\big)^{\infty}\end{aligned}

The interesting case is that the
while
statement diverges if and only if either the loop body
c
diverges after a finite number of normally executing
c
, or
c
is executed an infinite number of times.

The above style of modeling denotational semantics based on a typed Kleene Algebra can be naturally scaled to realistic settings by extended denotations and redefined operators.
In CompCert, the behavior of a C program encompasses not only state transitions but also the observable events (I/O and other system calls) triggered during execution. Therefore, we can formalize the denotation of C-like program statements with the following signature, where
trace
is the set of all finite sequences of observable events, and
itrace
the set of all infinite sequences of observable events.

⬇

Record

Denote
:

Type

:=

{

nrm
:

state

\rightarrow\;

trace

\rightarrow\;

state

\rightarrow\;

Prop
;

(*

nrm

\subseteq

state

\times

trace

\times

state

*)

…

err
:

state

\rightarrow\;

trace

\rightarrow\;

Prop
;

(*

err

\subseteq

state

\times

trace

*)

fin_dvg
:

state

\rightarrow\;

trace

\rightarrow\;

Prop
;

(*

fin_dvg

\subseteq

state

\times

trace

*)

inf_dvg
:

state

\rightarrow\;

itrace

\rightarrow\;

Prop

(*

inf_dvg

\subseteq

state

\times

itrace

*)

}.

Specifically, the terminating behavior here is extended to a ternary relation which additionally records the event trace generated during execution.
Diverging behavior is divided into finite and infinite parts for distinguishing behaviors with finite events and infinite events.
In addition, we use a set
err
to capture the aborting behavior of programs.
We then redefine operators
\circ
,
test
, and
\mathbb{1}
(and therefore the operator for reflexive transitive closure) as follows, where
R\subseteq\text{\footnotesize state}\times\text{\footnotesize trace}\times\text{\footnotesize state}
,
Y\subseteq\text{\footnotesize state}\times(\text{\footnotesize trace}\cup\text{\footnotesize itrace})
, “
nil
” is the empty sequence and “
\cdot
” is the concatenation of sequences.

\displaystyle\mathbb{1}\triangleq\{(\sigma,\text{\footnotesize nil},\sigma)\mid\sigma\in\text{\footnotesize state}\}\quad\quad\ \ \textbf{test}(X)\triangleq\{(\sigma,\text{\footnotesize nil},\sigma)\mid\sigma\in X\},\text{ for }X\subseteq\text{\footnotesize state}

\displaystyle R\circ Y\triangleq\{(\sigma_{1},\tau)\mid\exists\sigma_{2}\ \tau_{1}\ \tau_{2},(\sigma_{1},\tau_{1},\sigma_{2})\in R\wedge(\sigma_{2},\tau_{2})\in Y\wedge\tau=\tau_{1}\cdot\tau_{2}\}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} R_{1}\circ R_{2}\triangleq\{\left(\sigma_{1},\tau,\sigma_{3}\right)\mid\exists\sigma_{2}\ \tau_{1}\ \tau_{2},\ (\sigma_{1},\tau_{1},\sigma_{2})\in R_{1}\wedge(\sigma_{2},\tau_{2},\sigma_{3})\in R_{2}\wedge\tau=\tau_{1}\cdot\tau_{2}\}\end{aligned}

In summary, we use various behavior sets to define different behaviors of interest with typed operators, thus solving the limitations of traditional powerdomains in compiler verification scenarios. In our implementation, these sets operators are provided by a
Rocq
set library
(
Cao et al., 2023
)
.
Besides, we address two additional challenges for practical languages: supporting various control flow constructs (in §
3.2
) and defining program divergence in the presence of event traces (in §
3.3
).

3.2.
Semantics of Control Flow Constructs

For handling different control flow statements like
break
and
continue
in Clight, or
block
and
exit
in Csharpminor, we add corresponding fields of sets to the denotation record.

Clight

The Clight language provides control flow constructs including
break
,
continue
, and
return
statements to prematurely terminate the normal execution of statements. To capture the behavior of these program constructs, we add the following sets to
Denote
.

⬇

brk
:

state

\rightarrow\;

trace

\rightarrow\;

state

\rightarrow\;

Prop
;

(*

brk

\subseteq

state

\times

trace

\times

state

*)

ctn
:

state

\rightarrow\;

trace

\rightarrow\;

state

\rightarrow\;

Prop
;

(*

ctn

\subseteq

state

\times

trace

\times

state

*)

rtn
:

state

\rightarrow\;

trace

\rightarrow\;

state

\rightarrow\;

val

\rightarrow\;

Prop
.

(*

rtn

\subseteq

state

\times

trace

\times

state

\times

val

*)

These sets represent that for any element
(\sigma_{0},\tau,\sigma_{1})
in
\llbracket s\rrbracket.
(
brk
) or
\llbracket s\rrbracket.
(
ctn
), the execution of Clight statement
s
from
\sigma_{0}
could eventually reach state
\sigma_{1}
and then exit because of a
break
or
continue
statement, producing a sequence of observable events
\tau
. In other words, once a
break
or
continue
statement is encountered, the execution will end prematurely, and the corresponding set
brk
or
ctn
records how it ends. Therefore, we formalize such semantics as follows (selectively listed).

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\llbracket\textbf{break}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize brk})}\triangleq\mathbb{1},\text{ and other fields are assigned the empty set}.\\
&\llbracket\textbf{continue}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize ctn})}\triangleq\mathbb{1},\text{ and other fields are assigned the empty set}\\
&\llbracket s_{1};s_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize brk})}\triangleq\llbracket s_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize brk})}\cup\big(\llbracket s_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\circ\llbracket s_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize brk})}\big)\\
&\llbracket s_{1};s_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize ctn})}\triangleq\llbracket s_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize ctn})}\cup\big(\llbracket s_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\circ\llbracket s_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize ctn})}\big)\end{aligned}

The semantics of exiting by a
return
statement can be defined similarly since the
rtn
set has a similar meaning but additionally with a return value (where
val
is the set of values ranging over integers, floats, pointers, as well as an undefined value
Vundef
).
Clight uses one special loop (
loop

s_{1}

s_{2}
) to encode all three kinds of C loops. Its semantics is to repeatedly and sequentially execute statements
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
until a certain break or return statement, and a “continue jump” in
s_{1}
will branch to
s_{2}
.
Therefore, the
for
statement of C language for example can be defined as:

\textbf{for}(s_{1};b;s_{2})\ \{s_{3}\}\triangleq s_{1};\ \big(\textbf{loop }\big((\textbf{if }b\textbf{ then skip else}\textbf{ break});s_{3}\big)\ s_{2}\big)

We then formalize the terminating behavior of Clight loops as follows, where
N_{1}
denotes the behavior that the loop body
s_{1}
either ends normally or prematurely due to a continue statement in
s_{1}
, and
N_{12}
denotes the behavior of a “sequential” execution of loop bodies
s_{1}
and then
s_{2}
.

(3)

\displaystyle N_{1}=\llbracket s_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\cup\llbracket s_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize ctn})}\quad\quad N_{12}=N_{1}\circ\llbracket s_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}

(4)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \llbracket\textbf{loop }s_{1}\ s_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\triangleq&\ N_{12}^{*}\circ\big(\llbracket s_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize brk})}\cup(N_{1}\circ\llbracket s_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize brk})})\big)\end{aligned}

Csharpminor

The control flow of Csharpminor is structured with the
block
and
exit
statement. For instance, the execution of statement (block { block {
u_{1}
; exit(1)};
u_{2}
};
u_{3}
) is equivalent to the execution of (
u_{1};u_{3}
) since the statement
exit
(
n
) prematurely terminates the execution of the (
n
+1) layers of nested
block
statements.
We then use a function mapping from natural numbers to relations to capture this behavior, so that an element
(
\sigma_{0},\tau,\sigma_{1})\in

\llbracket u\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize blk})}_{n}
if and only if executing the Csharpminor statement
u
from
\sigma_{0}
could terminate at
\sigma_{1}
, and
n
records the layers of nested blocks to exit.
Thus, the denotation of Csharpminor statements
\llbracket u\rrbracket
satisfies the following equations.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \llbracket\textbf{block}\{u\}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\triangleq\llbracket u\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\cup\llbracket u\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize blk})}_{0}\quad\quad\,\llbracket\textbf{block}\{u\}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize blk})}_{n}\triangleq\llbracket u\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize blk})}_{n+1}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \llbracket\textbf{exit}(n_{0})\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize blk})}_{n}\triangleq\mathbb{1}\text{, if }n_{0}=n\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\llbracket\textbf{exit}(n_{0})\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize blk})}_{n}\triangleq\varnothing\text{, if }n_{0}\neq n\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \llbracket u_{1};u_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize blk})}_{n}\triangleq\llbracket u_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize blk})}_{n}\cup\big(\llbracket u_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\circ\llbracket u_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize blk})}_{n}\big)\end{aligned}

The loop structure of Csharpminor is an infinite loop (
Sloop

u
), whose behavior is to execute the loop body
u
repeatedly until a certain early exit. The semantics is defined as follows.

\displaystyle\llbracket\textbf{Sloop }u\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\triangleq\varnothing\quad\quad\llbracket\textbf{Sloop }u\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize blk})}_{n}\triangleq\llbracket u\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}^{*}\circ\llbracket u\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize blk})}_{n}

3.3.
Divergence with Event Trace

Handling divergence with event traces is more involved. Previous research indicates that simulations between semantics have to address the classic stuttering problem
(
Leroy, 2009
)
. Therefore, unlike defining divergence directly using
R^{\infty}
in simple languages, we must explicitly consider whether the event trace in program execution is empty or not. We demand that
R^{\infty}
is only defined if either all traces in
R
are nonempty (i.e., nonsilent), or all traces in
R
are empty (i.e., silent).
To this end, we use silent operator
\triangle
and nonsilent operator
\blacktriangle
to explicitly filter silent and nonsilent event traces as follows, where
R\subseteq\text{\footnotesize state}\times\text{\footnotesize trace}\times\text{\footnotesize state}
and
Y\subseteq\text{\footnotesize state}\times(\text{\footnotesize trace}\cup\text{\footnotesize itrace})
.

\displaystyle\blacktriangle R

\displaystyle\triangleq\{(\sigma,\tau,\sigma^{\prime})\mid(\sigma,\tau,\sigma^{\prime})\in R\wedge\tau\neq\text{\footnotesize nil}\}

\displaystyle\triangle R

\displaystyle\triangleq\{(\sigma,\tau,\sigma^{\prime})\mid(\sigma,\tau,\sigma^{\prime})\in R\wedge\tau=\text{\footnotesize nil}\}

\displaystyle\triangle Y

\displaystyle\triangleq\{(\sigma,\tau)\mid(\sigma,\tau)\in Y\wedge\tau=\text{\footnotesize nil}\}

Recall that
\llbracket s\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize fin\_dvg})}
and
\llbracket s\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize inf\_dvg})}
denote diverging behavior with finite and infinite event traces generated by executing statement
s
, respectively.
For Clight loops,
\llbracket\textbf{loop }s_{1}\ s_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize fin\_dvg})}
indicates that after sequentially executing the loop bodies
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
a finite number of times, a Clight loop could silently diverge since
s_{1}
does, or
s_{2}
does, or the entire loop itself silently diverges.
Similarly, the nonsilently diverging behavior (i.e, reacting behavior)
\llbracket\textbf{loop }s_{1}\ s_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize inf\_dvg})}
indicates that after sequentially executing the loop bodies
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
a finite number of times, a Clight loop could nonsilently diverge since
s_{1}
does, or
s_{2}
does, or the entire loop itself nonsilently diverges.
They are formalized as follows, where
N_{1}
and
N_{12}
are defined by Formula (
3
).

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \llbracket\textbf{loop }s_{1}\ s_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize fin\_dvg})}\triangleq N_{12}^{*}\circ\big(\llbracket s_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize fin\_dvg})}\cup(N_{1}\circ\llbracket s_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize fin\_dvg})})\cup(\triangle N_{12})^{\infty}\big)\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \llbracket\textbf{loop }s_{1}\ s_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize inf\_dvg})}\triangleq\Big(\big((\triangle N_{12})^{*}\circ\blacktriangle N_{12}\big)^{*}\circ(\triangle N_{12})^{*}\circ\mathbb{D}\Big)\cup\big((\triangle N_{12})^{*}\circ\blacktriangle N_{12}\big)^{\infty}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\begin{aligned} \text{where }\mathbb{D}=\llbracket s_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize inf\_dvg})}\cup\big(N_{1}\circ\llbracket s_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize inf\_dvg})}\big)\end{aligned}

Remark that
\mathbb{D}
denotes the reacting behavior by the loop body
s_{1}
or
s_{2}
,
and
(\triangle N_{12})^{*}\circ\blacktriangle N_{12}
denotes that one observable behavior must be triggered after silently looping a finite number of times.

4.
Denotational Semantics of Procedure Calls

So far, we have shown how to precisely define the denotational semantics of programs with nondeterministic and nonterminating behaviors without considering procedure calls.

Another challenge in applying such semantics to realistic compiler verification is how to model the semantics of open modules and make it compositional, in the sense that the semantics of every separately compiled module can be linked together and keep behavioral consistency with the linked semantics of source modules. Our key idea, extended from the standard way of defining denotational semantics for
whole programs
with procedure calls, is to define an open module’s denotation under a semantic “oracle” that describes the terminating behavior of external calls. Then when linking, the semantics is given by merging the denotation of modules and then taking fixed points, in which external calls may now be “handled” by one of the linked modules.
In this section, we begin with a simple language for introducing the standard denotational semantics for programs with procedure calls in §
4.1
, and then propose a denotational semantics for open modules and a novel semantic linking operator in §
4.2
. Finally, their divergence is defined in §
4.3
.

For presentation, this section ignores observable events and focuses solely on procedure calls.

4.1.
Standard Denotational Semantics of Procedure Calls (Whole Program)

Consider a language, PCALL, defined as follows: a whole program in PCALL consists of a list of procedures. Each procedure
p
, belonging to a set
Pdef
, is characterized by its procedure name
id_{p}
, a list of local variables
x_{1};...;x_{n}
, and its procedure body
c_{p}
, which is represented as a statement.

\begin{array}[]{l@{\qquad}l}\begin{aligned} \text{Statements: }c\triangleq\textbf{skip}\ |\ atom\ |\ \textbf{choice}(c_{1},c_{2})\ |\ c_{1};c_{2}\ |\ &\\
\textbf{if}\ b\ \textbf{then}\ c_{1}\ \textbf{else}\ c_{2}\ |\ \textbf{while}\ b\ \textbf{do}\ c\ |\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textbf{call}\ id}\;&\end{aligned}&\begin{aligned} &\text{Procedures: }p\triangleq(id_{p},x_{1};\dots;x_{n},c_{p})\\
&\text{Whole programs: }P\triangleq p_{1};\dots;p_{n}\end{aligned}\end{array}

In this toy language, procedure calls have no arguments and no return values.
Before defining its semantics, we partition program states into local and global components:
state

\triangleq\text{\footnotesize lstate}\times\text{\footnotesize gstate}
. Let the symbol
l
range over the set of local states
lstate
and
g
over the set of global states
gstate
.

The standard way
(
Back, 1983
;
Park, 1979
)
to define the denotational semantics of programs with procedure calls is parameterizing the denotation of procedures with the behavior of invoked procedures, and then taking the Kleene fixed point (see Thm.
2.3
) on recursive domain equations.

Specifically,
let
ident
be the set of procedure names. Consider a semantic oracle
\chi
(
\subseteq\text{\footnotesize ident}\times\text{\footnotesize gstate}\times\text{\footnotesize gstate}
) such that an element
(id,g_{0},g_{1})\in\chi
if and only if executing the procedure named
id
from global state
g_{0}
could normally terminate at global state
g_{1}
.
The terminating behavior of PCALL statements is still a binary relation
\llbracket c\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}
(
\subseteq\text{\footnotesize state}\times\text{\footnotesize state}
) so that an element
(\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1})\in\llbracket c\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}
if and only if
executing statement
c
from state
\sigma_{0}
could terminate at state
\sigma_{1}
, in which the semantics of procedure calls will be given by
\chi
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\llbracket\textbf{call }id\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\triangleq\{\left((l,g_{0}),(l,g_{1})\right)\mid(id,g_{0},g_{1})\in\chi\}

Notably, the local state remains unchanged after executing the procedure call.
The semantics of other PCALL statements can refer to the semantic definitions of WHILE statements, for example:

\displaystyle\llbracket c_{1};c_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\triangleq\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\circ\llbracket c_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}

We further use
\llbracket p\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}
(
\subseteq\{id_{p}\}\times\text{\footnotesize gstate}\times\text{\footnotesize gstate}
) to represent the terminating behavior of the procedure
p
such that an element
(id_{p},g_{0},g_{1})\in\llbracket p\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}
if and only if executing procedure
p
at global state
g_{0}
could eventually terminate at global state
g_{1}
. It’s easy to see
\llbracket p\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}
can be derived from the semantics of its body
\llbracket c_{p}\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}
as illustrated in Fig.
5
, and the formal definition is shown in Formula (
5
), where the function
Init
:
Pdef

\times

gstate

\rightarrow

state
gives the initial local and global state after allocating local variables for the execution of the procedure body
c_{p}
, and the function
Free
:
lstate

\times

gstate

\rightarrow

gstate
gives the final global state after freeing local addressed variables.

Figure 5
.
An illustration of the semantic definition of
\llbracket p\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}
.
This figure illustrates how the terminating semantics of a procedure \(p\) is derived from the terminating semantics of its body \(c_p\). Starting from an initial global state \(g_0\), the function \texttt{Init} allocates the procedure’s local variables and produces an initial local-global state \((l_1, g_1)\). The body semantics \(\fofchi{c_p}{nrm}\) then relates this input state to a final local-global state \((l_2, g_2)\) after normal termination of the procedure body. Finally, the function \texttt{Free} releases the local addressed variables and produces the resulting global state \(g_3\).

(5)

\llbracket p\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\triangleq\Set{(id_{p},g_{0},g_{3})}{\exists l_{1}\ l_{2}\ g_{1}\ g_{2},\ \begin{aligned} &\!\big((l_{1},g_{1}),(l_{2},g_{2})\big)\in\llbracket c_{p}\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\ \wedge\\
&(l_{1},g_{1})=\text{\small Init}(p,g_{0})\wedge g_{3}=\text{\small Free}(l_{2},g_{2})\end{aligned}}

Finally, the terminating behavior of the whole program, i.e.,
\llbracket p_{1};...;p_{n}\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm})
, can be defined as:

(6)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \llbracket p_{1};...;p_{n}\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm})\triangleq\mu\chi.\left(\llbracket p_{1}\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\cup\cdots\cup\llbracket p_{n}\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\right)\end{aligned}

According to the Kleene fixed point theorem, Formula (
6
) means that a procedure call could terminate if and only if there exists a natural number
k
such that the procedure call terminates with at most
k
layers of nested calls.
We proceed to discuss how we extend this approach to support semantic linking of open modules in §
4.2
, and how we define the diverging behavior in §
4.3
.

4.2.
Semantics of Open Modules and Semantic Linking

A more realistic program is usually composed of multiple modules (or partial programs), each of which contains a list of procedures and is compiled individually. We find that the traditional approach from
Back (1983)
(which we summarize in §
4.1
) can be easily extended to this setting by parameterizing the semantics of open modules with the terminating behavior of external procedures
8
8

8

External procedures are those not defined in the current module.

.

4.2.1.
Semantics of Open Modules

Formally, we use
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}
(
\subseteq\text{\footnotesize ident}\times\text{\footnotesize gstate}\times\text{\footnotesize gstate}
) to represent the terminating behavior of an open module. It means that an element
(id,g_{0},g_{1})\in\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}
if and only if there exists a procedure named
id
in module
M
and its execution beginning with global state
g_{0}
could finally terminate at global state
g_{1}
, in which the terminating behavior of external procedures is given by an oracle
\chi
(
\subseteq\text{\footnotesize ident}\times\text{\footnotesize gstate}\times\text{\footnotesize gstate}
). The terminating behavior of an open module
M
that consists of a list of procedures
p_{1};...;p_{n}
can then be defined as:

(a)

Semantics of a single procedure
p_{1}

(b)

Semantics of module
M
containing procedure
p_{1}

Figure 6
.
Comparison between the semantics of procedures and that of modules. Here raising edges denote procedure calls (abbr. cll.), falling edges denote procedure returns (abbr. ret.), and the dashed line represents the behavior of external procedure
q
.
The shaded areas show that for
\llbracket p_{1}\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}
, the behavior of every procedure call of
p_{1}
is interpreted by
\chi
, while for
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}
, only the behavior of external calls are interpreted by
\chi
.
This figure compares the terminating semantics of a single procedure with that of an open module containing that procedure. Subfigure (a) shows the semantics of procedure \(p_1\): starting from \(p_1\), all procedure calls made during its execution, including recursive calls to \(p_1\) itself and calls to other procedures such as the external procedure \(q\), are interpreted by the semantic oracle \(\chi\). Subfigure (b) shows the semantics of a module \(M\) containing \(p_1\): calls between procedures defined inside the module are resolved internally by the module semantics, while only calls to procedures outside the module, represented by the dashed edge to \(q\), are interpreted by \(\chi\).

(7)

\displaystyle\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}

\displaystyle\triangleq\mu\chi_{0}.\left(\llbracket p_{1}\rrbracket_{\chi_{0}\cup\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\cup\cdots\cup\llbracket p_{n}\rrbracket_{\chi_{0}\cup\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\right),\text{ where }M=p_{1};\dots;p_{n}

It is trivial to see that the standard whole-program denotational semantics given in Formula (
6
) is a special case of Formula (
7
). In Formula (
7
), the semantic oracle
\chi_{0}
(
\subseteq\text{\footnotesize ident}\times\text{\footnotesize gstate}\times\text{\footnotesize gstate}
) represents the terminating behavior of internal procedures, and the Kleene least fixed-point operator is used to capture arbitrary finite layers of internal calls.
It is worth noting that although both the terminating behavior of a single procedure and that of an open module are subsets of
\text{\footnotesize ident}\times\text{\footnotesize gstate}\times\text{\footnotesize gstate}
, they depend on different semantic oracles.
As shown in Fig.
6
, the semantics of a procedure depends on the semantics of all invoked procedures, even including recursive calls to itself. In comparison, the semantics of an open module depends on the semantics of external procedures only.

4.2.2.
Semantic Linking

Semantic linking means composing the behavior of individual modules as a whole so that cross-module calls between module
M_{1}
and module
M_{2}
before linking become “internal” calls after linking.
That is, after semantic linking, only external calls outside module
M_{1}
and module
M_{2}
need to be interpreted by a semantic oracle.
Therefore, the definition of semantic linking is straightforward:
assume that the terminating behavior of procedures outside module
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are given by an oracle
\chi
, the terminating behavior of semantic linking is defined as:

(8)

\displaystyle(\llbracket M_{1}\rrbracket\oplus\llbracket M_{2}\rrbracket)_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\triangleq\mu\chi_{0}.\left(\llbracket M_{1}\rrbracket_{\chi_{0}\cup\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\cup\llbracket M_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi_{0}\cup\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\right)

As we can see, similar to the semantic definition of modules, the semantic linking between two modules is defined by merging their denotations as if all the procedures reside in one module and then taking the fixed point.
Based on these definitions, the equivalence between
semantic and syntactic linking is shown as follows, which is reduced to Bekić’s theorem
(
Bekić, 1984
)
.
Therefore, proving horizontal compositionality in our framework is straightforward.

Theorem 4.1 (Equivalence between semantic and syntactic linking).

For any modules
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
,

\llbracket M_{1}\rrbracket\oplus\llbracket M_{2}\rrbracket=\llbracket M_{1}+M_{2}\rrbracket
, where “
+
” is syntactic linking, physically putting modules together.

Theorem 4.2 (Bekić’s theorem).

Given CPOs
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
, for any monotonic and continuous functions
f:A_{1}\times A_{2}\rightarrow A_{1}
and
g:A_{1}\times A_{2}\rightarrow A_{2}
,

\mu(x,y).\big(\mu x_{0}.f(x_{0},y),\mu y_{0}.g(x,y_{0})\big)=\mu(x,y).\big(f(x,y),g(x,y)\big)

Note that Thm.
4.1
is not specific to PCALL. It generally holds for settings where the semantics of open modules and their semantic linking are defined in the form of Formulae (
7
) and (
8
).

4.3.
Diverging Behavior of PCALL

The diverging behavior of a procedure can be induced either by its internal statements or by recursive procedure calls.
We define the diverging behavior of a PCALL program as follows: when defining statement denotation
\llbracket c\rrbracket_{\chi}
and procedure denotation
\llbracket p\rrbracket_{\chi}
,
we only consider internal divergence (caused by dead loops) first. Those caused by procedure calls (including dead loops in callee procedures and infinite layers of nested calls) are captured at the moment of defining module denotation
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\chi}
. For this purpose, we further add the following set
cll
to
CDenote
to record the point of procedure calls, so that an element
(\sigma_{0},(id,g))\in\llbracket c\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize cll})}
if and only if executing statement
c
from state
\sigma_{0}
could reach a point of procedure call named
id
at global state
g
.

⬇

cll
:

state

\rightarrow\;

query

\rightarrow\;

Prop
,

where
query

\triangleq

ident

\times

gstate
.

The calling behavior

\llbracket c\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize cll})}
is also recursively defined on the syntax of statements, for example,

\displaystyle\llbracket c_{1};c_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize cll})}\triangleq\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize cll})}\cup\big(\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\circ\llbracket c_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize cll})}\big)

\displaystyle\llbracket\textbf{call }id\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize cll})}\triangleq\big\{\big((l,g),(id,g)\big)\mid(l,g)\in\text{\footnotesize state}\big\}

Correspondingly, the calling behavior of a procedure
\llbracket p\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize cll})}
is derived from that of its body, i.e.,

\displaystyle\llbracket p\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize cll})}\triangleq\big\{\big((id_{p},g_{0}),(id,g_{2})\big)\mid\exists l_{1}\ g_{1},(l_{1},g_{1})=\text{\footnotesize Init}(p,g_{0})\wedge\big((l_{1},g_{1}),(id,g_{2})\big)\in\llbracket c_{p}\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize cll})}\big\}

4.3.1.
Diverging Behavior of Open Modules

We use
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize dvg})}
(
\subseteq\text{\footnotesize ident}\times\text{\footnotesize gstate}
) to denote the diverging behavior of open modules. Specifically,
(id,g)\in\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize dvg})}
if and only if there exists a procedure named
id
in module
M
and its execution beginning with global state
g
could diverge due to internal statements after a finite number of procedure calls, or an infinite number of internal procedure calls inside module
M
.
Thus, assume that the terminating behavior of external procedures is given by semantic oracle
\chi
, and let
\hat{\chi}=\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\cup\chi
(i.e.,
\hat{\chi}
specifies the terminating behavior of all procedures, including both internals and externals),
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize dvg})}
can be defined as:

\displaystyle\llparenthesis M\rrparenthesis_{\hat{\chi}}\triangleq\llbracket p_{1}\rrbracket_{\hat{\chi}}\cup\cdots\cup\llbracket p_{n}\rrbracket_{\hat{\chi}}\text{, where }M=p_{1};\dots;p_{n}

\displaystyle\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize dvg})}\triangleq\big(\llparenthesis M\rrparenthesis_{\hat{\chi}}.\text{({\footnotesize cll})}^{*}\circ\llparenthesis M\rrparenthesis_{\hat{\chi}}.\text{({\footnotesize dvg})}\big)\cup\llparenthesis M\rrparenthesis_{\hat{\chi}}.\text{({\footnotesize cll})}^{\infty}

where
\llparenthesis M\rrparenthesis_{\hat{\chi}}
is defined by simply merging corresponding behavior sets of procedures
p_{1},\dots,p_{n}
.

We further use
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize cll})}
(
\subseteq\text{\footnotesize query}\times\text{\footnotesize query}
) to denote the calling behavior of a module such that an internal procedure of module
M
could eventually call an external procedure after a finite number of internal calls. Let
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize dom})}
(
\subseteq\text{\footnotesize ident}
) be the set of procedure names defined in
M
, and then

\displaystyle\text{\small ExtnlQuery}(X)\triangleq\Set{(id,g)\mid id\not\in X},\text{ for }X\subseteq\text{\footnotesize ident}

\displaystyle\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize cll})}\triangleq\llparenthesis M\rrparenthesis_{\hat{\chi}}.\text{({\footnotesize cll})}^{+}\circ\textbf{test}\bigl(\text{\small ExtnlQuery}(\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize dom})})\bigr)

4.3.2.
Diverging Behavior of Semantic Linking

Based on the definitions above, semantic linking can be easily extended to the
dvg
field and
cll
field:
assume that the terminating behavior of external procedures outside
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
is given by semantic oracle
\chi
, and let
\hat{\chi}=(\llbracket M_{1}\rrbracket\oplus\llbracket M_{2}\rrbracket)_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\cup\chi
,

(\llbracket M_{1}\rrbracket\oplus\llbracket M_{2}\rrbracket)_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize dvg})}
can be defined as follows.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} (\llbracket M_{1}\rrbracket\oplus\llbracket M_{2}\rrbracket)_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize dvg})}\triangleq\ &\big(\llbracket M_{1}\rrbracket_{\hat{\chi}}.\text{({\footnotesize cll})}\cup\llbracket M_{2}\rrbracket_{\hat{\chi}}.\text{({\footnotesize cll})}\big)^{*}\circ\big(\llbracket M_{1}\rrbracket_{\hat{\chi}}.\text{({\footnotesize dvg})}\cup\llbracket M_{2}\rrbracket_{\hat{\chi}}.\text{({\footnotesize dvg})}\big)\ \cup\\
&\big(\llbracket M_{1}\rrbracket_{\hat{\chi}}.\text{({\footnotesize cll})}\cup\llbracket M_{2}\rrbracket_{\hat{\chi}}.\text{({\footnotesize cll})}\big)^{\infty}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} (\llbracket M_{1}\rrbracket\oplus\llbracket M_{2}\rrbracket)_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize cll})}\triangleq\big(\llbracket M_{1}\rrbracket_{\hat{\chi}}.\text{({\footnotesize cll})}\cup\llbracket M_{2}\rrbracket_{\hat{\chi}}.\text{({\footnotesize cll})}\big)^{+}\circ\textbf{test}\bigl(\text{\small ExtnlQuery}(\llbracket M_{1}+M_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize dom})})\bigr)\end{aligned}

Remark

There may exist traditional approaches
(
Back, 1983
)
that, for example, assume another semantic oracle
\chi^{\prime}(\subseteq

ident

\times

gstate
) to interpret the diverging behavior of procedure calls, so that the diverging behavior of a module can be defined by taking appropriate fixed points of recursive equations similar to Formula (
7
). However, this approach is not available for practical C modules, since one will meet the same stuttering problem as we do in §
3.3
when observable events are taken into account. Our approach successfully solves this problem. We can similarly define
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize fin\_dvg})}
and
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize inf\_dvg})}
for realistic C modules like how we handle Clight loops.
The key point is to use
\triangle\llparenthesis M\rrparenthesis_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize cll})}
and
\blacktriangle\llparenthesis M\rrparenthesis_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize cll})}
in the definition so that we can distinguish the cases that zero events happen before a call and the cases that at least one event happens before the call. We illustrate this in §
6.4
,
where the semantics of function calls and open modules in practical languages are defined similarly to the semantics of PCALL procedures and PCALL modules shown in this section, and the primary differences lie in the additional manipulation w.r.t. arguments and return values.

4.4.
Semantics of Unstructured Branches

We further consider unstructured branches on top of PCALL with the following GOTO language.

c\triangleq\textbf{skip}\ |\ atom\ |\ c_{1};c_{2}\ |\ \textbf{choice}(c_{1},c_{2})\ |\ \textbf{if}\ b\ \textbf{then}\ c_{1}\ \textbf{else}\ c_{2}\ |\ \textbf{while}\ b\ \textbf{do}\ c\ |\ \textbf{call}\ id\ |\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}j:c}\ |\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textbf{goto}\ j}

where
j
ranges over the set of labels. For handling
goto
statements, we add the following
gto
set to
CDenote
, so that an element
(\sigma_{0},(j,\sigma_{1}))\in\llbracket c\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize gto})}
if and only if executing statement
c
from state
\sigma_{0}
could reach a point of jumping by
goto
statement at state
\sigma_{1}
for the first time.

⬇

gto
:

state

\rightarrow\;

trace

\rightarrow\;

goto_info

\rightarrow\;

Prop
,

where
goto_info

\triangleq

label

\times

state
,

and
label

is the set of labels
.

We additionally define the behavior of
goto
statements as follows, where the normal execution of a program will be interrupted by a
goto
statement, so
\llbracket\textbf{goto }j\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}
is assigned the empty set.

\displaystyle\llbracket\textbf{goto }j\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\triangleq\emptyset\quad\quad\llbracket\textbf{goto }j\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize gto})}\triangleq\{(\sigma,(j,\sigma))\mid\sigma\in\text{\footnotesize state}\}

Existing semantic definition of other statements is the same as PCALL. The “goto” exiting behavior of other statements is recursively defined similarly to the calling behavior in §
4.3
, for example,

\displaystyle\llbracket c_{1};c_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize gto})}\triangleq\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize gto})}\cup\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\circ\llbracket c_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize gto})}

We then use the following auxiliary relations to denote the terminating, “goto” exiting, and diverging behavior of executing
c
from given labels, respectively.

\displaystyle\llfloor c\rrfloor_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\subseteq\text{\footnotesize goto\_info}\times\text{\footnotesize state}

\displaystyle\llfloor c\rrfloor_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize gto})}\subseteq\text{\footnotesize goto\_info}\times\text{\footnotesize goto\_info}

\displaystyle\llfloor c\rrfloor_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize dvg})}\subseteq\text{\footnotesize goto\_info}

They are defined by evaluating part of statement
c
from a given label, such that, for example, an element (
(j,\sigma_{0}),\sigma_{1})\in\llfloor c\rrfloor_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}
if and only if we can find a labeled position “
j:...
” in
c
and executing
c
from
j
at state
\sigma_{0}
could terminate at
\sigma_{1}
.
For instance, given statements
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
,

\llfloor c_{1};j_{1}:c_{2}\rrfloor_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\triangleq\{((j_{1},\sigma_{0}),\sigma_{1})\mid(\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1})\in\llbracket c_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\}

Finally, the behavior of a procedure
p
whose body
c
supports
goto
constructs is extended as follows.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \llbracket p\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\triangleq\Set{(id_{p},g_{0},g_{3})}{\exists l_{1}\ g_{1}\ l_{2}\ g_{2},\ \begin{aligned} &\!\big((l_{1},g_{1}),(l_{2},g_{2})\big)\in{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\llbracket c\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\cup\big(\llbracket c\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize gto})}\circ\mathbb{N}\big)}\\
&\wedge(l_{1},g_{1})=\text{\footnotesize Init}(p,g_{0})\wedge g_{3}=\text{\footnotesize Free}(l_{2},g_{2})\end{aligned}}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \llbracket p\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize dvg})}\triangleq\Set{(id_{p},g_{0})}{\exists l_{1}\ g_{1},(l_{1},g_{1})=\text{\footnotesize Init}(p,g_{0})\wedge\begin{aligned} \!(l_{1},g_{1})\in{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\llbracket c\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize dvg})}\cup\big(\llbracket c\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize gto})}\circ\mathbb{F}\big)}\end{aligned}}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \quad\text{ where }\mathbb{N}&=\llfloor c\rrfloor_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize gto})}^{*}\circ\llfloor c\rrfloor_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\quad\quad\mathbb{F}=\big(\llfloor c\rrfloor_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize gto})}^{*}\circ\llfloor c\rrfloor_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize dvg})}\big)\cup(\llfloor c\rrfloor_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize gto})})^{\infty}\end{aligned}

Here,
\mathbb{N}
denotes that executing
c
from a given label could terminate normally after a finite number of “goto” iterations, and
\mathbb{F}
denotes that executing
c
from a given label could diverge, either by a certain sub-statement that could diverge after a finite number of “goto” iterations or by an infinite number of “goto” iterations.
The procedure would then terminate if its body
c
normally terminate, or the body meets a
goto
statement (i.e.,
goto

j
) and then execution from the given label
j
would finally terminate. The diverging behaviors are handled similarly, with additional consideration of infinite iterations by
goto
. Intuitively, unstructured branchings resembles a loop with multiple entry points, where each labeled statement acts as an entry, and each
goto
statement specifies which entry to jump to.
Finally,
the way of defining the semantics of open modules and their semantic linking is equally applicable to programs with
goto
constructs, as further illustrated in §
6
.

5.
Denotational Semantics of Control Flow Graphs

Most compiler back-ends, including those of LLVM, GCC, and CompCert, are based on Control Flow Graphs (CFGs). We use the following CFG language to illustrate how the semantic linking shown in §
4
can be applied to these settings. We here do not consider observable events for simplicity.

Instructions

\displaystyle I\triangleq\mathbf{Do}(atom,j)\ |\ \mathbf{Call}(i,j)\ |\ \mathbf{Cond}(b,j_{1},j_{2})

Control flow graph

\displaystyle G\triangleq j\mapsto I

Procedures

\displaystyle p\triangleq(i_{p},j_{\text{\footnotesize entry}},j_{\text{\footnotesize exit}},G)

Modules

\displaystyle M\triangleq p_{1};...;p_{n}

A CFG is formulated as a finite mapping from labels to instructions.
Each instruction carries the labels of its possible successors. Specifically, the execution of
\mathbf{Do}(atom,j)
involves executing the atomic instruction
atom
(e.g., an assignment) and then branching to node
j
. The instruction
\mathbf{Call}(i,j)
means invoking procedure
i
and then branching to node
j
. Finally, the instruction
\textbf{Cond}(b,j_{1},j_{2})
denotes a conditional branch determined by the truth value of the pure condition
b
, which is assumed to have no side effects.
We model the nodes of CFGs with instructions rather than basic blocks since this simplifies semantics and reasoning over static analyses
(
Knoop et al., 1998
)
.

The set of program states is defined as
state

\triangleq\text{\footnotesize label}\times\text{\footnotesize lstate}\times\text{\footnotesize gstate}
.
We again use a semantic oracle
\chi
(
\subseteq\text{\footnotesize ident}\times\text{\footnotesize gstate}\times\text{\footnotesize gstate}
) to interpret the semantics of callee procedures, so as to apply the method presented in §
4
to define the semantics of open modules and semantic linking in this context. The denotational semantics of typical instructions can then be defined as follows.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathbf{jump}(j)\triangleq\{\big((j_{\text{\footnotesize pc}},l,g),(j^{\prime}_{\text{\footnotesize pc}},l,g)\big)\mid j^{\prime}_{\text{\footnotesize pc}}=j\},\text{ where }j_{\text{\footnotesize pc}}\text{ is the label of the current node.}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \llbracket\mathbf{Do}(atom,j)\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\triangleq\llbracket atom\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\circ\mathbf{jump}(j)\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \llbracket\mathbf{Call}(i,j)\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\triangleq\{\big((j_{\text{\footnotesize pc}},l,g),(j_{\text{\footnotesize pc}},l,g^{\prime})\big)\mid(i,g,g^{\prime})\in\chi\}\circ\mathbf{jump}(j)\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \llbracket\mathbf{Cond}(b,j_{1},j_{2})\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\triangleq\mathbf{test}(\llbracket b\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize tts})})\circ\mathbf{jump}(j_{1})\cup\mathbf{test}(\llbracket b\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize ffs})})\circ\mathbf{jump}(j_{2})\end{aligned}

We next use
\llbracket G\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}
to denote the general union of denotations of all nodes. Because a procedure could normally terminate if and only if executing instructions from the entry node could finally branch to the exit node after a finite number of branches between nodes,
the terminating behavior of procedures and open modules (i.e.,
\llbracket p\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}
and
\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}
) can then be defined as follows.

\displaystyle\llbracket G\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\triangleq{\textstyle\bigcup_{j}}\big(\llbracket G(j)\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\big)

\displaystyle\llbracket p\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\triangleq\{(i_{p},g,g^{\prime})\mid\exists l^{\prime},\big((j_{\text{\footnotesize entry}},l_{\text{\footnotesize empty}},g),(j_{\text{\footnotesize exit}},l^{\prime},g^{\prime})\big)\in{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\llbracket G\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}^{*}}\}

\displaystyle\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\triangleq\mu\chi_{0}.\left(\llbracket p_{1}\rrbracket_{\chi_{0}\cup\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\cup\cdots\cup\llbracket p_{n}\rrbracket_{\chi_{0}\cup\chi}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\right),\text{ where }M=p_{1};\dots;p_{n}

The semantics of open modules is defined the same way as that in §
4.2
, so is the semantic linking. In essence, the denotational semantics of control-flow graphs (CFGs) does not fundamentally differ from their small-step semantics, except for the treatment of procedure calls. The purpose of presenting the semantics of CFGs in this section is to illustrate that this semantic construction naturally scales to the CompCert back-end languages. Accordingly, the semantic linking operator defined in §
4.2
can also be applied to the CompCert back-end, in particular to its assembly languages.

6.
Semantics of CompCert Front-end Languages

6.1.
Languages and Semantic Domain

Syntax of Clight.

We write
s
for the syntax of Clight statements,
F
for Clight functions,
e
for Clight expressions, and
x,y,z
for program variables.
We recall the key definitions as follows, where
?term
denotes the optional occurrence of a syntactic term, and
\overrightarrow{term_{k}}
is a list of syntactic terms with the representative element
term_{k}
. The full syntax of Clight can be found in
(
Blazy et al., 2006
)
.

(9)

(Statements)

\displaystyle s\triangleq\ \begin{aligned} &\textbf{skip}\mid s_{1};s_{2}\mid\textbf{ if}(e)\ s_{1}\textbf{ else }s_{2}\mid\textbf{loop }s_{1}\ s_{2}\mid\textbf{call }?x\ e_{1}\ \overrightarrow{e_{2}}\mid x=e\mid\\
&\textbf{break}\mid\textbf{continue}\mid\textbf{return}\ ?e\mid\textbf{goto}\ j\mid j:\ s\mid\textbf{switch}\ e\ \overrightarrow{(?n_{k},s_{k})}\\
&\text{where }\,?term\triangleq\;?\mid term\qquad\overrightarrow{(?n_{k},s_{k})}\triangleq\text{\footnotesize LSnil}\mid(n_{1},s_{1});\overrightarrow{(?n_{2},s_{2})}\end{aligned}

(10)

(Functions)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} F\triangleq(\overrightarrow{(x_{k},type_{k})},\overrightarrow{(y_{k},type_{k})},\overrightarrow{(z_{k},type_{k})},s_{F},return\_type_{F})\end{aligned}

A Clight function is defined as a quintuple consisting, from left to right, of
the parameter list, the list of local variables, the list of temporary variables,
the function body, and the return type of
F
.

CompCert memory model

CompCert models memories as a collection of disjoint blocks,
each identified by a block identifier (ranged over by
b
of a countable set
\mathbb{B}
). Within a block, memory contents
are addressed by an integer offset (ranged over by
o
of the integer set
\mathbb{Z}
), and operations (e.g., load and
store) are defined in terms of these block-offset pairs (i.e., memory locations).
A memory state (of set
mem
) can be simply viewed as a mapping from memory locations to program values
9
9

9

We refer to
Leroy et al. (2012)
for the detailed definition of the CompCert memory model.
(of set
val
) that range over 32-bit integers, 64-bit floating-point numbers, pointers, and a special value
Vundef
.
This memory model is employed for all intermediate languages of CompCert.

Table 1
.
Program states and the query–reply interface for function calls.

Component

Formal definition

Representative element

Program states

{\text{\footnotesize state}\color[rgb]{1,1,1}{\text{\footnotesize c}}}\triangleq\text{\footnotesize tenv}\times\text{\footnotesize mem}

\quad(te,m)\in\text{\footnotesize state}

C queries

\text{\footnotesize cquery}\triangleq\text{\footnotesize ident}\times\text{\footnotesize list val}\times\text{\footnotesize mem}

\,(id,\vec{v},m)\in\text{\footnotesize cquery}

C replies

\text{\footnotesize creply}\triangleq\text{\footnotesize mem}\times\text{\footnotesize val}

\,\,\,\,(m^{\prime},v^{\prime})\in\text{\footnotesize creply}

Clight semantic infrastructures

As shown in Table
1
, a Clight program state is defined as a pair consisting of a temporary environment
te
and a memory state
m
, where a temporary environment is a mapping from variables to its values. A C function call provides a query consisting of the invoked function identifier
id
, the argument list
\vec{v}
, and the memory state
m
. After the function call, the corresponding reply consisting of a memory state
m^{\prime}
and a return value
v^{\prime}
may be received.

Clight statement denotations.

A Clight statement denotation is defined by the following signature.

CompCert classifies program behaviors into four categories: normal termination, abortion, silent divergence, and reacting divergence.
They are captured in our denotation by the sets
nrm
,
err
,
fin_dvg
, and
inf_dvg
, respectively.
Additionally, the sets
brk
,
ctn
, and
rtn
are introduced to account for Clight’s structured control flow, corresponding to the
break
,
continue
, and
return
statements.
Furthermore, the set
cll
is used to conveniently capture abortion and divergence arising from recursive calls, while the set
gto
is designed to capture possible unstructured branching by
goto
.

Function/Open module denotations

Functions or open modules communicate with each other by language interfaces
(
Koenig and Shao, 2021
;
Zhang et al., 2024
)
. Formally, a
language interface
is a tuple
\langle\text{\footnotesize query},\text{\footnotesize reply}\rangle
, where
query
is a set of queries and
reply
is a set of replies. The signature of function/open module denotations is parameterized by a language interface, as defined below.

⬇

\rightarrow\;

\subseteq

----------------------------

\rightarrow\;

(*

\times

\times

-----------

*)

:

\rightarrow\;

\rightarrow\;

;

(*

\subseteq

\times

--------------------
*)

:

\rightarrow\;

\rightarrow\;

\rightarrow\;

;

(*

\times

\times

\rightarrow\;

(*

\times

:

\rightarrow\;

\rightarrow\;

(*

\times

---------------
