7.
Behavior Refinement

Compilation correctness is ubiquitously formulated as a behavior refinement relation, which ensures that every behavior exhibited by the target program is one possible behavior of the source. As previously discussed, we define the semantics of program statements, procedures, and open modules through a series of behavior sets, using extended KATs and suitable fixed-point theorems. The highlight of our theoretical contribution is proposing a novel algebraic structure to unify various behavior refinements between different behavior sets.
In this section, we begin with typical examples of behavior refinement in §
7.1
, and then propose the novel refinement algebra in §
7.2
\sim
§
7.4
. We define Kripke relations for Cminorgen in §
7.5
, and finally explain our design choices in §
7.6

7.1.
Examples of Behavior Refinement

Example 7.1 (Behavior refinement for a simple transformation).

Consider a simple transformation that converts a WHILE program
c
into another WHILE program
\mathcal{T}(c)
with sequenced
skip
statements removed. Because how states are represented before and after the transformation is unchanged, we can use the set inclusion relation to define the behavior refinement between the programs before and after transformation, i.e.,

(30)

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c)\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\llbracket c\rrbracket\triangleq\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c)\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm})\subseteq\llbracket c\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm})\text{ and }\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c)\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize dvg})\subseteq\llbracket c\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize dvg})

For the transformation of type-safe and nondeterministic WHILE programs, the behavior refinement implies that compiler correctness allows the compiled program to exhibit fewer normal termination possibilities and fewer divergence possibilities. In other words, if the target program terminates normally, the source program must also be able to terminate normally; if the target program diverges, the source program must have a behavior that can diverge. The transformation correctness is then compositional in terms of sequential statements, in the sense that, for any
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
,

(31)

\displaystyle\text{if }\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c_{1})\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket\text{ and }\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c_{2})\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\llbracket c_{2}\rrbracket\text{, then }\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c_{1});\mathcal{T}(c_{2})\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\llbracket c_{1};c_{2}\rrbracket.

Its proof follows from the monotonicity of the composition and set union w.r.t. the set inclusion:

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c_{1});\mathcal{T}(c_{2})\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c_{1})\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\circ\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c_{2})\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\subseteq\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\circ\llbracket c_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}=\llbracket c_{1};c_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c_{1});\mathcal{T}(c_{2})\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize dvg})}

\displaystyle=\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c_{1})\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize dvg})}\cup\big(\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c_{1})\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\circ\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c_{2})\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize dvg})}\big)

∎

\displaystyle\subseteq\;\!\!\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize dvg})}\cup\big(\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\circ\llbracket c_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize dvg})}\big)=\llbracket c_{1};c_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize dvg})}

The transformation correctness of
while
loops is compositional as well, in the sense that, for any
c
,

(32)

\displaystyle\text{if }\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c)\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\llbracket c\rrbracket,\text{ then }\llbracket\textbf{while}\ b\ \textbf{do}\ \mathcal{T}(c)\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\llbracket\textbf{while}\ b\ \textbf{do}\ c\rrbracket

Its proof similarly follows from the monotonicity of operators
\circ
,
\cup
,
∗
, and
∞
w.r.t. the set inclusion.

\displaystyle\llbracket\textbf{while}\ b\ \textbf{do}\ \mathcal{T}(c)\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}

\displaystyle=\big(\textbf{test}(\llbracket b\rrbracket.\text{\footnotesize(tts)})\circ\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c)\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm})\big)^{*}\circ\textbf{test}(\llbracket b\rrbracket.\text{\footnotesize(ffs)})

\displaystyle\subseteq\big(\textbf{test}(\llbracket b\rrbracket.\text{\footnotesize(tts)})\circ\llbracket c\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm})\big)^{*}\circ\textbf{test}(\llbracket b\rrbracket.\text{\footnotesize(ffs)})

\displaystyle=\llbracket\textbf{while}\ b\ \textbf{do}\ c\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}

\displaystyle\llbracket\textbf{while}\ b\ \textbf{do}\ \mathcal{T}(c)\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize dvg})}

\displaystyle=\bigl(\textbf{test}(\llbracket b\rrbracket.\text{\footnotesize(tts)})\circ\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c)\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm})\bigr)^{*}\circ\bigl(\textbf{test}(\llbracket b\rrbracket.\text{\footnotesize(tts)})\circ\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c)\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize dvg})\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad\;\cup\bigl(\textbf{test}(\llbracket b\rrbracket.\text{\footnotesize(tts)})\circ\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c)\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm})\bigr)^{\infty}

\displaystyle\subseteq\bigl(\textbf{test}(\llbracket b\rrbracket.\text{\footnotesize(tts)})\circ\llbracket c\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm})\bigr)^{*}\circ\bigl(\textbf{test}(\llbracket b\rrbracket.\text{\footnotesize(tts)})\circ\llbracket c\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize dvg})\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad\;\cup\bigl(\textbf{test}(\llbracket b\rrbracket.\text{\footnotesize(tts)})\circ\llbracket c\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm})\bigr)^{\infty}

∎

\displaystyle=\llbracket\textbf{while}\ b\ \textbf{do}\ c\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize dvg})}

Example 7.2.

To take abortion into account, we extend the WHILE language to WHILEE, which is identical to WHILE except that it is not type-safe. In other words, WHILEE programs may abort (e.g., division by zero). We then use the following signature to capture WHILEE’s program behavior:

⬇

Record

EDenote
:

Type

:=

{

nrm
:

state

\rightarrow\;

state

\rightarrow\;

Prop
;

(*

nrm

\subseteq

state

\times

state

*)

err
:

state

\rightarrow\;

Prop
;

(*

err

\subseteq

state

*)

dvg
:

state

\rightarrow\;

Prop

(*

dvg

\subseteq

state

*)

}.

To allow for more flexible optimization, modern compilers usually do not guarantee behavioral consistency for programs with undefined behavior. That is, if the source program could abort, the compiled program may either terminate normally, diverge, or abort as well. In such cases, behavior refinement
\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c)\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\llbracket c\rrbracket
(still consider the simple transformation in Example
7.1
) can be redefined as follows, where
\llbracket c\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}
(
\subseteq\text{\footnotesize state}
) denotes potential aborting behaviors of program
c
.

(33)

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c)\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\llbracket c\rrbracket\triangleq\left\{\,\begin{aligned} &\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c)\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\subseteq\llbracket c\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\cup(\llbracket c\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\times\text{\footnotesize state})\text{ and }\\
&\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c)\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize dvg})}\subseteq\llbracket c\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize dvg})}\cup\llbracket c\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\text{ and }\\
&\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c)\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\subseteq\llbracket c\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\end{aligned}\right.

The transformation correctness of sequential statements like Formula (
31
) can be proved as follows.

(34)

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c_{1});\mathcal{T}(c_{2})\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}=\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c_{1})\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\circ\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c_{2})\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}

(35)

\displaystyle\qquad\subseteq\big(\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\cup(\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\times\text{\footnotesize state})\big)\circ\big(\llbracket c_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\cup(\llbracket c_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\times\text{\footnotesize state})\big)

(36)

\displaystyle\qquad\subseteq\big(\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\circ\llbracket c_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\big)\cup(\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\times\text{\footnotesize state})\cup\big(\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\circ(\llbracket c_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\times\text{\footnotesize state})\big)

(37)

\displaystyle\qquad\subseteq\big(\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\circ\llbracket c_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\big)\cup\Big(\big(\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\cup(\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\circ\llbracket c_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})})\big)\times\text{\footnotesize state}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad=\llbracket c_{1};c_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\cup(\llbracket c_{1};c_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\times\text{\footnotesize state})

(38)

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c_{1});\mathcal{T}(c_{2})\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize dvg})}=\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c_{1})\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize dvg})}\cup\bigl(\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c_{1})\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\circ\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c_{2})\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize dvg})}\bigr)

(39)

\displaystyle\qquad\subseteq\big(\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize dvg})}\cup\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\big)\cup\big(\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\cup(\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\times\text{\footnotesize state})\big)\circ\big(\llbracket c_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize dvg})}\cup\llbracket c_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\big)

(40)

\displaystyle\qquad\subseteq\bigl(\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize dvg})}\cup\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\circ\llbracket c_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize dvg})}\bigr)\cup\big(\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\cup(\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\circ\llbracket c_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})})\big)

\displaystyle\qquad=\llbracket c_{1};c_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize dvg})}\cup\llbracket c_{1};c_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}

(41)

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c_{1});\mathcal{T}(c_{2})\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}=\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c_{1})\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\cup\bigl(\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c_{1})\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\circ\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c_{2})\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\bigr)

(42)

\displaystyle\qquad\subseteq\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\cup\big(\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\cup(\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\times\text{\footnotesize state})\big)\circ\llbracket c_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}

∎

\displaystyle\qquad\subseteq\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\cup\bigl(\llbracket c_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\circ\llbracket c_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\bigr)=\llbracket c_{1};c_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}

Summary of existing proof system.

In the preceding examples, we showed by hand how relational Kleene algebra can be used to verify the correctness of simple program transformations. This style of reasoning is supported by a well-developed proof system: the equational theory of Kleene algebra with tests (KAT). Beyond such manual reasoning, a rich body of research has developed automated proof techniques for regular expressions, relation algebra, and KAT. Classic approaches build on the notion of derivatives
(
Brzozowski, 1964
)
, which enable the construction of automata directly from algebraic expressions. This idea has been extended to mechanized proofs of regular expression equivalence in Isabelle/HOL
(
Krauss and Nipkow, 2012
)
, decision procedures for (in-)equivalence of regular expressions in
Rocq

(
Moreira et al., 2012
)
,
Rocq
tools for KAT applied to while programs
(
Pous, 2013
)
, unified decision procedures for regular expression equivalence
(
Nipkow and Traytel, 2014
)
, and symbolic algorithms for language equivalence and KAT
(
Pous, 2015
)
. Together, these advances demonstrate the maturity of automated algebraic reasoning about relational (in-)equations.

Challenges for realistic compiler verification.

Despite the expressiveness of relational Kleene algebra and the maturity of its automated reasoning, directly applying these techniques to realistic compiler verification remains challenging. Existing methods focus primarily on equational reasoning over algebraic structures, whereas compiler correctness demands reasoning about simulation relations between source and target programs. In other words, the correctness of a compiler often cannot be expressed simply in terms of set inclusion between source and target denotations. Instead, one must account for changes in state representations across abstraction layers—most notably the transformation of memory models—so that correctness is proved not by inclusion alone but by carefully designed state-matching relations. We illustrate this point with the following examples.

Before showing general definitions, we first consider the compilation correctness of Cshmgen.

Example 7.3 (Behavior refinement for Cshmgen of CompCert).

Cshmgen is mainly responsible for translating structured C control flow into simpler primitives and erasing Clight types. In this process, the representation of program states remains unchanged. Suppose that a Clight statement
s
is transformed to Csharpminor by Cshmgen, we can then define the behavior refinement
\llbracket t\rrbracket\precsim\llbracket s\rrbracket
:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \forall\sigma\ \tau\ \sigma^{\prime},\ (\sigma,\tau,\sigma^{\prime})\in\llbracket t\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\Rightarrow\big((\sigma,\tau,\sigma^{\prime})\in\llbracket s\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\big)\vee\left(\exists\tau_{0},(\sigma,\tau_{0})\in\llbracket s\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\wedge\tau_{0}\leqslant_{T}\tau\right);\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \forall\sigma\ \tau,\ (\sigma,\tau)\in\llbracket t\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\Rightarrow\left(\exists\tau_{0},(\sigma,\tau_{0})\in\llbracket s\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\wedge\tau_{0}\leqslant_{T}\tau\right);\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \forall\sigma\ \tau,\ (\sigma,\tau)\in\llbracket t\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize fin\_dvg})}\Rightarrow(\sigma,\tau)\in\llbracket s\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize fin\_dvg})}\vee\left(\exists\tau_{0},(\sigma,\tau_{0})\in\llbracket s\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\wedge\tau_{0}\leqslant_{T}\tau\right);\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \forall\sigma\ \tau,\ (\sigma,\tau)\in\llbracket t\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize inf\_dvg})}\Rightarrow(\sigma,\tau)\in\llbracket s\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize inf\_dvg})}\vee\left(\exists\tau_{0},(\sigma,\tau_{0})\in\llbracket s\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\wedge\tau_{0}\leqslant_{T}\tau\right).\end{aligned}

where
\tau_{0}\leqslant_{T}\tau
means that
\tau_{0}
is the prefix of
\tau
.
This example illustrates the way how observable event traces are preserved for realistic compilation, especially when undefined behaviors (UB) are raised in the source program, which conforms to CompCert’s prefix-preserving view of UB.

We now generalize the above definition to capture the correctness of more complex phases of CompCert, where source and target program states may differ in representation and are related through a state-matching relation. The following example illustrates this generalization.

(a)

Refinement of terminating behavior

(b)

Refinement of diverging behavior

Figure 7
.
Behavior refinement of termination and divergence for CompCert, where
\text{\footnotesize xxx}\in\Set{\text{\footnotesize fin\_dvg},\text{\footnotesize inf\_dvg}}
.
This figure illustrates behavior refinement in CompCert for both normal termination and divergence. Subfigure (a) shows refinement of terminating behavior: each terminating execution of the target program must correspond either to a related terminating execution of the source program, preserving the observable trace and the state-matching relation, or to a premature abort of the source program, marked by a lightning symbol. Subfigure (b) shows refinement of diverging behavior: each diverging execution of the target program, with either finite or infinite observable trace, must correspond either to a related diverging execution of the source program or again to a premature source abort.

Example 7.4 (Behavior refinement for general phases of CompCert).

Suppose that a compilation phase of CompCert transforms a source statement
s
into the target statement
t
.
For a given state-matching relation
R
, we define their behavior refinement, denoted by
\llbracket t\rrbracket\precsim_{R}\llbracket s\rrbracket
, as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \forall\sigma_{t}\ \tau\ \sigma^{\prime}_{t}\ &\sigma_{s},\ (\sigma_{t},\tau,\sigma^{\prime}_{t})\in\llbracket t\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\Rightarrow(\sigma_{s},\sigma_{t})\in R\Rightarrow\\
&\big(\exists\sigma^{\prime}_{s},(\sigma_{s},\tau,\sigma^{\prime}_{s})\in\llbracket s\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\wedge(\sigma^{\prime}_{s},\sigma^{\prime}_{t})\in R\big)\vee\left(\exists\tau_{0},(\sigma_{s},\tau_{0})\in\llbracket s\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\wedge\tau_{0}\leqslant_{T}\tau\right);\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \forall\sigma_{t}\ \tau\ \sigma_{s},\,&(\sigma_{t},\tau)\in\llbracket t\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\Rightarrow(\sigma_{s},\sigma_{t})\in R\Rightarrow\left(\exists\tau_{0},(\sigma_{s},\tau_{0})\in\llbracket s\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\wedge\tau_{0}\leqslant_{T}\tau\right);\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \forall\sigma_{t}\ \tau\ \sigma_{s},\,&(\sigma_{t},\tau)\in\llbracket t\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize fin\_dvg})}\Rightarrow(\sigma_{s},\sigma_{t})\in R\Rightarrow\\
&(\sigma_{s},\tau)\in\llbracket s\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize fin\_dvg})}\vee\left(\exists\tau_{0},(\sigma_{s},\tau_{0})\in\llbracket s\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\wedge\tau_{0}\leqslant_{T}\tau\right);\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \forall\sigma_{t}\ \tau\ \sigma_{s},\,&(\sigma_{t},\tau)\in\llbracket t\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize inf\_dvg})}\Rightarrow(\sigma_{s},\sigma_{t})\in R\Rightarrow\\
&(\sigma_{s},\tau)\in\llbracket s\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize inf\_dvg})}\vee\left(\exists\tau_{0},(\sigma_{s},\tau_{0})\in\llbracket s\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\wedge\tau_{0}\leqslant_{T}\tau\right)\end{aligned}

Fig.
7
illustrates behavior refinement for both terminating and diverging executions. It states that for every terminating (resp. diverging) behavior of the target program, either there may exist consistent terminating (resp. diverging) behavior in the source end, or the source program may abort (denoted as
\lightning
) prematurely. Once the source program may abort, the compiled target program could do anything unpredictable.
Furthermore, though not depicted in Fig.
7
, the definition also requires that if the target program may abort, then the corresponding source program may abort as well. This rules out compilers that intend to translate a safe program into one that may aborts.
It is also worth noting that refinement between behavior sets entails refinement between the triggered observable behaviors, thereby demonstrating the same compilation correctness as in CompCert.

Besides, the correctness of each compilation phase can be proved with different state-matching relations and can be vertically composed to form end-to-end correctness of the whole compiler.
This is demonstrated by the following theorem, where
L_{1},L_{2}
and
L_{3}
are the semantics of statements.

Theorem 7.5.

For any matching relations
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
, if

L_{3}\precsim_{R_{2}}L_{2}
and
L_{2}\precsim_{R_{1}}L_{1}
, then
L_{3}\precsim_{R_{2}\circ R_{1}}L_{1}
.

However, the behavior refinement defined in Example
7.4
is inherently limited to closed settings, i.e., the verification of whole-program compilation, as is the case for the original CompCert
10
10

10

Extended by
Kang et al. (2016)
, CompCert supports verified separate compilation by the same compiler from version 2.7.
.

Toward verified compositional compilation (VCC)

Verified compositional compilation requires two dimensions of compositionality in open settings:
(i)
horizontal compositionality
requires that the compilation correctness of individual modules can be composed to obtain compilation correctness of the linked whole program;
(ii)
vertical compositionality
demands that correctness of each compilation phase can be composed to yield an end-to-end correctness across the compilation chain.

Several works stemming from CompCert have moved in this direction. Early attempts such as CompComp
(
Stewart et al., 2015
)
and CompCertM
(
Song et al., 2020
)
extend CompCert with interaction semantics
(
Beringer et al., 2014
)
and employ open simulations to prove compilation correctness of open modules. More recent developments, notably CompCertO
(
Koenig and Shao, 2021
)
and the work of
Zhang et al. (2024)
, introduce Kripke logical relations to express the correspondence between open module interactions. We will return to these developments in more detail later.

To support VCC in our denotation-based framework, we leverage the notion of Kripke relations and the Kripke interface relations (also called simulation conventions) from CompCertO. We formulate the denotation-based behavior refinement for open functions and modules as follows.

Definition 7.6 (Kripke relations).

Given sets
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
, and
W
, a
Kripke relation

R:W\rightarrow\{X\mid X\subseteq A_{1}\times A_{2}\}

is a family of relations indexed by the set of
Kripke worlds

W
,
written as
R:\mathcal{K}_{W}(A_{1},A_{2})
.

Kripke interface relations
(KIR) are used to relate the source and target language interfaces.

Definition 7.7 (Kripke interface relations, or KIR).

A Kripke interface relation between two language interfaces

\langle\text{\footnotesize query}_{1},\text{\footnotesize reply}_{1}\rangle
and
\langle\text{\footnotesize query}_{2},\text{\footnotesize reply}_{2}\rangle
is a tuple

B=\langle W_{B},\mathcal{\leadsto}_{B},{R}^{q}_{B},{R}^{r}_{B}\rangle,

where
\mathcal{\leadsto}_{B}
(
\subseteq W_{B}\times W_{B}
) is an accessibility
relation between worlds, and
{R}^{q}_{B}:\mathcal{K}_{W_{B}}(\text{\footnotesize query}_{1},\text{\footnotesize query}_{2})
and

{R}^{r}_{B}:\mathcal{K}_{W_{B}}(\text{\footnotesize reply}_{1},\text{\footnotesize reply}_{2})
are Kripke relations used for relating
queries and replies, respectively.

According to CompCertO
(
Koenig and Shao, 2021
)
, Kripke interface relations can be vertically composed as follows:
given Kripke interface relations

A=\langle W_{A},\mathcal{\leadsto}_{A},{R}^{q}_{A},{R}^{r}_{A}\rangle
and

B=\langle W_{B},\mathcal{\leadsto}_{B},{R}^{q}_{B},{R}^{r}_{B}\rangle
,

A{\bullet}B\triangleq\langle W_{A}\times W_{B},\leadsto_{AB},{R}^{q}_{A}\bullet{R}^{q}_{B},{R}^{r}_{A}\bullet{R}^{r}_{B}\rangle
,
where for any worlds
w_{A},w_{A}^{\prime}\in W_{A}
, and
w_{B},\,w_{B}^{\prime}\in W_{B}
,

\displaystyle(w_{A},w_{B})\leadsto_{AB}(w_{A}^{\prime},w_{B}^{\prime})\Leftrightarrow w_{A}\leadsto_{A}w_{A}^{\prime}\wedge w_{B}\leadsto_{B}w_{B}^{\prime};

\displaystyle{R}^{q}_{A}\bullet{R}^{q}_{B}(w_{A},w_{B})\triangleq{R}^{q}_{A}(w_{A})\circ{R}^{q}_{B}(w_{B});\text{ and }{R}^{r}_{A}\bullet{R}^{r}_{B}(w_{A},w_{B})\triangleq{R}^{r}_{A}(w_{A})\circ{R}^{r}_{B}(w_{B}).

Example 7.8 (Behavior refinement for open functions/modules of CompCert).

Suppose that a source open module
M_{s}
is transformed to a target module
M_{t}
.
We use semantic oracles
\chi_{s}
(
\subseteq\text{\footnotesize query}_{s}\times\text{\footnotesize trace}\times\text{\footnotesize reply}_{s}
) and
\chi_{t}

(\subseteq\text{\footnotesize query}_{t}\times\text{\footnotesize trace}\times\text{\footnotesize reply}_{t})
to specify the terminating behavior of invoked functions external to the source module
M_{s}
and functions external to the target module
M_{t}
respectively. Additionally, we use a semantic oracle
\chi_{e}
(
\subseteq\text{\footnotesize query}_{s}\times\text{\footnotesize trace}
) to specify the aborting behavior of functions external to the source module
M_{s}
.
Given a KIR
A=\langle W_{A},\mathcal{\leadsto}_{A},{R}^{q}_{A},{R}^{r}_{A}\rangle
between languages interfaces
\langle\text{\footnotesize query}_{s},\text{\footnotesize reply}_{s}\rangle
and
\langle\text{\footnotesize query}_{t},\text{\footnotesize reply}_{t}\rangle
, we define
\chi_{t}\precsim_{A}(\chi_{s},\chi_{e})
as follows.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \chi_{t}\precsim_{A}(\chi_{s},\chi_{e})\triangleq\forall w_{A}\ &q_{t}\ \tau\ r_{t}\ q_{s},\;(q_{t},\tau,r_{t})\in\chi_{t}\Rightarrow(q_{s},q_{t})\in{R}^{q}_{A}(w_{A})\Rightarrow\\
&\big(\exists w_{A}^{\prime}\,r_{s},(q_{s},\tau,r_{s})\in\chi_{s}\wedge w_{A}\leadsto_{A}w_{A}^{\prime}\wedge(r_{s},r_{t})\in{R}^{r}_{A}(w_{A}^{\prime})\big)\ \vee\\
&\big(\exists\tau_{0},(q_{s},\tau_{0})\in\chi_{e}\wedge\tau_{0}\leqslant_{T}\tau\big);\end{aligned}

Similarly, given a KIR
B=\langle W_{B},\mathcal{\leadsto}_{B},{R}^{q}_{B},{R}^{r}_{B}\rangle
,
we then define
\llbracket M_{t}\rrbracket_{\chi_{t}}\precsim_{B}\llbracket M_{s}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}^{\chi_{e}}
as follows, where
\llbracket M_{t}\rrbracket_{\chi_{t}}
has the denotation signature
FDenote
(defined in §
6.1
) with language interface
\langle\text{\footnotesize query}_{t},\text{\footnotesize reply}_{t}\rangle
, and
\llbracket M_{s}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}
has the denotation signature
FDenote
with language interface
\langle\text{\footnotesize query}_{s},\text{\footnotesize reply}_{s}\rangle
.

\displaystyle\llbracket M_{t}\rrbracket_{\chi_{t}}\precsim_{B}\llbracket M_{s}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}^{\chi_{e}}\triangleq\text{let }\llbracket M_{s}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}^{\chi_{e}}.(\text{\footnotesize err})\triangleq\llbracket M_{s}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\cup(\llbracket M_{s}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize cll})}\circ\chi_{e})\text{ in }

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\begin{aligned} \forall w_{B}\ q_{t}\ \tau\ r_{t}\ q_{s},\ &(q_{t},\tau,r_{t})\in\llbracket M_{t}\rrbracket_{\chi_{t}}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\Rightarrow(q_{s},q_{t})\in{R}^{q}_{B}(w_{B})\Rightarrow\\
&\big(\exists w_{B}^{\prime}\ r_{s},(q_{s},\tau,r_{s})\in\llbracket M_{s}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\wedge w_{B}\leadsto_{B}w_{B}^{\prime}\wedge(r_{s},r_{t})\in{R}^{r}_{B}(w_{B}^{\prime})\big)\ \vee\\
&\big(\exists\tau_{0},(q_{s},\tau_{0})\in\llbracket M_{s}\rrbracket_{\chi_{e}}^{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\wedge\tau_{0}\leqslant_{T}\tau\big);\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\begin{aligned} \forall w_{B}\ q_{t}\ \tau\ q_{t}^{\prime}\ q_{s},\ &(q_{t},\tau,q_{t}^{\prime})\in\llbracket M_{t}\rrbracket_{\chi_{t}}.\text{({\footnotesize cll})}\Rightarrow(q_{s},q_{t})\in{R}^{q}_{B}(w_{B})\Rightarrow\\
&\big(\exists w_{B}^{\prime}\ q_{s}^{\prime},(q_{s},\tau,q_{s}^{\prime})\in\llbracket M_{s}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize cll})}\wedge w_{B}\leadsto_{B}w_{B}^{\prime}\wedge(q_{s}^{\prime},q_{t}^{\prime})\in{R}^{q}_{B}(w_{B}^{\prime})\big)\ \vee\\
&\big(\exists\tau_{0},(q_{s},\tau_{0})\in\llbracket M_{s}\rrbracket_{\chi_{e}}^{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\wedge\tau_{0}\leqslant_{T}\tau\big);\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\begin{aligned} \forall w_{B}\ q_{t}\ \tau\ q_{s},\>\!(q_{t},\tau)\in\llbracket M_{t}\rrbracket_{\chi_{t}}.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\Rightarrow(q_{s},&\;q_{t})\in{R}^{q}_{B}(w_{B})\Rightarrow\\
&\!\!\!\big(\exists\tau_{0},(q_{s},\tau_{0})\in\llbracket M_{s}\rrbracket_{\chi_{e}}^{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\wedge\tau_{0}\leqslant_{T}\tau\big);\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\begin{aligned} \forall w_{B}\ q_{t}\ \tau\ q_{s},\ &(q_{t},\tau)\in\llbracket M_{t}\rrbracket_{\chi_{t}}.\text{({\footnotesize fin\_dvg})}\Rightarrow(q_{s},q_{t})\in{R}^{q}_{B}(w_{B})\Rightarrow\\
&(q_{s},\tau)\in\llbracket M_{s}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize fin\_dvg})}\vee\big(\exists\tau_{0},(q_{s},\tau_{0})\in\llbracket M_{s}\rrbracket_{\chi_{e}}^{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\wedge\tau_{0}\leqslant_{T}\tau\big);\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\begin{aligned} \forall w_{B}\ q_{t}\ \tau\ q_{s},\ &(q_{t},\tau)\in\llbracket M_{t}\rrbracket_{\chi_{t}}.\text{({\footnotesize inf\_dvg})}\Rightarrow(q_{s},q_{t})\in{R}^{q}_{B}(w_{B})\Rightarrow\\
&(q_{s},\tau)\in\llbracket M_{s}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize inf\_dvg})}\vee\big(\exists\tau_{0},(q_{s},\tau_{0})\in\llbracket M_{s}\rrbracket_{\chi_{e}}^{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\wedge\tau_{0}\leqslant_{T}\tau\big).\end{aligned}

Finally, for given Kripke interface relations
A
and
B
, the behavior refinement between open modules
M_{s}
and
M_{t}
, denoted by

\llbracket M_{t}\rrbracket\precsim_{A\twoheadrightarrow B}\llbracket M_{s}\rrbracket
, is defined as follows.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \llbracket M_{t}\rrbracket\precsim_{A\twoheadrightarrow B}\llbracket M_{s}\rrbracket\triangleq\forall\chi_{t}\,\chi_{s}\,\chi_{e},\;\chi_{t}\precsim_{A}(\chi_{s},\chi_{e})\Rightarrow\llbracket M_{t}\rrbracket_{\chi_{t}}\precsim_{B}\llbracket M_{s}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}^{\chi_{e}}.\end{aligned}

We illustrate the above definitions (typically for terminating behaviors) in Fig.
8
.
This formalization enables classical rely–guarantee reasoning
(
Liang et al., 2012
)
by the accessibility relation between worlds.
Specifically, the evolution of worlds in the callee’s behavior refinement
provides the rely condition for the caller’s behavior refinement.
Conversely, assuming
w_{A}\leadsto_{A}w_{A}^{\prime}
,
the evolution of worlds in the caller’s behavior refinement
must respect the guarantee condition
w_{B}\leadsto_{B}w_{B}^{\prime}
.
When each module is proved to satisfy its guarantee condition, and these guarantee conditions entail their rely conditions, their compilation correctness becomes horizontally compositional.

Theorem 7.9 (Horizontal compositionality).

For any source modules
M_{s},\,M_{s}^{\prime}
, and target modules
M_{t},\,M_{t}^{\prime}
,
if

\llbracket M_{t}\rrbracket\precsim_{A\twoheadrightarrow A}\llbracket M_{s}\rrbracket

and

\llbracket M_{t}^{\prime}\rrbracket\precsim_{A\twoheadrightarrow A}\llbracket M_{s}^{\prime}\rrbracket
, then

\llbracket M_{t}\rrbracket\oplus\llbracket M_{t}^{\prime}\rrbracket\precsim_{A\twoheadrightarrow A}\llbracket M_{s}\rrbracket\oplus\llbracket M_{s}^{\prime}\rrbracket.

(a)

Refinement of callee’s behavior

(b)

Refinement of caller’s behavior

Figure 8
.
Behavior refinement for CompCert open functions/modules.
This figure illustrates behavior refinement for open functions or modules in CompCert-style compositional verification. Subfigure (a) shows refinement of a callee’s behavior: when the target callee produces a terminating behavior under a target semantic oracle, there must be either a corresponding source callee behavior related by the reply relation, with the Kripke world advancing according to the accessibility relation, or a premature source-side error. This captures how external function behavior assumed by the target is matched by behavior available on the source side. Subfigure (b) shows refinement of a caller’s behavior: assuming related source and target call interfaces, each terminating behavior of the target caller must correspond either to a related terminating behavior of the source caller, again with suitable world evolution and related replies, or to a source-side error. Together, the two diagrams illustrate rely-guarantee reasoning for open modules, where refinement of callees provides assumptions for callers, and world evolution records the compatibility conditions needed for horizontal compositionality.

Theorem 7.10 (Vertical compositionality).

Given KIRs
A_{1},A_{2},B_{1},B_{2}
, and modules

M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}
,

\displaystyle\llbracket M_{3}\rrbracket\precsim_{A_{2}\Rightarrow B_{2}}\llbracket M_{2}\rrbracket\Rightarrow\llbracket M_{2}\rrbracket\precsim_{A_{1}\Rightarrow B_{1}}\llbracket M_{1}\rrbracket\Rightarrow\llbracket M_{3}\rrbracket\precsim_{A_{2}\bullet A_{1}\Rightarrow B_{2}\bullet B_{1}}\llbracket M_{1}\rrbracket.

Since the semantics of a function (e.g., Clight) is defined from the semantics of its function body (i.e., a statement), the correctness of function-level transformations can be derived from the correctness of statement-level transformations. We build the behavior refinement for open statements as follows, in a similar form to the behavior refinement for open modules.

Example 7.11 (Behavior refinement for open statement of CompCert).

A statement of an open module
M
may call functions external to
M
(i.e., open). Suppose that
a statement
s
in a source module
M_{s}
is compiled to a statement
t
in the compiled module
M_{t}
.
Given a Kripke relation
R_{B}:\mathcal{K}_{W_{B}}(\text{\footnotesize state}_{s},\text{\footnotesize state}_{t})
between the source and target program states, and an accessibility relation
\leadsto_{B}\;\subseteq\;\!W_{B}\times W_{B}
between worlds. The behavior refinement between
s
and
t
, denoted by
\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\chi_{t}}\precsim_{R_{B}}\llbracket s\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}^{\chi_{e}}
, is defined as follows,
where the semantic oracles
\chi_{s},\chi_{e}
and
\chi_{t}
are explained in Example
7.8
.

\displaystyle\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\chi_{t}}\precsim_{R_{B}}\llbracket s\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}^{\chi_{e}}\triangleq\text{let }\llbracket s\rrbracket_{\chi_{e}}^{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\triangleq\llbracket s\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\cup(\llbracket s\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize cll})}\circ\chi_{e})\text{ in}

\displaystyle\quad\begin{aligned} \forall w_{B}\ \sigma_{t}\ \tau\ \sigma^{\prime}_{t}\ \sigma_{s},\ &(\sigma_{t},\tau,\sigma^{\prime}_{t})\in\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\chi_{t}}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\Rightarrow(\sigma_{s},\sigma_{t})\in R_{B}(w_{B})\Rightarrow\\
&\big(\exists w_{B}^{\prime}\ \sigma^{\prime}_{s},(\sigma_{s},\tau,\sigma^{\prime}_{s})\in\llbracket s\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\wedge w_{B}\leadsto_{B}w_{B}^{\prime}\wedge(\sigma^{\prime}_{s},\sigma^{\prime}_{t})\in R_{B}(w_{B}^{\prime})\big)\ \vee\\
&\big(\exists\tau_{0},(\sigma_{s},\tau_{0})\in\llbracket s\rrbracket_{\chi_{e}}^{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\wedge\tau_{0}\leqslant_{T}\tau\big);\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\quad\begin{aligned} \forall w_{B}\ \sigma_{t}\ \tau\ \sigma_{s},\>\!(\sigma_{t},\tau)\in\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\chi_{t}}.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\Rightarrow(\sigma_{s},\sigma_{t})\in R_{B}(w_{B})\Rightarrow\big(\exists\tau_{0},(\sigma_{s},\tau_{0})\in\llbracket s\rrbracket_{\chi_{e}}^{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\wedge\tau_{0}\leqslant_{T}\tau\big);\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\quad\begin{aligned} \forall w_{B}\ \sigma_{t}\ \tau\ \sigma_{s},\ &(\sigma_{t},\tau)\in\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\chi_{t}}.\text{({\footnotesize fin\_dvg})}\Rightarrow(\sigma_{s},\sigma_{t})\in R_{B}(w_{B})\Rightarrow\\
&(\sigma_{s},\tau)\in\llbracket s\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize fin\_dvg})}\vee\big(\exists\tau_{0},(\sigma_{s},\tau_{0})\in\llbracket s\rrbracket_{\chi_{e}}^{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\wedge\tau_{0}\leqslant_{T}\tau\big);\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\quad\begin{aligned} \forall w_{B}\ \sigma_{t}\ \tau\ \sigma_{s},\ &(\sigma_{t},\tau)\in\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\chi_{t}}.\text{({\footnotesize inf\_dvg})}\Rightarrow(\sigma_{s},\sigma_{t})\in R_{B}(w_{B})\Rightarrow\\
&(\sigma_{s},\tau)\in\llbracket s\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize inf\_dvg})}\vee\big(\exists\tau_{0},(\sigma_{s},\tau_{0})\in\llbracket s\rrbracket_{\chi_{e}}^{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\wedge\tau_{0}\leqslant_{T}\tau\big).\end{aligned}

Example
7.4
is a special case of Example
7.11
if we define
R_{B}\triangleq\lambda w_{B}.R
and
\leadsto_{B}\;\triangleq W_{B}\times W_{B}
. In our
Rocq
implementation, we verify the statement transformation of the CompCert front-end with this general behavior refinement.
We show a concrete nontrivial Kripke relation for Cminorgen in §
7.5
.

Example 7.12 (Behavior refinement for typical optimizations).

Consider a standard constant propagation optimization on the PCALL language, where the variables known to have a constant value will be substituted with the constant.
At the optimization level, the algorithm first produces static analysis results, telling which variables hold constant values before and after every statement,
and then generates the target code by replacing the variable with its constant. For example:

⬇

source

code

c
:

x

=

=

x

+

1

:

[
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\pi_{0}:=\_\mapsto\bot}
]

{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\pi_{1}}
:=

x

\mapsto

At the verification level, behavior refinement is based on the analysis results. We use
\Pi
to denote the set of analysis results that map each variable to the constant value if the variable is constant, or
\bot
if not.
We use
R_{\pi}
to denote the state-matching relation between source and target program states. The relation
R_{\pi}
is parameterized by the analysis result
\pi
, which requires that
the value of a variable on the source state respects its value on the analysis result
\pi
.
Assume that the source code
c

\mathcal{T}_{\text{\footnotesize cp}}(c)
by standard constant propagation, generating analysis results
\pi_{1}
at the end of
c
with initially known result
11
11

11

All the variables are mapped to the unknown value
\bot
if we know nothing.

\pi_{0}
. We define
\llbracket\mathcal{T}_{\text{\footnotesize cp}}(c)\rrbracket\precsim_{(\pi_{0},\pi_{1})}\llbracket c\rrbracket
as follows.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \forall\sigma_{t}\ \sigma^{\prime}_{t}\ \sigma_{s},\ (\sigma_{t},\sigma^{\prime}_{t})\in&\,\llbracket\mathcal{T}_{\text{\footnotesize cp}}(c)\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\Rightarrow(\sigma_{s},\sigma_{t})\in R_{\pi_{0}}\Rightarrow\\
&\bigl(\exists\sigma^{\prime}_{s},\,\ (\sigma_{s},\sigma^{\prime}_{s})\in\llbracket c\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\wedge(\sigma^{\prime}_{s},\sigma^{\prime}_{t})\in R_{\pi_{1}}\bigr)\vee\sigma_{s}\in\llbracket c\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})};\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \forall\sigma_{t}\ \sigma_{s},\ \sigma_{t}\in\llbracket\mathcal{T}_{\text{\footnotesize cp}}(c)\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\Rightarrow(\sigma_{s},\sigma_{t})\in R_{\pi_{0}}\Rightarrow\sigma_{s}\in\llbracket c\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})};\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \forall\sigma_{t}\ \sigma_{s},\ \sigma_{t}\in\llbracket\mathcal{T}_{\text{\footnotesize cp}}(c)\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize dvg})}\Rightarrow(\sigma_{s},\sigma_{t})\in R_{\pi_{0}}\Rightarrow\sigma_{s}\in\llbracket c\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize dvg})}\ \vee\ \sigma_{s}\in\llbracket c\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})};\end{aligned}

Summary and our solution

Examples
7.4

\sim

7.12
have shown that nontrivial state-matching relations are required in realistic settings.
To extend the verification technique of the first two examples to such settings, we next propose the notion of Refinement Algebras (RAs). The key idea is to employ isomorphism functions to establish a structured correspondence between the denotational semantics of source and target programs, and then exploit the algebraic properties of semantic operators and RAs to achieve compositionality and to enable mechanized verification.

7.2.
Refinement Algebras

Our proof system is based on a notion of refinement algebras, whose definition is shown as follows.

Definition 7.13.

[Refinement algebras]
A Refinement Algebra (RA) is a tuple
(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{E},\mathcal{T},\gamma)
, where

•

\mathcal{S},\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{T}
are behavior sets. Specifically, the set
\mathcal{S}
represents one kind of safe behaviors of a source program (e.g., termination), and the set
\mathcal{T}
denotes the corresponding behavior set of the target program. The set
\mathcal{E}
denotes the aborting behavior set of the source program;

•

\gamma:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}
is a
monotonic
function transforming source behavior sets to the target:

(43)

\displaystyle\forall N_{1}\ N_{2}\in\mathcal{S},\,\forall E_{1}\ E_{2}\in\mathcal{E},\,N_{1}\subseteq N_{2}\Rightarrow E_{1}\subseteq E_{2}\Rightarrow\gamma(N_{1},E_{1})\subseteq\gamma(N_{2},E_{2})

To illustrate how RAs encode various transformation correctness discussed in §
7.1
, we begin with the following RA instances
12
12

12

For clarity of presentation, we use subscripts to distinguish gamma functions from different refinement algebra instances. In
Rocq
, the abstract refinement algebra (Def.
7.13
) is declared as a type class, and concrete refinement algebras can be instantiated for specific types. When applying an instance to define the refinement between behavior sets, Rocq’s type class resolution mechanism automatically selects the appropriate instance of gamma according to the type of its arguments.

for Example
7.2
, where
\mathcal{P}(A)
is the power set of a given set
A
.

Example 7.14 (Termination instance 1).

{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{1}\triangleq({\mathcal{S}}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{1},{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{1},{\mathcal{T}}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{1},{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{1})
, where for a given state set
state
,

{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{1}\triangleq\mathcal{P}(\text{\footnotesize state})
,

{\mathcal{S}}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{1}={\mathcal{T}}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{1}\triangleq\mathcal{P}(\text{\footnotesize state}\times\text{\footnotesize state})
.

\forall N\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{1}
,

E\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{1}
,

{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{1}(N,E)\triangleq N\cup(E\times\text{\footnotesize state}).

Example 7.15 (Divergence instance 1).

{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{\scriptsize D}}_{1}\triangleq({\mathcal{S}}^{\text{\scriptsize D}}_{1},{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{\scriptsize D}}_{1},{\mathcal{T}}^{\text{\scriptsize D}}_{1},{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize D}}_{1})
, where for a given state set
state
,

{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{\scriptsize D}}_{1}={\mathcal{S}}^{\text{\scriptsize D}}_{1}={\mathcal{T}}^{\text{\scriptsize D}}_{1}\triangleq\mathcal{P}(\text{\footnotesize state})
.

\forall N\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\text{\scriptsize D}}_{1}
,

E\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{\scriptsize D}}_{1}
,

{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize D}}_{1}(N,E)\triangleq N\cup E.

With the RA instances
{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{\scriptsize D}}_{1}
, we can encode
\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c)\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\llbracket c\rrbracket
of Example
7.2
as follows.

(44)

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c)\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\llbracket c\rrbracket\triangleq\left\{\begin{aligned} &\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c)\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm})\subseteq{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{1}(\llbracket c\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm}),\llbracket c\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize err}))\text{ and }\\
&\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c)\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize dvg})\subseteq{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize D}}_{1}(\llbracket c\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize dvg}),\llbracket c\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize err}))\text{ and }\\
&\llbracket\mathcal{T}(c)\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize err})\subseteq{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize D}}_{1}(\varnothing,\llbracket c\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize err}))\end{aligned}\right.

Example 7.16 (Termination instance 2).

{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{2}\triangleq({\mathcal{S}}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{2},{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{2},{\mathcal{T}}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{2},{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{2})
, where for a given state set
state
,

{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{2}\triangleq\mathcal{P}(\text{\footnotesize state}\times\text{\footnotesize trace})
,

{\mathcal{S}}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{2}={\mathcal{T}}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{2}\triangleq\mathcal{P}(\text{\footnotesize state}\times\text{\footnotesize trace}\times\text{\footnotesize state})
.

\forall N\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{2}
,

E\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{2}
,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} {\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{2}(N,E)\triangleq\Set{(\sigma,\tau,\sigma^{\prime})}{\big((\sigma,\tau,\sigma^{\prime})\in N\big)\vee(\exists\tau_{0},(\sigma,\tau_{0})\in E\wedge\tau_{0}\leqslant_{T}\tau)}\end{aligned}

Example 7.17 (Finite diverging instance 2).

{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{\scriptsize F}}_{2}\triangleq({\mathcal{S}}^{\text{\scriptsize F}}_{2},{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{\scriptsize F}}_{2},{\mathcal{T}}^{\text{\scriptsize F}}_{2},{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize F}}_{2})
, where for a given state set
state
,

{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{\scriptsize F}}_{2}={\mathcal{S}}^{\text{\scriptsize F}}_{2}={\mathcal{T}}^{\text{\scriptsize F}}_{2}\triangleq\mathcal{P}(\text{\footnotesize state}\times\text{\footnotesize trace})
.

\forall N\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\text{\scriptsize F}}_{2}
,

E\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{\scriptsize F}}_{2}
,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} {\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize F}}_{2}(N,E)\triangleq\Set{(\sigma,\tau)}{(\sigma,\tau)\in N\vee\left(\exists\tau_{0},(\sigma,\tau_{0})\in E\wedge\tau_{0}\leqslant_{T}\tau\right)}\end{aligned}

Example 7.18 (Infinite diverging instance 2).

{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{\scriptsize I}}_{2}\triangleq({\mathcal{S}}^{\text{\scriptsize I}}_{2},{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{\scriptsize I}}_{2},{\mathcal{T}}^{\text{\scriptsize I}}_{2},{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize I}}_{2})
, where for a given state set
state
,

{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{\scriptsize I}}_{2}={\mathcal{S}}^{\text{\scriptsize I}}_{2}={\mathcal{T}}^{\text{\scriptsize I}}_{2}\triangleq\mathcal{P}(\text{\footnotesize state}\times\text{\footnotesize itrace})
.

\forall N\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\text{\scriptsize I}}_{2}
,

E\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{\scriptsize I}}_{2}
,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} {\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize I}}_{2}(N,E)\triangleq\Set{(\sigma,\tau)}{(\sigma,\tau)\in N\vee\left(\exists\tau_{0},(\sigma,\tau_{0})\in E\wedge\tau_{0}\leqslant_{T}\tau\right)}\end{aligned}

With the RA instances
{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{2}
,
{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{\scriptsize F}}_{2}
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{\scriptsize I}}_{2}
, we can encode
\llbracket t\rrbracket\precsim\llbracket s\rrbracket
of Example
7.3
as follows.

(45)

\displaystyle\llbracket t\rrbracket\precsim\llbracket s\rrbracket\triangleq\left\{\begin{aligned} &\llbracket t\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\subseteq{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{2}(\llbracket s\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})},{\llbracket s\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}})\text{ and }\llbracket t\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\subseteq{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize F}}_{2}(\varnothing,\llbracket s\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})})\text{ and }\\
&\llbracket t\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize fin\_dvg})}\subseteq{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize F}}_{2}(\llbracket s\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize fin\_dvg})},\llbracket s\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})})\text{ and }\\
&\llbracket t\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize inf\_dvg})}\subseteq{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize I}}_{2}(\llbracket s\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize inf\_dvg})},\llbracket s\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})})\end{aligned}\right.

In fact, we can provide more general RA instances to encode nontrivial behavior refinement.
For instance, recall that we define the preservation of terminating behaviors in Example
7.4
as follows.

(46)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \llbracket t\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm})\precsim_{R}\;&(\llbracket s\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm}),\llbracket s\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize err}))\triangleq\forall\sigma_{t}\ \tau\ \sigma^{\prime}_{t}\ \sigma_{s},\ (\sigma_{t},\tau,\sigma^{\prime}_{t})\in\llbracket t\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\Rightarrow(\sigma_{s},\sigma_{t})\in R\Rightarrow\\
&\big(\exists\sigma^{\prime}_{s},(\sigma_{s},\tau,\sigma^{\prime}_{s})\in\llbracket s\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\wedge(\sigma^{\prime}_{s},\sigma^{\prime}_{t})\in R\big)\vee\left(\exists\tau_{0},(\sigma_{s},\tau_{0})\in\llbracket s\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\wedge\tau_{0}\leqslant_{T}\tau\right);\end{aligned}

We aim to define a termination instance (as given by Example
7.19
) of refinement algebras such that

\llbracket t\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm})\subseteq\gamma_{R}(\llbracket s\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm}),\llbracket s\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize err}))\Leftrightarrow\llbracket t\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm})\precsim_{R}(\llbracket s\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm}),\llbracket s\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize err}))

Example 7.19 (Termination instance 3).

{\mathcal{S}}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{3}\triangleq\mathcal{P}(\text{\footnotesize state}_{s}\times\text{\footnotesize trace}\times\text{\footnotesize state}_{s})
,
{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{3}\triangleq\mathcal{P}(\text{\footnotesize state}_{s}\times\text{\footnotesize trace})
, and
{\mathcal{T}}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{3}\triangleq\mathcal{P}(\text{\footnotesize state}_{t}\times\text{\footnotesize trace}\times\text{\footnotesize state}_{t})
. For a given relation
R\subseteq\text{\footnotesize state}_{s}\times\text{\footnotesize state}_{t}
,
\forall N\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{3},\,\forall E\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{3},

(47)

\displaystyle{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{3}(N,E)\triangleq\Set{(\sigma_{t},\tau,\sigma^{\prime}_{t})}{\forall\sigma_{s},(\sigma_{s},\sigma_{t})\in R\Rightarrow\,\begin{aligned} &(\exists\sigma^{\prime}_{s},(\sigma_{s},\tau,\sigma^{\prime}_{s})\in N\wedge(\sigma^{\prime}_{s},\sigma^{\prime}_{t})\in R)\\
&\vee\left(\exists\tau_{0},(\sigma_{s},\tau_{0})\in E\wedge\tau_{0}\leqslant_{T}\tau\right)\end{aligned}}

Similarly, we can define RA instances for finite divergence and infinite divergence as follows.

Example 7.20 (Finite divergence instance 3).

{\mathcal{S}}^{\text{\scriptsize F}}_{3}\triangleq\mathcal{P}(\text{\footnotesize state}_{s}\times\text{\footnotesize trace})
,
{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{\scriptsize F}}_{3}\triangleq\mathcal{P}(\text{\footnotesize state}_{s}\times\text{\footnotesize trace})
, and
{\mathcal{T}}^{\text{\scriptsize F}}_{3}\triangleq\mathcal{P}(\text{\footnotesize state}_{t}\times\text{\footnotesize trace})
. For a given relation
R\subseteq\text{\footnotesize state}_{s}\times\text{\footnotesize state}_{t}
,
\forall N\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\text{\scriptsize F}}_{3},\,\forall E\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{\scriptsize F}}_{3},

(48)

\displaystyle{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize F}}_{3}(N,E)\triangleq\Set{(\sigma_{t},\tau)}{\forall\sigma_{s},(\sigma_{s},\sigma_{t})\in R\Rightarrow\,(\sigma_{s},\tau)\in N\vee(\exists\tau_{0},(\sigma_{s},\tau_{0})\in E\wedge\tau_{0}\leqslant_{T}\tau)}

Example 7.21 (Infinite divergence instance 3).

{\mathcal{S}}^{\text{\scriptsize I}}_{3}\triangleq\mathcal{P}(\text{\footnotesize state}_{s}\times\text{\footnotesize itrace})
,
{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{\scriptsize I}}_{3}\triangleq\mathcal{P}(\text{\footnotesize state}_{s}\times\text{\footnotesize trace})
, and
{\mathcal{T}}^{\text{\scriptsize I}}_{3}\triangleq\mathcal{P}(\text{\footnotesize state}_{t}\times\text{\footnotesize itrace})
. For a given relation
R\subseteq\text{\footnotesize state}_{s}\times\text{\footnotesize state}_{t}
,
\forall N\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\text{\scriptsize I}}_{3},\,\forall E\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{\scriptsize I}}_{3},

(49)

\displaystyle{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize I}}_{3}(N,E)\triangleq\Set{(\sigma_{t},\tau)}{\forall\sigma_{s},(\sigma_{s},\sigma_{t})\in R\Rightarrow\,(\sigma_{s},\tau)\in N\vee(\exists\tau_{0},(\sigma_{s},\tau_{0})\in E\wedge\tau_{0}\leqslant_{T}\tau)}

With the RA instances
{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{3}=({\mathcal{S}}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{3},{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{3},{\mathcal{T}}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{3},{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{3})
,
{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{\scriptsize F}}_{3}=({\mathcal{S}}^{\text{\scriptsize F}}_{3},{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{\scriptsize F}}_{3},{\mathcal{T}}^{\text{\scriptsize F}}_{3},{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize F}}_{3})
, and
{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{\scriptsize I}}_{3}=({\mathcal{S}}^{\text{\scriptsize I}}_{3},{\mathcal{E}}^{\text{\scriptsize I}}_{3},{\mathcal{T}}^{\text{\scriptsize I}}_{3},{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize I}}_{3})
, we can encode
\llbracket t\rrbracket\precsim_{R}\llbracket s\rrbracket
of Example
7.4
as follows, and obviously we have
\llbracket t\rrbracket\sqsubseteq_{R}\llbracket s\rrbracket\Leftrightarrow\llbracket t\rrbracket\precsim_{R}\llbracket s\rrbracket
.

(50)

\displaystyle\llbracket t\rrbracket\sqsubseteq_{R}\llbracket s\rrbracket\triangleq\left\{\begin{aligned} &\llbracket t\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm})\subseteq{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{3}(\llbracket s\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm}),\llbracket s\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize err}))\text{ and }\llbracket t\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize err})\subseteq{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize F}}_{3}(\varnothing,\llbracket s\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize err}))\text{ and }\\
&\llbracket t\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize fin\_dvg})\subseteq{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize F}}_{3}(\llbracket s\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize fin\_dvg}),\llbracket s\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize err}))\text{ and }\\
&\llbracket t\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize inf\_dvg})\subseteq{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize I}}_{3}(\llbracket s\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize inf\_dvg}),\llbracket s\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize err}))\end{aligned}\right.

From these RA instances, we observe that the RA-based formulation, via the
\gamma
function, provides an algebraic characterization of the correspondence between source and target behaviors. This formulation not only offers a uniform and compositional foundation for proving compiler correctness, but also enables the extension of algebraic verification methods from simple cases to more realistic compilation settings. Before turning to more complex RA instances, we first proceed to introduce enhanced RA structures to further consolidate the algebraic underpinnings of our framework.

7.3.
Enhanced Refinement Algebras

Since the union and composition operators are ubiquitous in the semantic definition of composite statements, we define the following enhanced RAs to facilitate structured compiler verification.

Definition 7.22 (Union-preserved RAs).

A refinement algebra
\mathcal{A}=(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{E},\mathcal{T},\gamma)
is
union-preserved
iff:

(51)

\displaystyle\forall N_{1}\,N_{2}\in\mathcal{S},\;\forall E_{1}\,E_{2}\in\mathcal{E},\,\gamma(N_{1},E_{1})\cup\gamma(N_{2},E_{2})\subseteq\gamma(N_{1}\cup N_{2},E_{1}\cup E_{2})

Definition 7.23 (Concatenation-preserved RAs).

Refinement algebras
\mathcal{A}_{1}=(\mathcal{S}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{1},\gamma_{1})
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}=(\mathcal{S}_{2},\mathcal{E}_{2},\mathcal{T}_{2},\gamma_{2})
are
concatenation-preserved
to refinement algebra
\mathcal{A}_{3}=(\mathcal{S}_{3},\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{3},\gamma_{3})
iff:
given operators

\circ:\mathcal{T}_{1}\times\mathcal{T}_{2}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{3}
,

\circ:\mathcal{S}_{1}\times\mathcal{S}_{2}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{3}
, and

\circ:\mathcal{S}_{1}\times\mathcal{E}_{2}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{1}
s.t. for any

N_{1},\,N_{2},\,E_{1},E_{2}
,

(52)

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}(N_{1},E_{1})\circ\gamma_{2}(N_{2},E_{2})\subseteq\gamma_{3}\big(N_{1}\circ N_{2},E_{1}\cup(N_{1}\circ E_{2})\big)

Particularly, if RAs
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{A}
are concatenation-preserved to
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{A}
is
self-concatenation-preserved
.
Note that for a given RA
(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{E},\mathcal{T},\gamma)
, writing
\gamma(N,E)
implicitly means that
N\in\mathcal{S}
and
E\in\mathcal{E}
.

Lemma 7.24 (Instances of enhanced refinement algebras).

{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{i}
,
{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{\scriptsize F}}_{i}
, and
{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{\scriptsize I}}_{i}
(defined in §
7.2
) are union-preserved refinement algebras;
(ii)
{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{i}
is self-concatenation-preserved;
(iii)
{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{i}
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{\scriptsize F}}_{i}
are
concatenation-preserved to
{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{\scriptsize F}}_{i}
; and
(iv)
{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{i}
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{\scriptsize I}}_{i}
are
concatenation-preserved to
{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{\scriptsize I}}_{i}
for
i=1,2,3
.
Concrete operators for these RA instances are provided in §
3.1
according to the type of behavior sets.

This figure schematically illustrates that CompCert-style behavior refinement is preserved by sequential composition for normal termination. The figure visually summarizes the concatenation-preservation property used to prove that refinement of sequential statements follows from refinement of their components.

Figure 9
.
The behavior refinement for CompCert is compositional w.r.t. composition.

We first provide a brief preview of how the enhanced RA instances from Lemma
7.24
enable a uniform refinement proof (see §
8
for details). For a structural transformation of sequential statements (as in Example
7.1
), the proof proceeds as follows: the crucial step
(\ref{pr:ind_hypo})\subseteq(\ref{pr:concat_incl})
follows immediately from the concatenation-preservation property of the underlying RA instances. Fig.
9
provides a schematic illustration of this property for normally terminating behaviors in CompCert.

(53)

\displaystyle\llbracket t_{1};t_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}=\llbracket t_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\circ\llbracket t_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}

(54)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \subseteq{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{i}(\llbracket s_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})},\ \llbracket s_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})})\circ{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{i}(\llbracket s_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})},\ \llbracket s_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})})\end{aligned}

(55)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \subseteq{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{i}(\llbracket s_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\circ\llbracket s_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})},\ \llbracket s_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\cup(\llbracket s_{1}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\circ\llbracket s_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}))\end{aligned}

(56)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} ={\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{i}(\llbracket s_{1};s_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})},\llbracket s_{1};s_{2}\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})})\end{aligned}

Since the star
*
and infinity
\infty
operators are ubiquitous in the semantic definition of loops and recursion, we define the following enhanced RAs to facilitate structured compiler verification.

Definition 7.25 (Star-preserved RAs).

RAs
\mathcal{A}_{1}=(\mathcal{S}_{1},\mathcal{E},\mathcal{T}_{1},\gamma_{1})
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}=(\mathcal{S}_{2},\mathcal{E},\mathcal{T}_{2},\gamma_{2})
are
star-preserved
to
\mathcal{A}_{2}
iff: given operators

\circ:\mathcal{T}_{1}\times\mathcal{T}_{2}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{2}
,

\circ:\mathcal{S}_{1}\times\mathcal{S}_{2}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{2}
, and

\circ:\mathcal{S}_{1}\times\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
,
and star operators

*:\mathcal{T}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{1}
and
*:\mathcal{S}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{1}
such that for any

N_{1}\in\mathcal{S}_{1}
,

N_{2}\in\mathcal{S}_{2}
,

E_{1}
and
E_{2}\in\mathcal{E}
,

(57)

\displaystyle(\gamma_{1}(N_{1},E_{1}))^{*}\circ\gamma_{2}(N_{2},E_{2})\subseteq\gamma_{2}\big(N_{1}^{*}\circ N_{2},N_{1}^{*}\circ(E_{1}\cup E_{2})\big)

Definition 7.26 (Infinity-preserved RAs).

RAs
\mathcal{A}_{1}=(\mathcal{S}_{1},\mathcal{E},\mathcal{T}_{1},\gamma_{1})
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}=(\mathcal{S}_{2},\mathcal{E},\mathcal{T}_{2},\gamma_{2})
are
infinity-preserved
to
\mathcal{A}_{2}
iff: given composition operators

\circ:\mathcal{T}_{1}\times\mathcal{T}_{2}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{2}
,

\circ:\mathcal{S}_{1}\times\mathcal{S}_{2}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{2}
, and

\circ:\mathcal{S}_{1}\times\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
,
star operators

*:\mathcal{T}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{1}
,

*:\mathcal{S}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{1}
,
and infinity operators

\infty:\mathcal{T}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{2}
,

\infty:\mathcal{S}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{2}

such that for any

N_{1}\in\mathcal{S}_{1}
,

N_{2}\in\mathcal{S}_{2}
, and

E_{1},E_{2}\in\mathcal{E}
,

(58)

\displaystyle\big((\gamma_{1}(N_{1},E_{1}))^{*}\circ\gamma_{2}(N_{2},E_{2})\big)\cup\bigl(\gamma_{1}(N_{1},E_{1})\bigr)^{\infty}\subseteq\gamma_{2}\big((N_{1}^{*}\circ N_{2})\cup N_{1}^{\infty},N_{1}^{*}\circ(E_{1}\cup E_{2})\big)

Connection to fixed points

Given sets
X
and
Y
, the form
X^{*}\circ Y
is actually the Kleene (least) fixed point of function
f(x)=Y\cup(X\circ x)
, and
(X^{*}\circ Y)\cup X^{\infty}
is the greatest fixed point of function
f
. They are frequently used in the semantic definition of loops and recursion (as shown in §
3
and §
4
), so we use enhanced RAs to facilitate the compilation correctness proof of loops and recursion.

Lemma 7.27 (Instances of star/infinity-preserved refinement algebras).

(i)
{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{i}
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{\scriptsize F}}_{i}
(defined in §
7.2
) are
star-preserved to
{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{\scriptsize F}}_{i}
;
and
(ii)
{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{i}
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{\scriptsize I}}_{i}
are
infinity-preserved to
{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{\scriptsize I}}_{i}
for
i=1,2,3
.
