7.5.
Kripke Relations for Cminorgen

CompCert employs a hierarchy of memory relations—ranging from simple identity to extension and, in the most intricate cases, memory injection. Each phase is thus verified with the weakest relation sufficient for capturing its memory effects, which keeps proofs as lightweight as possible.

Among phases of CompCert, Cminorgen is one of the most challenging phase: it eliminates scalar local variables that are never addressed with the & operator from activation records and coalesces separate memory blocks allocated to addressed local variables within a function activation into a single block representing the activation record as a whole, thus reshaping memory layout in a nontrivial way. In the original CompCert proof, this is justified using the memory injection relation. In our framework, we recast this verification by exposing memory injections in a Kripke relation.
We show key definitions as follows, where the memory model of CompCert is explained in §
6.1
.

Memory injection of CompCert

CompCert introduces
injection functions
to track the general correspondence between memory blocks. An injection function
f:\text{\footnotesize ifun}
(
ifun

\triangleq\mathbb{B}\rightharpoonup\mathbb{B}\times\mathbb{Z}
) is a partial function mapping source memory blocks to target locations, in the following form:

f(b_{1})=\bot\quad\text{ or }\quad f(b_{1})=(b_{2},\delta).

The former means that
b_{1}
is unmapped, and the later means that a location in
b_{1}
is mapped to a target location of block
b_{2}
with
\delta
offset increased. Given an injection function
f
,
we use
v_{1}\mapsto_{f}^{\text{\scriptsize V}}v_{2}
to assert the correspondent of program values (naturally lifted to
\overrightarrow{v_{1}}\mapsto_{f}^{\text{\scriptsize V}}\overrightarrow{v_{2}}
for a list of values), and use
m_{1}\mapsto_{f}^{\text{\scriptsize M}}m_{2}
to represent
memory injections
. Specifically,
a pointer value
\text{\footnotesize Vptr}(b_{1},o_{1})
is injected to
\text{\footnotesize Vptr}(b_{2},o_{2})
if there is a
\delta\in\mathbb{Z}
s.t.
f(b_{1})={(b_{2},\delta)}
and
o_{2}=o_{1}+\delta
; integers or floating numbers are injected if the source value is identical to the target value; and
the special value
Vundef
can be injected to any values.
Memory injection
m_{1}\mapsto_{f}^{\text{\scriptsize M}}m_{2}
holds if memory contents in
m_{1}
are preserved to
m_{2}
for all injected addresses (simplified for presentation purposes), i.e.,

\displaystyle\text{for any }b_{1}\;b_{2}\;o_{1}\;o_{2},\;

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \text{\footnotesize Vptr}(b_{1},o_{1})\mapsto_{f}^{\text{\scriptsize V}}\text{\footnotesize Vptr}(b_{2},o_{2})\Rightarrow m_{1}(b_{1},o_{1})\mapsto_{f}^{\text{\scriptsize V}}m_{2}(b_{2},o_{2}).\end{aligned}

We reuse the notion of Kripke memory relation for protection called
injp

(
Koenig and Shao, 2021
;
Zhang et al., 2024
)
to formulate rely-guarantee reasoning
(
Liang et al., 2012
)
in our framework.

Definition 7.35 (The Kripke memory relation
injp
).

\text{\footnotesize injp}\triangleq\langle W_{\text{\scriptsize injp}},\leadsto_{\text{\scriptsize injp}},R_{\text{\scriptsize injp}}\rangle
, where
W_{\text{\scriptsize injp}}\triangleq\text{\footnotesize ifun}\times\text{\footnotesize mem}\times\text{\footnotesize mem}
,
R_{\text{\scriptsize injp}}:\mathcal{K}_{W_{\text{\tiny injp}}}(\text{\footnotesize mem},\text{\footnotesize mem})
is a Kripke relation between memory states such that

\forall f\,m_{1}\,m_{2},\;(m_{1},m_{2})\in R_{\text{\scriptsize injp}}(f,m_{1},m_{2})\Leftrightarrow m_{1}\mapsto_{f}^{\text{\scriptsize M}}m_{2}

and
\leadsto_{\text{\scriptsize injp}}(\subseteq W_{\text{\scriptsize injp}}\times W_{\text{\scriptsize injp}})
is an accessibility relation s.t. for any worlds
(f,m_{1},m_{2}),(f^{\prime},m_{1}^{\prime},m_{2}^{\prime})
,

\displaystyle(f,m_{1},m_{2})\leadsto_{\text{\scriptsize injp}}(f^{\prime},m_{1}^{\prime},m_{2}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\begin{aligned} \left\{\begin{aligned} (1)&\ \text{\footnotesize unmapped}(f)\subseteq\text{\footnotesize unchanged\_on}(m_{1},m_{1}^{\prime});\\
(2)&\ \text{\footnotesize out\_of\_reach}(f,m_{1})\subseteq\text{\footnotesize unchanged\_on}(m_{2},m_{2}^{\prime});\\
(3)&\ \text{\footnotesize separately\_increased}(f,f^{\prime},m_{1},m_{2});\text{ and}\\
(4)&\ \text{\footnotesize mem\_forward}(m_{1},m_{1}^{\prime})\land\text{\footnotesize mem\_forward}(m_{2},m_{2}^{\prime}).\end{aligned}\right.\end{aligned}

Here
\text{\footnotesize unchanged\_on}(m,m^{\prime})
denotes the set of memory locations where the memory contents remain unchanged between
m
and
m^{\prime}
.
Clause (1) asserts that values at locations unmapped by
f
remain unchanged.
Clause (2) indicates that values at locations not mapped to by
f
from valid blocks of
m_{1}
are also unchanged.
Clause (3) states that
f^{\prime}
extends
f
by adding mappings for new blocks while preserving the existing mappings, and new blocks are not permitted to map into the existing memory blocks of
m_{2}
.
Clause (4) represents side conditions that are not essential to the core idea.

Intuitively, the relation
\leadsto_{\text{\scriptsize injp}}
governs the evolution of worlds across internal executions and external calls while enforcing disciplined memory protection. Concretely, private and public memory locations are identified by an injection
f
: public locations are those related by
f
, whereas private locations are either unmapped in the source or out-of-reach in the target (having no preimage through
f
). During world evolution, memory cells at private locations must remain unchanged, ensuring that the environment cannot observe or modify them, while public locations are preserved up to the injection so that loads, stores, and pointer comparisons behave consistently between the source and target. These constraints enable
injp
to account for complex memory transformations, such as allocating new stack slots for spilled register variables, reorganizing stack frames, and extending or coalescing memory blocks across compilation phases, while preserving behavioral consistency, as demonstrated in CompCertO
(
Koenig and Shao, 2021
;
Zhang et al., 2024
)
.

The Kripke memory relation

injp
can be naturally integrated into our framework as follows.

Definition 7.36 (The Kripke relation for Statement Transformation of Cminorgen).

Given the set of program states
\text{\footnotesize state}_{s},\text{\footnotesize state}_{t}
(respectively for Csharpminor and Cminor), where
\text{\footnotesize state}_{s}=\text{\footnotesize state}_{t}\triangleq\text{\footnotesize tenv}\times\text{\footnotesize mem}
.
We define the Kripke relation
R_{B}:\mathcal{K}_{W_{\text{\tiny injp}}}(\text{\footnotesize state}_{s},\text{\footnotesize state}_{t})
for Cminorgen as follows, for any
(f,m_{1},m_{2})\in W_{\text{\scriptsize injp}}
,
(te_{1},m_{1})\in\text{\footnotesize state}_{s}
, and
(te_{2},m_{2})\in\text{\footnotesize state}_{t}
,

\displaystyle\bigl((te_{1},m_{1}),(te_{2},m_{2})\bigr)\in R_{B}(f,m_{1},m_{2})\Leftrightarrow m_{1}\mapsto_{f}^{\text{\scriptsize M}}m_{2}\land\forall x,\;te_{1}(x)\mapsto_{f}^{\text{\scriptsize V}}te_{2}(x)

Definition 7.37 (The Kripke interface relation for Cminorgen).

A_{\text{\scriptsize C}}\triangleq\langle W_{\text{\scriptsize injp}},\leadsto_{\text{\scriptsize injp}},{R}^{q}_{\text{\scriptsize C}},{R}^{r}_{\text{\scriptsize C}}\rangle
, where
for any C queries
q_{1}=(id_{1},\overrightarrow{v_{1}},m_{1})
,
q_{2}=(id_{2},\overrightarrow{v_{2}},m_{2})
,
and C replies
r_{1}=({v_{1}^{\prime}},m_{1}^{\prime})
,
r_{2}=({v_{2}^{\prime}},m_{2}^{\prime})
,

\displaystyle\bigl((id_{1},\overrightarrow{v_{1}},m_{1}),(id_{2},\overrightarrow{v_{2}},m_{2})\bigr)\in{R}^{q}_{\text{\scriptsize C}}(f,m_{1},m_{2})

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow id_{1}=id_{2}\land\overrightarrow{v_{1}}\mapsto_{f}^{\text{\scriptsize V}}\overrightarrow{v_{2}}\land m_{1}\mapsto_{f}^{\text{\scriptsize M}}m_{2}

\displaystyle\bigl(({v_{1}^{\prime}},m_{1}^{\prime}),({v_{2}^{\prime}},m_{2}^{\prime})\bigr)\in{R}^{r}_{\text{\scriptsize C}}(f^{\prime},m_{1}^{\prime},m_{2}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow v_{1}^{\prime}\mapsto_{f^{\prime}}^{\text{\scriptsize V}}v_{2}^{\prime}\land m_{1}^{\prime}\mapsto_{f^{\prime}}^{\text{\scriptsize M}}m_{2}^{\prime}

Using the above Kripke relations (with
A=B\triangleq A_{\text{\scriptsize C}}
in Def.
7.31
), we have successfully reverified Cminorgen—one of the most intricate memory-reorganizing phases in CompCert—within our framework, which indicates that our proof techniques are scalable to the remainder of CompCert.

7.6.
Design Choices

In this subsection, we explain why
\gamma
is designed to take two arguments rather than just one.
For illustration, recall that Example
7.4
defines the preservation of terminating behaviors as follows.

(60)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \llbracket t\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm}){\precsim_{R}}\;&(\llbracket s\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize nrm}),\llbracket s\rrbracket.(\text{\footnotesize err}))\triangleq\forall\sigma_{t}\ \tau\ \sigma^{\prime}_{t}\ \sigma_{s},\ (\sigma_{t},\tau,\sigma^{\prime}_{t})\in\llbracket t\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\Rightarrow(\sigma_{s},\sigma_{t})\in R\Rightarrow\\
&\big(\exists\sigma^{\prime}_{s},(\sigma_{s},\tau,\sigma^{\prime}_{s})\in\llbracket s\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\wedge(\sigma^{\prime}_{s},\sigma^{\prime}_{t})\in R\big)\vee\left(\exists\tau_{0},(\sigma_{s},\tau_{0})\in\llbracket s\rrbracket.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\wedge\tau_{0}\leqslant_{T}\tau\right);\end{aligned}

where
the termination behavior of a target program may correspond either to the terminating behavior of the source program or to its aborting behavior, with different requirements: starting from related initial states, the former requires the final states to be related, whereas the latter does not.

We next show that designing
\gamma
-functions with a single parameter would be less flexible to meet the two different requirements than that with two parameters by contraposition.

Specifically, suppose we intended to find instances of
\gamma:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}
to encode Formula (
60
) for algebraic reasoning. There may exist two choices, and they must satisfy the soundness conditions as follows.

(61)

\displaystyle\text{Chioce 1: }\;N_{t}\subseteq{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{R}(N_{s})\cup\bigl({\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize E}}_{R}(E_{s})\times\text{\footnotesize state}_{t}\bigr);\qquad\text{Choice 2: }\;N_{t}\subseteq{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{R}\bigl(N_{s}\cup(\widetilde{E}_{s}\times\text{\footnotesize state}_{s})\bigr)

(62)

\displaystyle\text{Soundness of chioce 1:}\qquad N_{t}\subseteq{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{R}(N_{s})\cup\bigl({\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize E}}_{R}(E_{s})\times\text{\footnotesize state}_{t}\bigr)\;\Rightarrow\;N_{t}\precsim_{R}(N_{s},E_{s})

(63)

\displaystyle\text{Soundness of choice 2:}\qquad N_{t}\subseteq{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{R}\bigl(N_{s}\cup(\widetilde{E}_{s}\times\text{\footnotesize state}_{s})\bigr)\;\Rightarrow\;N_{t}\precsim_{R}(N_{s},E_{s})

(64)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \text{where }&\widetilde{E}_{s}\triangleq\Set{(\sigma,\tau)\in\text{\footnotesize state}_{s}\times\text{\footnotesize trace}\mid\exists\tau_{0}\;\tau_{1},\;(\sigma,\tau_{0})\in E_{s}\land\tau=\tau_{0}\cdot\tau_{1}}\\
&{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{R}:\mathcal{P}(\text{\footnotesize state}_{s}\times\text{\footnotesize trace}\times\text{\footnotesize state}_{s})\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(\text{\footnotesize state}_{t}\times\text{\footnotesize trace}\times\text{\footnotesize state}_{t})\\
&{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize E}}_{R}:\mathcal{P}(\text{\footnotesize state}_{s}\times\text{\footnotesize trace})\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(\text{\footnotesize state}_{t}\times\text{\footnotesize trace})\end{aligned}

where in Choice 2 we employ a trace-extension operator
\widetilde{E}_{s}
for the source aborting behavior
E_{s}
such that the observable event trace of
E_{s}
can be a prefix of the target terminating behavior
N_{t}
.
In fact, we can define the following instances of
\gamma
that satisfy the above soundness conditions.

(65)

\displaystyle{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{R}(N)\triangleq\Set{(\sigma_{t},\tau,\sigma^{\prime}_{t})}{\forall\sigma_{s},(\sigma_{s},\sigma_{t})\in R\Rightarrow(\exists\sigma^{\prime}_{s},(\sigma_{s},\tau,\sigma^{\prime}_{s})\in N\wedge(\sigma^{\prime}_{s},\sigma^{\prime}_{t})\in R)}

(66)

\displaystyle{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize E}}_{R}(E)\triangleq\Set{(\sigma_{t},\tau)}{\forall\sigma_{s},(\sigma_{s},\sigma_{t})\in R\Rightarrow\exists\tau_{0},(\sigma_{s},\tau_{0})\in E\wedge\tau_{0}\leqslant_{T}\tau}

(a)

Behavior refinement of choice 1

(b)

Behavior refinement of choice 2

Figure 10
.
Choice 1 separates correspondences for normal termination and abortion using distinct

\gamma
-instances, but they fail to cover full cases:
{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{R}
fails for (
\Sigma_{E}-\Sigma_{N}
) and
{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize E}}_{R}
fails for (
\Sigma_{N}-\Sigma_{E}
). Choice 2 forces uniform correspondence between the final states, even when the target terminates normally but the source aborts.
This figure compares two unsuccessful design choices for defining behavior refinement between target normal termination and source normal termination or abortion. Subfigure (a), Choice 1, uses two separate \(\gamma\)-instances: one for source normal termination and one for source abortion. The diagram highlights a mismatch between the set \(\Sigma_N\) of source initial states that may terminate and the set \(\Sigma_E\) of source initial states that may abort. States in \(\Sigma_E \setminus\Sigma_N\) are not properly handled by the normal-termination instance, and states in \(\Sigma_N \setminus\Sigma_E\) are not properly handled by the abortion instance, so the two cases cannot be cleanly combined. Subfigure (b), Choice 2, forces normal termination and abortion into a single combined relation. This gives uniform correspondence with the target’s normal termination, but it also requires assigning an artificial final source state even when the source aborts and has no real final state. The figure illustrates why both choices are inadequate for realistic compiler verification.

Choice 1
employs distinct instances of
\gamma
to separately capture the two correspondences between source and target behaviors. The instance
{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{R}
characterizes cases where, from any related initial state, the source program may terminate normally with a matching final state. The instance
{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize E}}_{R}
characterizes cases where, from any related initial state, the source program may abort.
However, there may exist related source states from which the source program does not terminate or does not abort. More specifically, we define
\Sigma_{N}\triangleq\Set{\sigma_{s}}{\exists\tau\,\sigma_{s}^{\prime},\,(\sigma_{s},\tau,\sigma_{s}^{\prime})\in N_{s}}
, and
\Sigma_{E}\triangleq\Set{\sigma_{e}}{\exists\tau,\,(\sigma_{e},\tau)\in E_{s}}
, which respectively denotes the set of initial states for termination and abortion.
As illustrated in Fig.
10
,
those states in
\Sigma_{N}
but not in
\Sigma_{E}
are not properly quantified by
{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize E}}_{R}
, and those states in
\Sigma_{E}
but not in
\Sigma_{N}
are not properly quantified by
{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{R}
.
Simply taking the union of
{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{R}
and
{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize E}}_{R}
does not solve this problem.

Choice 2
avoids this issue by taking the union of
N_{s}
and
\widetilde{E_{s}}\times\text{\footnotesize state}_{s}
first.
This enforces a uniform treatment of normal termination and abortion, aligning both with the target’s normal termination.
As a result, whenever the source program aborts, one must provide an artificial final source state related to the actual target final state, even though aborted executions do not yield canonical final states.
That is, for any target state there must exist a related source state, which may not in fact exist.
This additional requirement is unnatural and often infeasible under general state-matching relations.
Therefore, both choices are ill-suited for realistic compiler verification.
By contraposition, we conclude that it would be more challenging to instantiate a single-parameterized
\gamma
for algebraic compiler verification, especially when the source semantics is nondeterministic.

8.
Compositional Correctness

Refinement algebras further enhance the compositionality of compiler verification. The semantic operators in extended KATs enable the semantic composition of program substructures, while refinement algebras support the compositionality of behavioral refinement. Their strong algebraic properties allow us to design automated proof tactics to mechanize compiler verification.
In this section, taking the transformation of Clight statements in Cshmgen as an example, we first show the proof style of our framework in §
8.1
, along with two essential automated tactics. We then present the correctness of compilation phases in §
8.2
and the module-level compositionality in §
8.3
.

8.1.
Proof Style

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{T}_{\text{\footnotesize Cshmgens}}(n_{b},n_{c},s_{1})=\textbf{Succeed}(u_{1})\;\land\;\mathcal{T}_{\text{\footnotesize Cshmgens}}(n_{b},n_{c},s_{2})=\textbf{Succeed}(u_{2})\end{array}}{\mathcal{T}_{\text{\footnotesize Cshmgens}}(n_{b},n_{c},s_{1};s_{2})=\textbf{Succeed}(u_{1};u_{2})}\;\textsc{Seq}

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\end{array}}{\mathcal{T}_{\text{\footnotesize Cshmgens}}(n_{b},n_{c},\textbf{break})=\textbf{Suc.}(\textbf{exit }n_{b})}\;\textsc{Brk}\quad\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\end{array}}{\mathcal{T}_{\text{\footnotesize Cshmgens}}(n_{b},n_{c},\textbf{continue})=\textbf{Suc.}(\textbf{exit }n_{c})}\;\textsc{Ctn}

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{T}_{\text{\footnotesize Cshmgens}}(1,0,s_{1})=\textbf{Succeed}(u_{1})\;\land\;\mathcal{T}_{\text{\footnotesize Cshmgens}}(0,n_{c}+1,s_{2})=\textbf{Succeed}(u_{2})\end{array}}{\mathcal{T}_{\text{\footnotesize Cshmgens}}(n_{b},n_{c},\textbf{loop }s_{1}\ s_{2})=\textbf{Succeed}\bigl(\textbf{block}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\{}\textbf{Sloop }(\textbf{block }\{u_{1}\};u_{2}){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\}}\bigr)}\;\textsc{Loop}

Figure 11
.
Original Cshmgen rules from CompCert (selectively listed), where
Suc.
is short for
Succeed
.
This figure lists selected statement-translation rules from the original Cshmgen phase of CompCert, written as inference rules. The Seq rule states that if statements \(s_1\) and \(s_2\) translate successfully to \(u_1\) and \(u_2\), then the sequential statement \(s_1;s_2\) translates to the sequential composition \(u_1;u_2\). The Brk rule translates \textbf{break} to a successful result containing \textbf{exit} \(n_b\), and the Ctn rule translates \textbf{continue} to a successful result containing \textbf{exit} \(n_c\). The Loop rule translates a Clight loop with body statements \(s_1\) and \(s_2\) into a Csharpminor term built from nested \textbf{block} constructs and an \textbf{Sloop}; the translated first body \(u_1\) is placed inside an inner block, followed by the translated second body \(u_2\). Overall, the figure illustrates how Cshmgen compiles structured control flow into lower-level block, loop, and exit constructs.

The proof style in our framework is demonstrated by the following proof sketch of Lemma
8.1
, where
\mathcal{T}_{\text{\footnotesize Cshmgens}}
denotes the statement translation of Cshmgen, with selected compilation rules shown in Fig.
11
.
We next omit the
R_{B}
, defined in Def.
7.30
, for the behavior refinement
\sqsubseteq_{(R_{B},n_{b},n_{c},\chi_{e})}
.

Lemma 8.1.

For any natural numbers

n_{b}
,
n_{c}
, and callee function behaviors
\chi_{t}
,
\chi_{s}
,
\chi_{e}
, if
n_{b}\neq n_{c}
and
\chi_{t}\subseteq{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{R_{B}}(\chi_{s},\chi_{e})
, then for any Clight statement
s
and Csharpminor statement
u
,

\displaystyle\text{if}\ \ \mathcal{T}_{\text{\footnotesize Cshmgens}}(n_{b},n_{c},s)=\textbf{Succeed}(u),\text{ then }\llbracket u\rrbracket_{\chi_{t}}\sqsubseteq_{(n_{b},n_{c},\chi_{e})}\llbracket s\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.

Proof sketch.

By induction on the statement
s
, we derive proof obligations asserting that for every syntactic construct of
s
, if each substructure of
s
satisfies the refinement relation with its compiled counterpart, then the entire statement
s
does as well. To illustrate how the refinement algebra works in the proof, we demonstrate the core ideas behind three key proof obligations.

Case 1:
s
is a sequential statement

By induction, since
\mathcal{T}_{\text{\footnotesize Cshmgens}}(n_{b},n_{c},s_{1};s_{2})=\textbf{Succeed}(u)
, then

\mathcal{T}_{\text{\footnotesize Cshmgens}}(n_{b},n_{c},s_{1})=\textbf{Succeed}(u_{1})
,

\mathcal{T}_{\text{\footnotesize Cshmgens}}(n_{b},n_{c},s_{2})=\textbf{Succeed}(u_{2})
, and
u=u_{1};u_{2}
. Prove that:

(67)

\displaystyle\text{if }\llbracket u_{1}\rrbracket_{\chi_{t}}\sqsubseteq_{(n_{b},n_{c},\chi_{e})}\llbracket s_{1}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}\!\text{ and }\llbracket u_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi_{t}}\sqsubseteq_{(n_{b},n_{c},\chi_{e})}\llbracket s_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}\text{, then }\llbracket u_{1};u_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi_{t}}\sqsubseteq_{(n_{b},n_{c},\chi_{e})}\llbracket s_{1};s_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.

For the sake of brevity, let
N_{i}
,
E_{i}
and
C_{i}
respectively represent

\llbracket s_{i}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}
,
\llbracket s_{i}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize err})}
and
\llbracket s_{i}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize cll})}
for
i=
1, 2. The proofs for the refinement of terminating behaviors are shown as follows.

(68)

\displaystyle\llbracket u_{1};u_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi_{t}}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}=\llbracket u_{1}\rrbracket_{\chi_{t}}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\circ\llbracket u_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi_{t}}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}

(69)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \subseteq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{R_{B}}(N_{1},\ E_{1}\cup C_{1}\circ\chi_{e})}\circ{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{R_{B}}(N_{2},\ E_{2}\cup C_{2}\circ\chi_{e})}\end{aligned}

(70)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \subseteq{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{R_{B}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}N_{1}\circ N_{2}},\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(E_{1}\cup C_{1}\circ\chi_{e})\cup}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}N_{1}\circ\left(E_{2}\cup C_{2}\circ\chi_{e}\right)})\end{aligned}

(71)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \subseteq{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{R_{B}}(N_{1}\circ N_{2},\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(E_{1}\cup N_{1}\circ E_{2})\cup}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\left(C_{1}\cup N_{1}\circ C_{2}\right)\circ\chi_{e}})\end{aligned}

(72)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} ={\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{R_{B}}(\llbracket s_{1};s_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})},\llbracket s_{1};s_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\cup\llbracket s_{1};s_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize cll})}\circ\chi_{e})\end{aligned}

The key to the above proofs is the inclusion (
69
)
\subseteq
(
70
), which is directly solved by the concatenation-preserved property of RAs.
The inclusion (
70
)
\subseteq
(
71
) is by the axioms of Kleene Algebras;
the inclusion (
68
)
\subseteq
(
69
) is by hypotheses;
and the equivalence in (
68
) and between (
71
)
\sim
(
72
) holds by definition.
We see that refinement algebras reduce refinement proofs into the proof of relational propositions in Kleene Algebras.
Other cases for sequential statements can be proved similarly.

Case 2:
s
is a loop statement

By structural induction, the proof goal for the terminating case for example is to show:
if
\llbracket u_{1}\rrbracket_{\chi_{t}}\sqsubseteq_{(1,0,\chi_{e})}\llbracket s_{1}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}\!\text{ and }\llbracket u_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi_{t}}\sqsubseteq_{(0,n_{c}+1,\chi_{e})}\llbracket s_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}
, then

(73)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\llbracket\textbf{block}\{\textbf{Sloop }(\textbf{block }\{u_{1}\};u_{2})\}\rrbracket_{\chi_{t}}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\ \subseteq\\
&\quad\quad{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{R_{B}}\big(\llbracket\textbf{loop }s_{1}\ s_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})},\llbracket\textbf{loop }s_{1}\ s_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\cup(\llbracket\textbf{loop }s_{1}\ s_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize cll})}\circ\chi_{e})\big)\end{aligned}

Since the semantics of loops is defined with extended KATs operators
∗
(for the reflexive transitive closure) and
∞
(for the infinite iterations of composition), the behavior refinement between them can be efficiently verified using the instances of star-preserved RAs and infinity-preserved RAs.

In our
Rocq
implementation, proof obligations for composition statements (e.g., case 1 and case 2) is mechanically
solved by two automated new proof tactics:
gamma_simpl
and
solvefix
. The
gamma_simpl
tactic automatically reduces the behavior refinement between source and target programs to relational propositions of extended KATs, by properties of refinement algebras. The
solvefix
tactic mechanically verifies these relational propositions by properties of extended KATs. More specifically,
by unfolding the definition of the left-hand side of Formula (
73
), the goal is to show:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\big(({\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\llbracket u_{1}\rrbracket_{\chi_{t}}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket u_{1}\rrbracket_{\chi_{t}}.\text{({\footnotesize blk})}_{0}})\circ{\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}\llbracket u_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi_{t}}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}}\big)^{*}\ \circ\\
&\quad\big({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,1}\llbracket u_{1}\rrbracket_{\chi_{t}}.\text{({\footnotesize blk})}_{1}}\cup(({\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\llbracket u_{1}\rrbracket_{\chi_{t}}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket u_{1}\rrbracket_{\chi_{t}}.\text{({\footnotesize blk})}_{0}})\circ{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}\llbracket u_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi_{t}}.\text{({\footnotesize blk})}_{0}})\big)\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \quad\quad\quad\ \ \subseteq&\ {\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{R_{B}}(\llbracket\textbf{loop }s_{1}\ s_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})},\llbracket\textbf{loop }s_{1}\ s_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\cup(\llbracket\textbf{loop }s_{1}\ s_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize cll})}\circ\chi_{e}))\end{aligned}

We then use the induction hypothesis to rewrite every set of the left-hand side of this goal, and remember

\llbracket s_{i}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\cup(\llbracket s_{i}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize cll})})\circ\chi_{e}
) as
{E_{i}}
for brevity. The proof goal is reduced to:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\big(({\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{R_{B}}(\llbracket s_{1}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})},E_{1})}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{R_{B}}(\llbracket s_{1}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize ctn})},E_{1})})\circ{\color[rgb]{1,0.39,0.13}{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{R_{B}}(\llbracket s_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})},E_{2})}\big)^{*}\ \circ\\
&\quad\big({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,1}{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{R_{B}}(\llbracket s_{1}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize brk})},E_{1})}\cup({\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{R_{B}}(\llbracket s_{1}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})},E_{1})}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{R_{B}}(\llbracket s_{1}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize ctn})},E_{1})})\circ{\color[rgb]{0,1,1}{\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{R_{B}}(\llbracket s_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize brk})},E_{2})}\big)\end{aligned}

(74)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\subseteq&\ {\gamma}^{\text{\scriptsize N}}_{R_{B}}(\llbracket\textbf{loop }s_{1}\ s_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})},\llbracket\textbf{loop }s_{1}\ s_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\cup(\llbracket\textbf{loop }s_{1}\ s_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize cll})}\circ\chi_{e}))\end{aligned}

By applying properties of underlying RA instances (using
gamma_simpl
), we reduce the proof goal to:

(75)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\big((\llbracket s_{1}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\cup\llbracket s_{1}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize ctn})})\circ\llbracket s_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\big)^{*}\ \circ\\
&\quad\quad\big(\llbracket s_{1}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize brk})}\cup({\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}(}\llbracket s_{1}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\cup\llbracket s_{1}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize ctn})}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0})}\circ\llbracket s_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize brk})})\big)\subseteq\llbracket\textbf{loop }s_{1}\ s_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})};\end{aligned}

(76)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\big((\llbracket s_{1}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\cup\llbracket s_{1}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize ctn})})\circ\llbracket s_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\big)^{*}\ \circ\\
&\quad\quad\big(E_{1}\cup(E_{1}\cup E_{1})\cup({\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}(}\llbracket s_{1}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\cup\llbracket s_{1}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize ctn})}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0})}\circ E_{2})\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\cup(E_{1}\cup E_{1})\cup({\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}(}\llbracket s_{1}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize nrm})}\cup\llbracket s_{1}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize ctn})}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0})}\circ E_{2})\big)\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\ \ \subseteq\ \llbracket\textbf{loop }s_{1}\ s_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize err})}\cup(\llbracket\textbf{loop }s_{1}\ s_{2}\rrbracket_{\chi_{s}}.\text{({\footnotesize cll})}\circ\chi_{e}).\end{aligned}

Finally, by properties of extended KATs (using tactic
solvefix
), proof goals (
75
) and (
76
) are solved.

Although these equations may appear lengthy, they are actually quite intuitive. For instance, the left-hand side of Formula (
75
)
says that after repeatedly executing
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
several times—where each time either
s_{1}
terminates normally, or
s_{1}
exits early due to a
continue
and then
s_{2}
terminates normally—iterated execution may either break in
s_{1}
or
s_{2}
. This behavior is precisely the semantics of the loop on the right-hand side.
Therefore, using automated proof tactics to solve them is suitable.

Figure 12
.
Proofs for the refinement of procedure calls. Here,
R_{A}
relates global states, and
R_{B}
relates pairs of local and global states. They satisfy that for any
l_{s},g_{s}
, and
l_{t},g_{t}
, if
((l_{s},g_{s}),(l_{t},g_{t}))\in R_{B}
, then
(g_{s},g_{t})\in R_{A}
.
After procedure calls, if
l_{s}
and
l_{t}
is already related in
R_{B}
, and for any
g_{s}^{\prime},g_{t}^{\prime}
,
(g_{s}^{\prime},g_{t}^{\prime})\in R_{A}
, then
((l_{s},g_{s}^{\prime}),(l_{t},g_{t}^{\prime}))\in R_{B}
.

This figure illustrates the proof idea for refinement of procedure calls. It shows how refinement of a caller’s behavior is reduced to refinement of the callee’s behavior through two state relations: \(R_A\), relating source and target global states, and \(R_B\), relating full states consisting of local and global components. The relation \(R_B\) is compatible with \(R_A\): whenever the full source and target states are related by \(R_B\), their global states are related by \(R_A\). During a procedure call, the local states of caller and callee remain unchanged, while the callee execution transforms only the global states. After the call returns, if the updated global states are related by \(R_A\), then combining them again with the unchanged local states yields full states related by \(R_B\). The figure highlights that caller refinement follows from callee refinement together with preservation of the local-state correspondence across procedure calls.

Case 3:
s
is a call statement

In our framework, the behavior refinement of procedure calls before and after the compilation depends on that of callee procedures. We use the PCALL language to illustrate the key idea of proofs for simplicity, as shown in Fig.
12
. The refinement of the caller’s behavior is derived from the refinement of the callee’s behavior. The correspondence between local states is preserved since they remain unchanged before and after the procedure call.
The behavior refinement between Clight function calls and the compiled Csharpminor function calls is proved in a similar way, with additional considerations for arguments and return values.

It’s worth noting that the behavior refinement of atomic statements has no algebraic structure and is verified directly according to their definitions.
We reuse some of CompCert original proofs for these cases, such as those for the transformation of expressions.
∎

By the properties of extended KATs, proofs are finished in a purely relational subsystem.

Figure 13
.
Refinement proofs for sequential and looping cases by automated Rocq tactics.
This figure contains two Rocq proof scripts that automate refinement proofs for sequential and loop constructs. Subfigure (a) shows the lemma \code{seq\_sem\_refines}. It states that if two target denotations \(dt_1\) and \(dt_2\) each refine the corresponding source denotations \(ds_1\) and \(ds_2\) under the same refinement parameters \code{nbrk}, \code{ncnt}, and \code{Hree}, then the sequential composition \code{seq\_sem dt1 dt2} refines the source sequential semantics \code{Clit.seq\_sem ds1 ds2}. The proof uses a short sequence of automated tactics, including preprocessing, rewriting with the hypotheses, simplifying \(\gamma\)-instances, folding callee relations, and solving fixed-point obligations. Subfigure (b) shows the lemma \code{loop\_sem\_refines}. It states that if \(dt_1\) refines \(ds_1\) under break and continue indices \(1\) and \(0\), and \(dt_2\) refines \(ds_2\) under indices \(0\) and \code{S ncnt}, then the translated loop built from \code{Sblock\_sem}, \code{loop\_sem}, and sequential composition refines the Clight loop semantics \code{Clit.loop\_sem ds1 ds2}. Its proof follows the same automated style, with additional simplification steps for empty sets. The figure illustrates that both sequential and looping refinement proofs can be discharged concisely by automated Rocq tactics.

Three key tactics are employed in the proofs of Fig.
13
:
try_rewrite
,
gamma_simpl
and
solvefix
.
The tactic
try_rewrite
reduces proof goals by rewriting with the refinement conditions, typically of the form
x\subseteq\gamma(y,z)
.
The tactic
gamma_simpl
discharges
\gamma
-instances by exploiting the algebraic properties of the underlying RA instances.
The tactic
solvefix
resolves KAT (in-)equations by applying the equational laws of Kleene algebra with tests.
Several auxiliary tactics are also used for supporting purposes, such as
preprocess
for unfolding definitions and simplifying by definitional equalities,
empty_simpl
for eliminating empty sets, and
handle_special
for handling special operators.

Remark

The
solvefix
tactic is designed for KAT (in-)equations and can also handle simple equations with infinity operators (i.e., those grammatically equal), which are sufficient in our setting. More general cases with infinity operators are undecidable and must be handled manually. Atomic statements lack algebraic structure and are therefore verified directly by their definitions.

8.2.
Compilation Correctness

The CompCert front-end compiles Clight to Cminor through the Cshmgen and Cminorgen phases. We use
\mathcal{T}_{\text{\footnotesize Cshmgenf}}
for the transformation of Clight functions by Cshmgen, and
\mathcal{T}_{\text{\footnotesize Cmingenf}}
for the transformation of Csharpminor functions by Cminorgen, whose correctness is shown as follows.

8.2.1.
Compilation Correctness from Clight to Cminor

We first define the identity Kripke interface relation as:
A_{\text{\scriptsize U}}\triangleq\langle\text{\footnotesize Unit},\leadsto_{\text{\scriptsize U}},\lambda w.\mathbb{1},\lambda w.\mathbb{1}\rangle
,
\leadsto_{\text{\scriptsize U}}\,\triangleq\text{\footnotesize Unit}\times\text{\footnotesize Unit}
, and
Unit
denotes a unit set. The KIR
A_{\text{\scriptsize C}}
is defined in Def.
7.37
, and “
\sqsubseteq_{\_\twoheadrightarrow\_}
” is defined in Def.
7.31
. We then have the following theorems.

Theorem 8.2 (Cshmgen correctness).

For any Clight function
F_{s}
and Csharpminor function
F_{u}
,

\displaystyle\text{if }\,\mathcal{T}_{\text{\footnotesize Cshmgenf}}(F_{s})=\textbf{Succeed}(F_{u}),\text{ then }\llbracket F_{u}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq_{A_{\text{\scriptsize U}}\twoheadrightarrow A_{\text{\scriptsize U}}}\llbracket F_{s}\rrbracket.

Remark that Thm.
8.2
is deduced from the translation correctness of Clight statements (i.e., Lemma
8.1
), and the original CompCert proof is reused for verifying the translation of initialization when entering the function and the translation of memory releasing when leaving the function.

Theorem 8.3 (Cminorgen correctness).

For any Csharpminor function
F_{u}
, Cminor function
F_{t}
,

\text{if }\,\mathcal{T}_{\text{\footnotesize Cmingenf}}(F_{u})=\textbf{Succeed}(F_{t}),\text{ then }\llbracket F_{t}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq_{A_{\text{\scriptsize C}}\twoheadrightarrow A_{\text{\scriptsize C}}}\llbracket F_{u}\rrbracket.

8.2.2.
Optimization Correctness

To demonstrate the applicability of our approach beyond the CompCert front-end, we model typical back-end optimizations of CompCert (i.e., standard constant propagation and dead code elimination) on extended PCALL and CFG languages that additionally account for event traces. We refer to these as
trace-enriched
PCALL/CFG languages. Their denotations have the signature
FDenote
, and the associated correctness theorems are given as follows.

Theorem 8.4 (Soundness of constant propagation on PCALL).

If a trace-enriched PCALL program
p
is optimized into
\mathcal{T}_{\text{\footnotesize cp}}(p)
by constant propagation,
then
\llbracket\mathcal{T}_{\text{\footnotesize cp}}(p)\rrbracket\sqsubseteq_{A_{\text{\scriptsize U}}\twoheadrightarrow A_{\text{\scriptsize U}}}\llbracket p\rrbracket
.

Theorem 8.5 (Soundness of dead code elimination on PCALL).

If a trace-enriched PCALL program
p
is optimized into
\mathcal{T}_{\text{\footnotesize dce}}(p)
by dead code elimination,
then
\llbracket\mathcal{T}_{\text{\footnotesize dce}}(p)\rrbracket\sqsubseteq_{A_{\text{\scriptsize U}}\twoheadrightarrow A_{\text{\scriptsize U}}}\llbracket p\rrbracket
.

Similarly, we have optimization correctness on the trace-enriched control-flow-graph language.

Theorem 8.6 (Correctness of constant propagation on CFGs).

If a trace-enriched CFG
p
is optimized into
\mathcal{T}_{\text{\footnotesize cp}}(p)
by constant propagation,
then
\llbracket\mathcal{T}_{\text{\footnotesize cp}}(p)\rrbracket\sqsubseteq_{A_{\text{\scriptsize U}}\twoheadrightarrow A_{\text{\scriptsize U}}}\llbracket p\rrbracket
.

Theorem 8.7 (Correctness of dead code elimination on CFGs).

If a trace-enriched CFG
p
is optimized into
\mathcal{T}_{\text{\footnotesize dce}}(p)
by dead code elimination,
then
\llbracket\mathcal{T}_{\text{\footnotesize dce}}(p)\rrbracket\sqsubseteq_{A_{\text{\scriptsize U}}\twoheadrightarrow A_{\text{\scriptsize U}}}\llbracket p\rrbracket
.

Additionally, we have the correctness of generating CFGs from syntax trees of PCALL as follows.

Theorem 8.8 (Correctness of CFGs generation).

If a trace-enriched CFG
\mathcal{T}_{\text{\footnotesize cfg}}(p)
is generated from a trace-enriched PCALL program
p
, then
\llbracket\mathcal{T}_{\text{\footnotesize cfg}}(p)\rrbracket\sqsubseteq_{A_{\text{\scriptsize U}}\twoheadrightarrow A_{\text{\scriptsize U}}}\llbracket p\rrbracket
.

8.3.
Compositional Correctness

Based on the definition of refinement between function denotations in Def.
7.31
, we then have the following results, where Lemma
8.9
helps derive the refinement of modules from the refinement of functions, Thm.
8.10
is used to compose the correctness of each compilation phase, and Thm.
8.11
shows the correctness of module-level compositionality.

Specifically,
in our implementation, we follow CompCertO’s approach
(
Koenig and Shao, 2021
;
Zhang et al., 2024
)
to support vertical compositionality and use denotation-based semantic linking along with fixed-point-related theorems to support horizontal compositionality.

Lemma 8.9.

For any open modules
M_{s}
and
M_{t}
, and for any Kripke interface relations
A
and
B
,

\text{if }\llparenthesis M_{t}\rrparenthesis\sqsubseteq_{A\twoheadrightarrow B}\llparenthesis M_{s}\rrparenthesis,\text{ then }\llbracket M_{t}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq_{A\twoheadrightarrow B}\llbracket M_{s}\rrbracket.

Theorem 8.10 (Vertical compositionality).

For any modules
M_{1}
,
M_{2}
,
M_{3}
, and KIRs
A_{1},A_{2},B_{1},B_{2}
,

\text{if }\llbracket M_{3}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq_{A_{2}\twoheadrightarrow B_{2}}\llbracket M_{2}\rrbracket\;\text{and}\;\llbracket M_{2}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq_{A_{1}\twoheadrightarrow B_{1}}\llbracket M_{1}\rrbracket,\text{ then }\llbracket M_{3}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq_{A_{2}\bullet A_{1}\twoheadrightarrow B_{2}\bullet B_{1}}\llbracket M_{1}\rrbracket.

Theorem 8.11 (Horizontal compositionality).

For any modules
S_{1}
,
S_{2}
,
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
, and a KIR
A
,

\text{if }\llbracket T_{1}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq_{A\twoheadrightarrow A}\llbracket S_{1}\rrbracket\;\text{ and }\;\llbracket T_{2}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq_{A\twoheadrightarrow A}\llbracket S_{2}\rrbracket,\text{ then }\llbracket T_{1}\rrbracket\oplus\llbracket T_{2}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq_{A\twoheadrightarrow A}\llbracket S_{1}\rrbracket\oplus\llbracket S_{2}\rrbracket.

The horizontal compositionality (termination case) is elegantly proved by the following lemma, where
\mu x.f(x)
can be
(\llbracket T_{1}\rrbracket\oplus\llbracket T_{2}\rrbracket)_{\chi_{t}}.(\text{\footnotesize nrm})
,
\mu x.g(x)
can be
(\llbracket S_{1}\rrbracket\oplus\llbracket S_{2}\rrbracket)_{\chi_{s}}.(\text{\footnotesize nrm})
, and
\mu x.h(x)
can be
(\llbracket S_{1}\rrbracket\oplus\llbracket S_{2}\rrbracket)^{\chi_{e}}_{\chi_{s}}(\text{\footnotesize err})
14
14

14

(\llbracket S_{1}\rrbracket\oplus\llbracket S_{2}\rrbracket)^{\chi_{e}}_{\chi_{s}}(\text{\footnotesize err})
represents a fixed point equivalent to
(\llbracket S_{1}\rrbracket\oplus\llbracket S_{2}\rrbracket)_{\chi_{s}}(\text{\footnotesize err})\cup(\llbracket S_{1}\rrbracket\oplus\llbracket S_{2}\rrbracket)_{\chi_{s}}(\text{\footnotesize cll})\circ\chi_{e}
.
.
This lemma itself can be easily proved by induction.
Other proof obligations can be systematically solved by the
gamma_simpl
and
solvefix
tactics akin to proofs for Formula (
77
).

Lemma 8.12.

Given a refinement algebra
\mathcal{A}=(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{E},\mathcal{T},\gamma)
,
with CPOs
(\mathcal{S},\subseteq)
,
(\mathcal{E},\subseteq)
, and
(\mathcal{T},\subseteq)
,
and Scott-continuous functions

f:\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{T},\,g:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{S},\,h:\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{E}.

We have:

\displaystyle\bigl(\forall N_{t}\in\mathcal{T},\,N_{s}\in\mathcal{S},\,E_{s}\in\mathcal{E},\;N_{t}\subseteq\gamma(N_{s},E_{s})\Rightarrow f(N_{t})\subseteq\gamma(g(N_{s}),h(E_{s}))\bigr)\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\mu x.\,f(x)\;\subseteq\;\gamma\bigl(\mu x.\,g(x),\;\mu x.\,h(x)\bigr).

By the horizontal compositionality (Thm.
8.11
) and the equivalence between semantic linking and syntactic linking (Thm.
4.1
), we have the following separate compilation correctness.

Lemma 8.13 (Compositional correctness of the CompCert front-end).

For any Clight open modules
S_{1},\dots,S_{n}
and Cminor open modules
T_{1},\dots,T_{n}
, if
\ T_{i}
is separately compiled from
S_{i}
by the CompCert front-end for each
i
, then

\llbracket T_{1}\rrbracket\oplus\cdots\oplus\llbracket T_{n}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq_{A_{\text{\scriptsize C}}\twoheadrightarrow A_{\text{\scriptsize C}}}\llbracket S_{1}\rrbracket\oplus\cdots\oplus\llbracket S_{n}\rrbracket
.

Theorem 8.14 (Final theorem).

For any Clight open modules
S_{1},\dots,S_{n}
and Cminor open modules
T_{1},\dots,T_{n}
, if
\ T_{i}
is separately compiled from
S_{i}
by the CompCert front-end for each
i
, then

\llbracket T_{1}+\cdots+T_{n}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq_{A_{\text{\scriptsize C}}\twoheadrightarrow A_{\text{\scriptsize C}}}\llbracket S_{1}+\cdots+S_{n}\rrbracket.

Handling global environments

The global environment
\mathbb{g}
maps functions to their definitions and does not vary.
Suppose that
F
is a function of program
P
.
For
F
’s semantics
\llbracket F\rrbracket^{\mathbb{g}}
, the global environment
\mathbb{g}
is loaded from the program
P
following the original loading process of CompCert.

When a compilation phase modifies the memory layout (e.g., Cminorgen), it is essential that the global environment be preserved under memory injection. Given a global environment
\mathbb{g}
, we extend the Kripke relation
R_{B}
with respect to
\mathbb{g}
(denoted
{R}^{\mathbb{g}}_{B}
) as follows: for any world
(f,m_{1},m_{2})
,

(78)

\displaystyle\forall a\,b,\;(a,b)\in{R}^{\mathbb{g}}_{B}(f,m_{1},m_{2})\Leftrightarrow(a,b)\in R_{B}(f,m_{1},m_{2})\land\text{\small genv\_preserved}(\mathbb{g},f,m_{1},m_{2}).

Similarly, a Kripke interface relation
B=\langle W_{B},\leadsto_{B},{R}^{q}_{B},{R}^{q}_{B}\rangle
is extended under a global environment
\mathbb{g}
, if the Kripke relations
{R}^{q}_{B}
and
{R}^{r}_{B}
are extended under
\mathbb{g}
according to Formula (
78
).
Readers may safely skip these technical details
without compromising the conceptual grasp of our framework.

9.
Comparison with small-step-based Compiler Verification

We compare our work with small-step-based approach in two aspects:
(1) how we prove behavior refinement of statements and procedures; and (2) how we support module-level compositionality.

9.1.
Comparison in Behavior Refinement

The proof of behavior refinement relies on establishing an invariant (i.e., a matching relation) between source and target program states, which must be preserved throughout execution. The denotation-based approach, by focusing on overall outcomes rather than step-by-step processes, simplifies the construction of such invariants and alleviates the concern of accounting for intermediate and nondeterminism correspondence. We illustrate the two points using concrete examples.

Simplification of program states and invariants

Original invariant of
Cminorgen
(informal) includes:

match_function

The source and target functions currently executed are related.

match_statement

The source and target statements currently executed are related.

match_continuation

The source and target continuations (control contexts) are related.

value_inject

Function arguments and return values are related by the injection
f
.

match_memory

The source and target memories are related by injection
(
m_{s}\mapsto_{f}^{\text{\scriptsize M}}m_{t}
).

match_callstack

Each stack frame corresponds with the following relations:

–
match_tenv

Temporary environments correspond under injection
(
te_{s}\mapsto_{f}^{\text{\scriptsize E}}te_{t}
).

–
match_env

Local environments are related, with pointers related by
f
.

–
padding_freeable

Padding target memory between local variables can be safely released.

–
match_bounds

Local variable addresses lie in valid memory blocks
(tracked by
nextblock
).

Figure 14
.
Informal description of the simulation invariant for
Cminorgen
in CompCert.
This figure summarizes, in informal terms, the simulation invariant used in CompCert’s \textsf{Cminorgen} proof. It lists the components that must be related between a source state and a target state during simulation. These include the currently executing source and target functions, the current statements, and the continuations or control contexts. It also requires that function arguments and return values are related by a memory injection \(f\), and that the source and target memories are related by the same injection. For each corresponding stack frame, the invariant additionally relates temporary environments, local environments with pointers mapped by \(f\), the ability to safely free padding memory between local variables in the target, and validity bounds for local variable addresses tracked by \texttt{nextblock}. Overall, the figure highlights that CompCert’s original front-end simulation invariant must simultaneously maintain correspondence across functions, statements, continuations, values, memories, and stack-frame environments.

In our framework, we presented the definitions of program states for CompCert front-end languages in Table
1
, define the
Cshmgen
proof invariant as the identity relation in Def.
7.30
, and describe the
Cminorgen
invariant in §
7.5
.
By comparison, a front-end state in CompCert, illustrated here using Clight as a representative example, is originally defined with the following structure, with Csharpminor and Cminor adopting a similar pattern.

⬇

Clight
.
state

=

|

ItnlState
:

function

\rightarrow\;

statement

\rightarrow\;

cont

\rightarrow\;

env

\rightarrow\;

tenv

\rightarrow\;

mem

\rightarrow\;

state

|

Callstate
:

fundef

\rightarrow\;

val

\rightarrow\;

list

val

\rightarrow\;

cont

\rightarrow\;

mem

\rightarrow\;

state

|

RtrnState
:

val

\rightarrow\;

cont

\rightarrow\;

mem

\rightarrow\;

state
.

Here, each state represents either:
(i) an internal execution (
ItnlState
) of a function at a particular statement with its continuation, local environment, temporary environment, and the current memory; (ii)
a function call (
Callstate
) with a function definition, arguments, continuation, and the current memory; or (iii) a function return (
RtrnState
) with a return value, continuation, and the memory.
Consequently, establishing correspondences between source and target program states requires reasoning about all components of these enriched states, as summarized in Fig.
14
.

This figure illustrates the structure of a front-end program state in CompCert. The state contains several components used by the small-step semantics: the currently executing function, the current statement, the continuation or control context, the local environment, the temporary environment, and the memory state, together with related call and return states. The figure highlights that only the temporary environment and memory form the dynamic part of the state, shown in light red, because they can change during statement execution. The other components are static or control-related information. The figure is used to emphasize that the denotational approach keeps only the dynamic part in program states and treats the static local environment as a parameter instead.

Figure 15
.
A front-end state in CompCert.
This figure illustrates the structure of a front-end program state in CompCert. The state contains several components used by the small-step semantics: the currently executing function, the current statement, the continuation or control context, the local environment, the temporary environment, and the memory state, together with related call and return states. The figure highlights that only the temporary environment and memory form the dynamic part of the state, shown in light red, because they can change during statement execution. The other components are static or control-related information. The figure is used to emphasize that the denotational approach keeps only the dynamic part in program states and treats the static local environment as a parameter instead.

In the original proofs of CompCert, all constraints over these components have to be maintained at each proof step, as illustrated in Fig.
16(a)
.
In comparison, the program state in denotational semantics defined in Table
1
only needs to include the memory state and temporary environment (i.e., the real dynamic part that can be updated by store operations or variable assignments), as colored by light red in Fig.
15
.
The local environment (i.e., the static part that is not modified during the execution of statements) is treated as a parameter of the semantic functions rather than as a component of the program state, and other information specific to small-step semantics is not required in our state definition. Furthermore, simplifying the state definition also reduces the complexity of establishing invariants. We quantify the simplification offered by our framework by comparing the
Rocq
definitions of program states and invariants in the CompCert front-end with that in our work in Table
4
.

In proofs, we derive the function transformation correctness from the statement (i.e., function body) transformation correctness, as illustrated in Fig.
16(b)
.
Our invariants for Cminorgen (defined in §
7.5
) only involve the correspondence of dynamic parts (i.e., memory state and temporary environment), while the correspondence for static components (i.e., local environment and
padding_freeable
), once established at function entry, is taken as a prerequisite for the refinement proof of statement transformation, and is preserved until function exit through the accessibility relation
\leadsto_{\text{\scriptsize injp}}
(defined in §
7.5
).
Thus, we lightened the proof burden of maintaining the invariant in each proof step by decomposing the refinement proof into smaller and simpler obligations.

(a)

Small-step-based proof style

(b)

Denotation-based proof style

Figure 16
.
Comparison between small-step-based approach and denotation-based approach in Cminorgen.
This figure compares two proof styles for Cminorgen. Subfigure (a) shows the traditional small-step-based proof style used in CompCert. At each simulation step, the proof must maintain a large invariant relating many components of the source and target states, including the current function, statement, continuation, environments, memory, and call stack. Subfigure (b) shows the denotation-based proof style. The proof is decomposed into smaller obligations: function transformation correctness is derived from statement or function-body transformation correctness, and the invariant focuses only on the dynamic components, namely memory state and temporary environment. Static information such as the local environment is established at function entry and then preserved separately through an accessibility relation. The figure highlights that the denotation-based approach reduces the burden of maintaining complex invariants at every proof step.

.

Elimination of intermediate correspondence

CompCert uses forward simulation to verify each compilation phase. This works well when each step of the source program indeed corresponds to multiple steps in the target program.
However, the correspondence between the source program and the target program can sometimes be many-to-one instead of one-to-many. In this case, verifying the forward simulation requires additional proof steps for the construction of the intermediate state correspondence.
In more complex compilation phases, no matter whether using forward or backward simulation, additional intermediate correspondences must be constructed. For instance, CompCertM
(
Song et al., 2020
)
requires mixed simulation to verify compilation correctness due to its modifications of interaction semantics. In comparison, the denotation-based approach can simplify such verification process by focusing on the overall execution of programs.

(a)

Refinement in small-step semantics

(b)

Refinement in denotational semantics

Figure 17
.
Comparison between the refinement process for small-step semantics and that for denotational semantics. In Fig.
17(a)
,
R
and
G
respectively represent rely and guarantee conditions, vertical curved arrows denote the matching relation between the source and the target program states held throughout the execution,
and vertical shaded arrows in Fig.
17(b)
denote the refinement between function denotations.
This figure compares refinement for open modules in small-step semantics and in denotational semantics. Subfigure (a) shows the small-step style. An incoming call to the target module is matched with a corresponding incoming call to the source module, and then the proof proceeds step by step through execution. Each outgoing external call of the target must match a corresponding outgoing external call of the source, and their returns must also match before the proof can continue. Throughout the execution, a state-matching relation is maintained between source and target states, while rely and guarantee conditions constrain how external calls and internal computations may affect the calling context. Subfigure (b) shows the denotational style. Instead of matching execution step by step, the semantics of an open module is viewed as mapping callee behavior to caller behavior through semantic oracles. Refinement is expressed vertically between the target and source function denotations: if the external source-function behavior is refined by the external target-function behavior, then the denotation of the compiled target module is refined by that of the source module. The figure emphasizes that the denotational approach captures rely and guarantee conditions through refinement between semantic oracles and module denotations, avoiding step-by-step matching of intermediate states and calls.

We use the following examples (previously presented in §
2.1
) to demonstrate that handling intermediate correspondence and nondeterminism is straightforward in our framework.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\text{Source program }S:\ \ \text{\footnotesize print(x); y = f(x); print(y);}\\
&\text{Transformed to }T:\ \ \text{\footnotesize y = g(x); print(x); print(y);}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\text{Source program }S^{\prime}:\ \ \text{\footnotesize choice(x = 0, x = 1); print(0); print(x);}\\
&\text{Transformed to }T^{\prime}:\ \ \text{\footnotesize print(0); choice(x = 0, x = 1); print(x);}\end{aligned}

Assume that they are type-safe programs. The following semantics is derived by definitions, where
\llbracket\text{\footnotesize print(x)}\rrbracket\triangleq\Set{(\sigma,\tau,\sigma)}{\tau=\sigma(\text{\footnotesize x})}
, and
\sigma(\text{\footnotesize x})
means the value of a temporary variable
x
on state
\sigma
.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\llbracket S\rrbracket=\Set{(\sigma,\tau,\sigma^{\prime})}{(\sigma,\text{\footnotesize nil},\sigma^{\prime})\in\llbracket f\rrbracket\land\tau=\text{\footnotesize OUT}(\sigma(\text{\footnotesize x}))\text{\footnotesize:\!:}\text{\footnotesize OUT}(\sigma^{\prime}(\text{\footnotesize y}))\text{\footnotesize:\!:}\text{\footnotesize nil}}\\
&\llbracket T\rrbracket=\Set{(\sigma,\tau,\sigma^{\prime})}{(\sigma,\text{\footnotesize nil},\sigma^{\prime})\in\llbracket g\rrbracket\land\tau=\text{\footnotesize OUT}(\sigma(\text{\footnotesize x}))\text{\footnotesize:\!:}\text{\footnotesize OUT}(\sigma^{\prime}(\text{\footnotesize y}))\text{\footnotesize:\!:}\text{\footnotesize nil}}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\llbracket S^{\prime}\rrbracket=\Set{(\sigma,\tau,\sigma^{\prime})}{\tau=\text{\footnotesize OUT}(\text{\footnotesize 0})\text{\footnotesize:\!:}\text{\footnotesize OUT}(\sigma^{\prime}(\text{\footnotesize x}))\text{\footnotesize:\!:}\text{\footnotesize nil}\land\bigl(\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma[x\mapsto\text{\footnotesize 0}]\vee\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma[x\mapsto\text{\footnotesize 1}]\bigr)}\\
&\llbracket T^{\prime}\rrbracket=\Set{(\sigma,\tau,\sigma^{\prime})}{\tau=\text{\footnotesize OUT}(\text{\footnotesize 0})\text{\footnotesize:\!:}\text{\footnotesize OUT}(\sigma^{\prime}(\text{\footnotesize x}))\text{\footnotesize:\!:}\text{\footnotesize nil}\land\bigl(\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma[x\mapsto\text{\footnotesize 0}]\vee\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma[x\mapsto\text{\footnotesize 1}]\bigr)}\end{aligned}

As a result, we can straightforwardly conclude that
\llbracket S^{\prime}\rrbracket=\llbracket T^{\prime}\rrbracket
and if
\llbracket g\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\llbracket f\rrbracket
, then
\llbracket T\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\llbracket S\rrbracket
.

9.2.
Comparison in Supporting Verified Compositional Compilation

Verified compositional compilers have been an important research topic for a long time, as evidenced by extensive research efforts such as

Beringer et al. (2014)
,

Ramananandro et al. (2015)
,

Stewart et al. (2015)
,

Song et al. (2020)
,

Koenig and Shao (2021)
, and

Zhang et al. (2024)
.

The common style of developing compositional semantics for supporting module-level compositionality is based on small-step semantics
(including
Beringer et al. (2014)
,
Stewart et al. (2015)
,
Song et al. (2020)
,
Koenig and Shao (2021)
, and
Zhang et al. (2024)
),
in which the description of behavior refinement must introduce extra mechanisms to capture inter-module function calls. Because C functions in a module can both be called by other modules and call other modules, a C module’s compilation correctness described by small-step semantics, as shown in Fig.
17(a)
, looks like:

•

If an incoming call in the target language corresponds to a call in the source language (i.e., the arguments and programs states match with each other), then the first outgoing calls would match; and further, if the returned value and program states of these two first outgoing calls match with each other, then the second outgoing calls would match, etc. until the original incoming calls return.

•

For horizontal compositionality, the compilation correctness of internal C functions relies on external
calls satisfying certain well-behavedness conditions (known as rely-conditions; e.g., external calls
do not modify the private memory of callers). In turn, the execution of internal C functions itself guarantees some well-behavedness conditions (known as guarantee-conditions, e.g., they do
not modify the private memory of their calling environments).

In comparison, the definition of our behavior refinement is much different.
Since the semantics of open modules is modeled as a function from callee’s denotation to caller’s denotation, i.e., the behavior of every external call is interpreted by semantic oracle
\chi_{s}
(for the source module) and
\chi_{t}
(for the target module),
our compilation correctness is described as follows (shown in Fig.
17(b)
):
if the behavior of external source functions is refined by the behavior of external target functions, then the denotation of source module is refined by the denotation of the compiled target module.
The refinement of semantic oracles (i.e., callee’s behavior) provides rely conditions for the refinement of caller’s behavior, and the refinement of caller’s behavior itself satisfies guarantee conditions.

Ramananandro et al. (2015)
develop a compositional semantics on top of small-step semantics by
modeling the behavior of external calls as special events.
Such a special event records the procedure name and the memory state before and after the external call.
When composing the behavior of multiple procedures, one has to interpret these special traces, in which when an external procedure is called, instead of asking an oracle to obtain its semantics, the special event trace of the invoked procedure is used to interpret it, and so on.
For example, consider two procedures
p
and
q
that form two separate modules and do not use any memory state for simplicity.
If the behavior of
p
is sequentially calling
q
three times, and the behavior of
q
is sequentially outputting zero for two times, then their semantics can be
\llbracket p\rrbracket
=
Extcall
(
q
)::
Extcall
(
q
)::
Extcall
(
q
)::
nil

and
\llbracket q\rrbracket
=
OUT
(0)::
OUT
(0)::
nil
, where
Extcall
(
q
) is a special event and
OUT
(0) is the original event of CompCert.
After the semantic linking, the behavior of
p
would be outputting zero for six times, i.e.,
OUT
(0)::
OUT
(0)::
OUT
(0)::
OUT
(0)::
OUT
(0)::
OUT
(0)::
nil
.
That is, they compose semantics by replacing special events (based on the small-step semantics of CompCert), which can be viewed as progressing incrementally over the program’s execution time. The key to proving behavior refinement for them is to establish the correspondence between events step by step. Therefore, their main proof structure is similar to that of small-step semantics. In comparison, our refinement proofs are built upon RA instances and are achieved in a more compositional way.

9.3.
Comparison in vertical compositionality

(a)

Vertical composition in small-step semantics

(b)

Vertical composition in our framework

Figure 18
.
Comparison between vertical composition of open simulations and vertical composition of behavior refinement in our framework, where
q_{i}
and
r_{i}
represents the query and the reply of a function call in module
M_{i}
. The solid arrow
\rightarrow
and dashed arrow
\dashrightarrow
respectively mean internal steps and external steps.
This figure compares two styles of vertical composition for open-module correctness across two compilation phases, from module \(M_1\) to \(M_2\) and then to \(M_3\). Subfigure (a) shows the small-step style based on open simulations. Starting from an incoming query \(q_3\) to \(M_3\), the proof first splits its correspondence through an intermediate query \(q_2\) and then to \(q_1\). After internal execution steps, shown by solid arrows, each module may issue outgoing external queries \(q_1'\), \(q_2'\), and \(q_3'\); their pairwise correspondences must be merged into a direct correspondence between \(q_1'\) and \(q_3'\). The corresponding outgoing replies are then split again through the intermediate module, and after more internal steps the incoming replies \(r_1\), \(r_2\), and \(r_3\) are merged to obtain a direct correspondence between \(r_1\) and \(r_3\). Dashed arrows denote external steps, and the thick arrows emphasize the nontrivial splitting and merging of correspondences across phases. Subfigure (b) shows vertical composition in the denotational framework. Instead of following the interactive execution step by step, the proof has two higher-level steps: first, refinement of callee behavior between \(M_1\) and \(M_3\) is decomposed through the intermediate module \(M_2\); second, the caller-behavior refinements for \(M_3\) versus \(M_2\) and for \(M_2\) versus \(M_1\) are merged to derive refinement of \(M_3\) versus \(M_1\). The figure emphasizes that the denotational approach reasons about overall caller and callee behaviors, avoiding the repeated stepwise splitting and merging required by small-step simulations.

Consider three modules
M_{1}
,
M_{2}
, and
M_{3}
such that
M_{1}
is compiled into
M_{2}
, and
M_{2}
is further compiled into
M_{3}
by two sequential compilation phases. The vertical compositionality is to show if
\llbracket M_{3}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\llbracket M_{2}\rrbracket
and
\llbracket M_{2}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\llbracket M_{1}\rrbracket
, then
\llbracket M_{3}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\llbracket M_{1}\rrbracket
.
We compare the vertical composition of behavior refinement in our framework with the vertical composition of open simulations between interactive semantics in Fig.
18
, where dashed fat arrows mean direct deduction by assumptions we know, and solid fat arrows mean that one needs to split the direct correspondence or merge the correspondence of two compilation phases (usually quite nontrivial). As shown in Fig.
18(a)
, the vertical composition of open simulations typically proceeds as follows:

•

Split the initial correspondence of incoming queries
. The direct correspondence between incoming calls with initial queries
q_{1}
and
q_{3}
is decomposed. That means, there exists an incoming query
q_{2}
such that
q_{3}
corresponds to
q_{2}
, and
q_{2}
corresponds to
q_{1}
.

•

Merge correspondences of outgoing queries
. After zero or more internal steps, an outgoing call may occur. By assumption, outgoing queries (
q_{i}^{\prime}
for module
M_{i}
) are separately related, which are then merged into a direct correspondence between
q_{1}^{\prime}
and
q_{3}^{\prime}
.

•

Split the correspondence of outgoing replies
. Following the outgoing calls with related queries
q_{1}^{\prime}
and
q_{3}^{\prime}
, their corresponding replies
r_{1}^{\prime}
and
r_{3}^{\prime}
are assumed to be directly related. This correspondence is decomposed again for further deduction of internal steps.

•

Merge correspondences of incoming replies
. After zero or more internal steps, the replies of initial incoming queries are separately related by assumptions. Finally, by merging relations of each phase, a direct correspondence between incoming replies
r_{1}
and
r_{3}
is established.

In comparison, the vertical composition in our framework, as shown in Fig.
18(b)
, goes through two steps:
(i) decomposing the refinement of callee’s behaviors between module
M_{1}
and
M_{3}
; and (ii) merging the refinements of caller’s behaviors between
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
, and between
M_{2}
and
M_{3}
. In other words, our framework approaches vertical composition from the perspectives of overall behaviors of the callee and the caller functions, whereas the small-step-based approach through the lens of the program’s interactive process.
The connection between these two perspectives lies in that the first and last solid fat arrow in Fig.
18(a)
corresponds to the last fat arrow in Fig.
18(b)
, and the second and third solid fat arrow in Fig.
18(a)
corresponds to the first fat arrow in Fig.
18(b)
.

(a)

CompComp

(b)

CompCertM

(c)

CompCertO

Figure 19
.
Refinements in existing VCC methods.
Adapted from
Zhang et al. (2024)
.
© 2024 the owner/author(s). Licensed under CC BY 4.0
(
https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/
).
Changes: replaced
\precsim
with
\sqsubseteq
.
This figure compares three refinement designs used in existing verified compositional compiler frameworks. Subfigure (a), labeled CompComp, depicts a single uniform refinement relation used across all compilation phases. This relation supports both vertical and horizontal composition, but it is heavyweight and tied to a restricted calling convention. Subfigure (b), labeled CompCertM, shows several phase-specific refinement relations feeding into one uniform refinement relation. The idea is that each individual compilation phase may be verified with its own refinement, and then lifted to a common refinement that supports composition. Subfigure (c), labeled CompCertO, shows refinement relations composed by concatenation along a compilation chain. This makes vertical composition straightforward, because consecutive refinements can be chained through intermediate languages, but it exposes the intermediate semantics and complicates horizontal composition with separately compiled environment modules. Overall, the figure contrasts the tradeoffs among a single uniform refinement, a family of refinements with a common target, and explicit concatenation of phase-specific refinements.

Though the above process of vertical composition seems intuitive, constructing interpolating program states to transitively connect evolving source and target states across external calls of open modules turns out to be a big challenge, as evidenced by a series of efforts
(
Hur et al., 2012
;
Neis et al., 2015
;
Patterson and Ahmed, 2019
;
Song et al., 2020
;
Zhang et al., 2024
)
.

Zhang et al. (2024)
make a comprehensive conclusion of challenges and approaches to
verified compositional compilers, as shown in Fig.
19
.
Generally, an ideal VCC framework expects to find a refinement relation “
\sqsubseteq
” satisfying the following properties, where
L
(with subscripts) denotes the semantics of open modules.

(79)

Vertical compositionality:

\displaystyle L_{3}\sqsubseteq L_{2}\Rightarrow L_{2}\sqsubseteq L_{1}\Rightarrow L_{3}\sqsubseteq L_{1}

(80)

Horizontal compositionality:

\displaystyle L_{2}\sqsubseteq L_{1}\Rightarrow L_{2}^{\prime}\sqsubseteq L_{1}^{\prime}\Rightarrow L_{2}\oplus L_{2}^{\prime}\sqsubseteq L_{1}\oplus L_{1}^{\prime}

(81)

Adequacy w.r.t. semantics:

\displaystyle\llbracket M_{1}+M_{2}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\llbracket M_{1}\rrbracket\oplus\llbracket M_{2}\rrbracket

The final goal of VCC is to prove that, for example, if a C module
a.c
is compiled to assembly code

a.s
going through
\text{\small a.c}\rightarrow\text{\small a.i1}\rightarrow\text{\small a.i2}\rightarrow\text{\small a.s}
, and an environment module is separately compiled by a different compiler (typically, an assembly library
b.s
without any compilation), then
\llbracket\text{\small a.s}+\text{\small b.s}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\llbracket\text{\small a.c}\rrbracket\oplus L_{b}
, where
L_{b}
is the top-level semantic specification of
b.s
. The proof of this goal usually proceeds as follows.

(i) Show individual phases correctness, i.e.,
\llbracket\text{\small a.s}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\llbracket\text{\small a.i2}\rrbracket
,

\llbracket\text{\small a.i2}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\llbracket\text{\small a.i1}\rrbracket
and

\llbracket\text{\small a.i1}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\llbracket\text{\small a.c}\rrbracket
.

(ii) Show end-to-end correctness by vertical compositionality, i.e.,
\llbracket\text{\small a.s}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\llbracket\text{\small a.c}\rrbracket
and
\llbracket\text{\small b.s}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq L_{b}
.

(iii) Show that
\llbracket\text{\small a.s}\rrbracket\oplus\llbracket\text{\small b.s}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\llbracket\text{\small a.c}\rrbracket\oplus L_{b}
holds by horizontal compositionality.

(iv) Show that
\llbracket\text{\small a.s}+\text{\small b.s}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\llbracket\text{\small a.c}\rrbracket\oplus L_{b}
holds by adequacy (
81
) and vertical compositionality.

In practice, each compilation phase may use different refinement relations, since intermediate representations (or IRs) may have different ways of interaction with environment (so called
language interface
).

CompComp
uses a unified refinement relation for different compilation phases, which is both vertically and horizontally compositional but heavyweight and limited to interaction with C-like calling conventions. Thus CompComp does not support adequacy w.r.t. assembly semantics.

CompCertM
proposes a RUSC (Refinement Under Self-relation Context) theory featured with a fixed collection of refinement relations
\{\sqsubseteq_{1},...,\sqsubseteq_{n}\}
and a uniform refinement
\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{R}}
, so that individual compilation phases can be verified with one of
\{\sqsubseteq_{1},...,\sqsubseteq_{n}\}
. The RUSC theory guarantees that given semantics of open modules
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
, if
L_{2}\sqsubseteq_{i}L_{1}
, then
L_{2}\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{R}}L_{1}
for
1\leq i\leq n
, where
\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{R}}
is both vertically and horizontally compositional.

CompCertO
introduces a naive product operator for concatenation of refinements, i.e.,

L_{3}\sqsubseteq_{2}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\sqsubseteq_{1}L_{1}
iff there exists some
L_{2}
such that
L_{3}\sqsubseteq_{2}L_{2}
and
L_{2}\sqsubseteq_{1}L_{1}
. Thus, vertical composition in CompCertO is trivial. However, as pointed out by Zhang et al., horizontal composition in CompCertO would expose the intermediate semantics of the compilation chain, in the sense that (see Fig.
19(c)
)
for horizontal composition with the compilation correctness of
a.c
, namely
\llbracket\text{\small a.s}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\sqsubseteq_{2}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\sqsubseteq_{1}\llbracket\text{\small a.c}\rrbracket
, one has to show the environment module satisfying the same chain of refinement concatenation, i.e.,
\llbracket\text{\small b.s}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\sqsubseteq_{2}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\sqsubseteq_{1}L_{b}
. Zhang et al. address this problem by introducing a direct refinement of open modules (denoted as
\sqsubseteq_{\text{\footnotesize ac}}
). They show that the concatenation of refinements in CompCertO can be further refined to the direct refinement between C and assembly. For example, if
\llbracket\text{\small a.s}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\sqsubseteq_{2}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\sqsubseteq_{1}\llbracket\text{\small a.c}\rrbracket
and
\sqsubseteq_{3}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\sqsubseteq_{2}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\sqsubseteq_{1}\ \preccurlyeq\ \sqsubseteq_{\text{\footnotesize ac}}
, then
\llbracket\text{\small a.s}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq_{\text{\footnotesize ac}}\llbracket\text{\small a.c}\rrbracket
. In this perspective, the concatenation of refinements for CompComp and CompCertO can be refined to themselves, i.e.,
\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{C}}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{C}}\ \preccurlyeq\ \sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{C}}
and
\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{R}}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{R}}\ \preccurlyeq\ \sqsubseteq_{\mathcal{R}}
, while Zhang et al. achieve this with less restrictions by discovering a uniform and compositional Kripke relation for describing the evolution of memory injections across external calls (shown in Def.
7.35
and used in our Cminorgen proof).
In our setting, given KIRs
A_{1},A_{2}
and
B_{1},B_{2}
,

\sqsubseteq_{A_{1}\twoheadrightarrow B_{2}}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\sqsubseteq_{A_{2}\twoheadrightarrow B_{2}}
means
\sqsubseteq_{A_{2}\bullet A_{1}\twoheadrightarrow B_{2}\bullet B_{1}}
(see Def.
7.7
).
That is, the vertical compositionality shown in Example
7.8
follows the same approach of CompCertO and can achieve direct refinement following Zhang et al’s approach.

(

We demonstrate the lightweight extensibility of our framework by presenting the incremental code required to support two new features that were not accommodated in our earlier development.
The first one is the support of procedure calls in toy languages. Adding the support of procedure calls to the WHILE and earlier CFG languages totally requires an additional 1,025 lines of code for definitions and proofs of the constant propagation and dead code elimination optimizations (corresponding to the first five portions in Table
7
).
The second feature is unconditional branching introduced in §
4.4
. We now support this feature in the CompCert front-end (i.e., the last two portions in Table
